    OSA

    • A Review of Pharmacological Therapies for Obstructive Sleep Apnea and Impact on Cardiovascular Outcomes
      Robinson, De Sarkar, Moore, Malhotra | 2026
      The scientific paper advocates for a personalized approach to treating Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA), focusing on six potential pharmacological strategies including enhancing upper airway dilator muscle activity, stabilizing breathing control, blocking cardiovascular consequences, addressing symptoms, promoting CPAP adherence, and facilitating weight loss. Promising results have been shown for a drug combination of atomoxetine and oxybutynin, GLP-1 receptor agonists like tirzepatide, and future pharmacological strategies include orforglipron and retatrutide. The authors suggest that a combination of therapies targeting mechanical airway obstruction, underlying obesity, and cardiovascular risk factors will optimize clinical outcomes for OSA patients, with CPAP remaining the standard of care for OSA evaluation and treatment by sleep medicine specialists.
      Keywords: Obstructive sleep apnea, GLP-1 receptor agonists, Pharmacological therapies, Cardiovascular disease
    • A neuroprosthesis for restoring hand movement and sensation in a person with complete tetraplegia
      Bouton | 2026
      The scientific paper presents a hybrid Dual Neuromodulation (DNB) system that combines an intracortical brain-computer interface, transcutaneous spinal cord stimulation, and peripheral stimuli for restoring motor and sensory functions in individuals with spinal cord injuries. The study on one participant showed significant improvements in motor control and sensory recovery, with potential for home use. Two related studies were discussed, both focusing on neural modulation in response to tactile stimuli and emphasizing the importance of understanding brain activity associated with different body parts and various types of stimuli. Challenges remain in optimizing stimulation parameters, adapting to interindividual variability, and streamlining identification of effective stimulation patterns for the DNB system.
    • Aquaporin-4 mis-localization slows glymphatic clearance of α-synuclein and promotes α-synuclein pathology and aggregate propagation
      Braun, Simon, Jang, Sanderson, Swierz, Sevao, Pincus, Schaser, Elliott, Lim, Unni, Schindler, Keene, Latimer, Iliff | 2024
      This research paper suggests that mislocalization of AQP4, a water channel protein in the perivascular region, impairs glymphatic function, which is responsible for clearing alpha-synuclein from brain tissue. This impairment promotes the aggregation of alpha-synuclein and potentially contributes to the development and propagation of synucleinopathies like Lewy Body Dementia and Parkinson's disease. The findings are based on post-mortem human brain tissue analysis and experiments using transgenic mice, and suggest that glymphatic impairment may contribute to the development and progression of synucleinopathies.
      Keywords: Glymphatic, aquaporin-4, AQP4, Astrocytes, Perivascular endfeet, α-syntrophin, α-synuclein, propagation, PFFs, Lewy body dementia
    • Beyond Breathing: Lung as a Sensory Organ A Report from the NHLBI Workshop on Lung Sensing and Its Implication in Diseases
      Jendzjowsky, Kuo, Reznikov, Chen, Li, Prescott, Liu, Seeholzer, Tschumperlin, Taylor-Clark, Rajagopal, Haber, Noël, Kotas, Drake, Pabelick, Rosenblatt, Mouradian, Su, Gomez, Lin, Lu, Mongodin, Vuga, Zhou, Prakash, Sun | 2026
      The scientific paper discusses a workshop on 'Lung Sensing', a sensory interface beyond gas exchange in the lungs. The research focuses on understanding various types of sensory cells involved, their roles in lung diseases like COPD, asthma, and small-cell lung cancer, and potential future directions for studying lung sensing to uncover novel therapeutic targets and improve disease management.
    • Clinical Application of Near Infrared Blood Oxygen Monitoring in Cervical Spinal Cord Injury
      Alshorman, Altahla, Gao, Liu | 2024
      The study "Clinical Application of Near-Infrared Blood Oxygen Monitoring in Cervical Spinal Cord Injury" investigates the use of near-infrared light-Oxygen saturation (NIR-SO2) technology to monitor spinal cord blood supply during surgery for cervical spinal cord injuries. The research, conducted on 37 patients, found an improvement in regional SO2 after laminectomy and durotomy, suggesting that NIR-SO2 is safe, feasible, and could potentially predict recovery and guide treatment choices for such patients.
      Keywords: Near-infrared light, Blood oxygen saturation, Cervical spinal cord injury, Laminectomy, Durotomy
    • Evidence for acid-sensing ion channel 3 (ASIC3) involvement in cough resulting from aspiration of gastric fluid
      Chou, Mori, Pavelkova, Liu, Liptak, Meeker, Halicka, Undem, Kollarik, Canning | 2026
      This study, published in J Physiol 604.3 (2026), investigates the role of ASICs in coughing triggered by aspirated gastric fluid using an animal model. Key findings suggest that ASIC3-dependent coughing is initiated by gastric fluid aspiration and can be inhibited by the ASIC3 blocker diclofenac. The research implies potential implications for identifying patients at risk for aspiration pneumonia and developing therapeutic strategies for cough hypersensitivity.
    • Expert-level probabilistic breathing event detector informs phenotyping of sleep apnea
      Kjaer, Hanif, Brink-Kjaer, Olsen, Sum-Ping, Carrillo, Sands, Redline, Stone, Jennum, Mignot | 2026
      This study introduces an expert-level probabilistic breathing event detector (ABED), a deep learning model trained on over 5000 polysomnographies, to enhance sleep apnea phenotyping by providing accurate identification of various types of sleep-disordered breathing events and offering insights into their etiology. The model can outperform human experts in large-scale studies and shows potential benefits for increased diagnosis rates and more consistent scoring of apnea events. However, its robustness to low signal quality is limited for EEG and thoracic modalities.
    • Glucagon-­Like Peptide-­1 Receptor Agonists in Obesity-­Induced Respiratory Pathophysiology
      Ruiz, Furquim, Davaanyam, Williams, Polotsky, Amorim | 2026
      The scientific paper published in Acta Physiologica (2026) explores the potential for Glucagon-Like Peptide-1 Receptor Agonists (GLP1RAs), traditionally used for Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (TD2M) and obesity, to address respiratory disorders like Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA). The study finds that Liraglutide and Tirzepatide, including the dual receiver long-acting agonist Tirzepatide which was recently approved by the FDA for OSA in overweight individuals, reduce AHI in patients with obesity and moderate/severe OSA. The research suggests that GLP1RAs may have a promising role in treating OSA, independent of weight loss, and ongoing clinical trials for triple-hormone receptor agonist retatrutide offer hope for future treatments in this area.
    • Heart-­Lung Interactions in Health and Disease
      Kadosh, Baidats, Jones, Wilkerson, Velner, Reuveny, Segel | 2026
      This study demonstrates that oxygen supplementation significantly improves exercise performance in patients with Pulmonary Arterial Hypertension (PAH) and Chronic Thromboembolic Pulmonary Hypertension (CTEPH), compared to room air, due to enhanced oxygen delivery to muscles and pulmonary vasodilation. However, supplemental oxygen does not improve exercise performance in healthy individuals. The study suggests potential benefits of oxygen supplementation for PAH/CTEPH patients during exercise training, challenging current guidelines that do not recommend O2 supplementation for non-hypoxemic patients, but further research is needed to confirm these findings.
      Keywords: CPET, exercise physiology, O2 supplementation, pulmonary vascular disease, V̇O2 kinetics
    • Hyperglycosylation is a metabolic driver of Alzheimer’s disease
      Liu, Ribas, Medina, Nielsen, Clarke, Ma, Mueller, Plasencia, Sheer, Simpson, Soto, Sudderth, Cai, Cantrell, Colpaert, Shedlock, Wu, Young, Kooser, Chen, Huang, He, Alonge, Guo, Bian, Vander Kooi, Gentry, Sun, Quinones, Son, Azadi, Deberardinis, Prokop, Allison, Yang, Chen, Huang, He, Guo, Gao, Kooi, Gentry, & Sun | 2026
      This Nature Metabolism study investigates the role of hyperglycosylation, specifically N-glycosylation, in Alzheimer's disease (AD). The research suggests that excessive N-glycosylation contributes to the conserved phenotype of brain hyperglycosylation in AD, and reducing this excess improves social recognition memory in AD mouse models. Genetic knockdown of glycan biosynthetic enzymes showed improved cognitive outcomes, while glucosamine supplementation worsened behavior in these mice. A retrospective analysis of electronic health records revealed that glucosamine supplementation was associated with accelerated AD progression and reduced survival in patients with mild cognitive impairment (MCI) and dementia, highlighting the need for further investigation into cell-type-specific spatial information and pharmacological interventions targeting the hexosamine pathway in animal models.
    • Intracellular calcium release activates carotid body afferents by fentanyl
      Peng, Kabil, Wang, Xie, Fox, Prabhakar | 2025
      The research reveals that fentanyl activates carotid body afferents through a KOR-Gaq-PLC-IP3R-Ca2+ signaling pathway, which increases intracellular calcium levels in glomus cells and counteracts opioid-induced respiratory depression. This newly discovered mechanism may help reduce opioid-related respiratory complications and enhance opioid safety.
      Keywords: carotid body afferents, G-protein coupled receptors, inositol trisphosphate (IP3)
    • Is sustained hypercapnia required to initiate plasticity in humans exposed to mild intermittent hypoxia?
      Mateika, Barok, Kissane | 2024
      The opinion paper published in The Journal of Physiology (2024) challenges the common belief that sustained hypercapnia is necessary for initiating plasticity during mild intermittent hypoxia exposure in humans, particularly in the context of Methylene blue-induced hypoxia with sustained carbon dioxide (MIHCO2). The paper proposes preliminary findings suggesting that MIHCO2 improves upper airway patency and has potential direct effects on cardiovascular outcomes, as shown by a significant decrease in blood pressure following repeated daily exposure to MIHCO2 in OSA patients treated nightly with CPAP. Future research will investigate the efficacy of different doses of MIHCO2, hypoxic sensitivity, and potential safety issues associated with exposure.
    • MINIREVIEW Intermittent Hypoxia: Cause of or Therapy for Systemic Hypertension?
      Serebrovskaya, Manukhina, Smith, Downey, Mallet | 2008
      This mini-review discusses the conflicting effects of Intermittent Hypoxia (IH) on systemic hypertension, particularly its potential as a treatment due to its ability to lower blood pressure in individuals with hypertension. The review highlights research conducted primarily in the former Soviet Union, focusing on mechanisms such as enhanced sarcoplasmic reticular Ca2+ sequestration and increased antioxidant defenses that contribute to IH's effects on preventing endothelial dysfunction and high blood pressure in rats with various forms of experimental hypertension. The article also distinguishes Intermittent Hypoxia Treatment (IHT) from Obstructive Sleep Apnea, emphasizing IHT's potential benefits over traditional drug therapies due to its minimal side effects and possible stimulation of an organism's general resistance. However, the study concludes that further research is necessary to determine safe and effective protocols for IH application. The paper also provides references related to nitric oxide, intermittent hypoxia, and cardiovascular physiology.
      Keywords: angiogenesis, hypertension, intermittent hypoxia, nitric oxide, obstructive sleep apnea
    • Neuromodulation for the treatment of moderate to severe asthma – a pilot first-in-human clinical study
      Gehlsen, Carrasco, Hooghius, Pyles | 2025
      The July 2025 study published in the Journal of Asthma presents two pilot clinical trials investigating neuromodulation as a potential treatment for moderate to severe asthma. One trial used a non-invasive device to stimulate the vagus nerve, showing significant improvements in pulmonary function tests and asthma symptom scores after six weeks for 10 participants. The other study involved SCS implantation in 11 patients with chronic pain, resulting in complete elimination of asthma symptoms within days, improved asthma control, reduced medication usage, and enhanced quality of life. Future research will focus on larger controlled clinical studies to further establish the safety and efficacy of these neuromodulation techniques as treatment options for asthma patients.
    • Noradrenergic neuromodulation produces a NMDAR-dependent network state of respiratory rhythmogenesis in the preBötzinger Complex
      Tryba, Viemari, Wang, Garcia | 2026
      This preprint study investigates the effects of Norepinephrine (NE) and Nitric Oxide (NO) on the preBotanic Complex (preBötC), a critical brainstem region for respiratory rhythm generation, in rodents. The research finds that NE increases action potential frequency while decreasing the average action potential number during network bursts, and NO attenuates synchrony by activating soluble guanylyl cyclase and cGMP-dependent protein kinase (PKG). Combined application of NA and NO leads to complex interactions with overall effects dependent on their relative concentrations. The study suggests potential roles for NE and NO modulation in abnormal respiratory patterns associated with chronic hypoxia, offering insights for new therapeutic strategies for treating hypoxia-related respiratory disorders. However, further peer review is necessary as this is a preprint.
    • Novel oxygen sensing mechanism in the spinal cord involved in cardiorespiratory responses to hypoxia
      Barioni, Derakhshan, Tenorio Lopes, Onimaru, Roy, McDonald, Scheibli, Baghdadwala, Heidari, Bharadia, Ikeda, Yazawa, Okada, Harris, Dutschmann, Wilson | 2022
      The study by Barioni et al., published in Science Advances, identifies a novel oxygen sensing mechanism in the spinal cord's sympathetic preganglionic neurons (SSPNs), which contributes to cardiorespiratory responses during hypoxia. This mechanism involves nitric oxide synthase 1 (NOS1) and nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADPH) oxidase (NOX2). The research has potential clinical implications for various respiratory and sympathetic conditions, such as obesity, atherosclerosis, diabetes, asthma, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, sleep apnea, acute mountain sickness, subdued carotid body activity, intermittent hypoxia-induced cardiorespiratory plasticity, and severe asphyxia-like conditions.
    • Obstructive Sleep Apnea, Positive Airway Pressure, and Implications of Early Treatment in Parkinson Disease
      Lee, Neilson, Montaño, May, Sicard, Cho, Iliff, Elliott, Lim, Scott | 2025
      The scientific paper 'Obstructive Sleep Apnea, Positive Airway Pressure, and Implications of Early Treatment in Parkinson Disease' suggests that Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) is a modifiable midlife risk factor for developing Parkinson's disease (PD), especially among women. The study found a higher incidence of PD among veterans with OSA compared to those without, and early treatment with CPAP significantly reduced the number of PD cases and associated risks such as falls, fractures, and mortality. However, limitations include an inability to assess CPAP adherence beyond administrative codes and a need for further prospective studies to confirm and extend these findings.
    • Protocol for head-restrained drug self-administration in mice
      Doncheck, Clarke, Gordon, Boquiren, Manusky, Baek, Paniccia, Dinu, Dereschewitz, Grant, Stuber, Otis | 2026
      This protocol article describes a low-cost, standardized method for training head-fixed mice to self-administer drugs using 3D-printed equipment and open-source software (REACHER/Labrynth). The aim is to facilitate research on drug reward and motivation in rodents using head fixation systems. Key steps include habituating mice, training nose poke behavior, validating self-administration procedures, and implementing experimental manipulations like modulating drug doses or reward schedules. The method is compatible with neurotechnologies requiring mechanical stability for studying substance use disorders at unprecedented resolution.
    • Pulmonary and carotid body afferents in OIRD
      Peng, Ramirez, Prabhakar | 2026
      The scientific paper "Pulmonary and carotid body afferents in Opioid-Induced Respiratory Depression (OIRD)" investigates the effects of opioids on various bodily systems, particularly the respiratory system, through a review of previous studies and experiments on rodent models. Key findings reveal that morphine and enkephalins activate vagal pulmonary C-fiber afferents, causing apnea and increased laryngeal nerve activity, while opioids also stimulate carotid body activity via κ-opioid receptors (k -ORs), involving calcium signaling in glomus cells. The research provides insights into potential new therapeutic strategies for managing OIRD.
      Keywords: Pulmonary vagal C fibers, Laryngeal constriction, Carotid body, Glomus cells, k-opioid receptors, Ca2+ signaling
    • Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance
      Kipnis, Benveniste, Eichmann, Thomas, Reich, Lewis, Tsai, Drieu, Bakker, Kelley, Lundgaard, Mestre, Zott, Eide, van Osch, van Veluw, Iliff, Yang, Santambrogio, Da Mesquita, Daneman, Rustenhoven, Goldman, Nedergaard
      The scientific paper "Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance" aims to advance our understanding of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) dynamics in brain waste clearance, focusing on periarterial spaces, glymphatic and meningeal lymphatic systems, their functional connections, and immune cell involvement. Key findings reveal primary CSF influx along periarterial spaces, solute clearance through multiple routes, and functional influence by vascular pulsation, neural activity, sleep, and immunity. The study highlights the role of immune cells at brain-border niches in monitoring solute efflux, linking brain clearance to neuroimmune interactions. The research aims to uncover new therapeutic avenues for various disorders such as autoimmune, neurodegenerative, neoplastic, and psychiatric diseases.
    • Respiratory-related evoked potential measures of respiratory sensory gating
      Chan, Davenport | 2008
      The scientific study by Chan and Davenport (2008) investigates the principle of sensory gating in respiratory sensation using paired occlusion-elicited RREPs (Respiratory-related Event-Related Potentials). The research confirms the presence of temporal sensory gating for respiratory mechanoreceptor stimuli, somatosensory stimuli via skin air puffs, and auditory stimuli. Reduced sensory gating, or increased S2/S1 ratio, could potentially serve as a marker for respiratory-related anxiety disorders in asthma patients.
      Keywords: respiratory sensation, inspiratory occlusion, cerebral cortex, mechanosensation
    • Role of circadian gene expression in the divergent effects of intermittent hypoxia and sleep fragmentation on blood pressure
      Chen, Yu, Xi | 2026
      The review article published in Clinical Science (2026) examines the impact of Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) on blood pressure regulation through disruption of circadian gene expression, focusing on the role of hypoxia-inducible factor 1α (HIF-1α). The research suggests that while conventional treatments like CPAP are effective for OSA, novel therapies targeting circadian clock genes could improve cardiovascular health, particularly in populations with disrupted sleep patterns or heightened salt sensitivity. Controlled moderate intermittent hypoxia is proposed to have potential benefits on the heart and vasculatures through modulation of specific gene expressions, but further research is needed for effective implementation of these novel therapies.
    • Sleep Disorders: Cholinergic premotor modulation of hypoglossal motor activity identified from combined ‘opto-dialysis’ in vivo
      Amin Ladha, Raina, da Silveira Scarpellini, Carolina, Liu, Wen-Ying, Hattie Liu, Liu, Kevin Patrick Grace, Kevin, Montandon, Gaspard, Horner, Richard L. | 2026
      The research paper, led by R. Amin Ladha and R. L. Horner, explores the role of cholinergic neurons in controlling tongue movements during sleep using a combined optogenetics (optical manipulation of neurons) and dialysis (substance removal from living organisms) approach. The study reveals complex mechanisms of muscarinic acetylcholine receptor (MAchR) and nicotinic acetylcholine receptor (N AchR) modulation in respiratory hypoglossal motor output from the HMN, offering potential targets for future therapeutic interventions related to sleep disorders.
      Keywords: genioglossus, hypoglossal motor nucleus, obstructive sleep apnea, upper airway
    • Spinal cord stimulation for upper limb motor function in people with chronic post-stroke hemiparesis: a feasibility trial
      2026
      The Nature Medicine study investigates the feasibility of cervical epidural spinal cord stimulation (SCS) for improving upper limb motor function in individuals with chronic post-stroke hemiparesis over a four-week period. Significant improvements were observed in strength, reduced spasticity, and increased Fugl-Meyer Assessment points. The study suggests that cervical SCS is safe, feasible, and effective for chronic post-stroke hemiparesis, but further research is needed to determine long-term effectiveness and safety, as well as the determinants of responsiveness to SCS, particularly spared sensory function.
    • The Rostral Ventrolateral Medulla Input to Central Amygdala Regulates Anxiety-­Like Behaviors in Mice for Chronic Light Exposure
      Jing, Tan, Xu, Yuan, Fan, Wang | 2026
      This Original Article, published in 2026 in CNS Neuroscience & Therapeutics, investigates the impact of chronic light exposure on anxiety-like behaviors in mice and the role of the rostral ventrolateral medulla (RVLM) input to the central amygdala (CeA). The study reveals that chronic light exposure enhances anxiety-like behaviors, which are regulated by the RVLM input to the CeA, suggesting a potential novel approach for treating anxiety disorders under conditions of prolonged light exposure. However, the research was limited by not addressing physical or pathological disconnection effects under light exposure conditions, being conducted exclusively in male mice, and not publicly releasing the data due to privacy or ethical restrictions. Future studies are necessary to validate the RVLM→CeA bidirectional feedback loop hypothesis and determine whether findings generalize to female subjects.
      Keywords: anxiety-­like behaviors, blood pressure, central amygdala, chronic light exposure, rostral ventrolateral medulla
    • The crucial role of normative ageing in respiratory pathogenesis
      Weight*, Tatler*, Huckstepp*, Adcock, Barnes, van Beek, Christopher, Clifford, Dhasmana, Hart, Hashmat, Noble, Sayers, Short, Stinson, Calimport†, Bentley†
      This scientific paper, written by the International Consortium to Classify Ageing-Related Pathologies (ICCARP) respiratory working group, explores genetic, epigenetic, and inflammatory factors contributing to age-related changes in lung function. Key findings suggest chronic low-grade inflammation plays a significant role in these changes, while senescent cells, telomere shortening, and altered secretomes also contribute. The paper proposes classifying pulmonary fibrosis driven by senescence as an ageing-related pathology and advocates for a multiprofessional approach to manage lung aging due to its complex nature involving both physiological and psychological aspects. However, the research relies heavily on inference from currently classified diseases and has a paucity of human data, highlighting the need for further study of human tissue samples across various ages to better understand lung ageing, particularly regarding the mucociliary system.
    • The hypoxic ventilatory response and arousal burden predict the magnitude of ventilatory long-term facilitation in humans with obstructive sleep apnoea
      Mateika, Hammo, Kissane, Azarbarzin | 2025
      In a 2025 study, researchers led by Jason H. Mateika found that hypoxic ventilatory response (HVR) and arousal burden are significant predictors of progressive augmentation (PA) and ventilatory long-term facilitation (VLTF) in individuals with obstructive sleep apnea (OSA). Arousal burden might be a more accurate predictor of the magnitude of PA or VLTF than hypoxic burden. The study was funded by several institutions, including the United States Department of Veterans Affairs and the American Academy of Sleep Medicine Foundation.
      Keywords: arousal, intermittent hypoxia, obstructive sleep apnoea, respiratory plasticity
    • [3] OBSTRUCTIVE SLEEP APNEA ENDOTYPE PREDICTION DURING WAKEFULNESS USING VOICE BIOMARKERS
      Akbari, Taghibeyglou, Assadi, Madulid, Brown, Vena, Sands, Yadollahi
      This study investigates the potential of voice biomarkers for predicting different Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) endotypes during wakefulness as a non-invasive alternative to overnight polysomnography (PSG). The researchers used machine learning algorithms on voice recordings from 390 participants and found that specific combinations of biomarkers significantly distinguished between mild, moderate, and severe OSA endotypes. The study reports high correlation coefficients for airway collapsibility and muscle compensation endotypes, suggesting a simplified and cost-effective method for OSA diagnosis and monitoring. However, further validation in larger and more diverse cohorts is necessary to confirm its potential.
      Keywords: Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA), Endotype Prediction, Voice Biomarkers, Supervised Autoencoder, Feature Selection, Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP)

    TBI_SCI

    • Body-Brain Integration: The Lower Brainstem in Sleep-Wake Regulation
      Yao, Dan | 2026
      This review paper published in Annual Review of Neuroscience (2026) emphasizes the role of the lower brainstem, specifically key regions such as the Nucleus of the Solitary Tract, Parabrachial Nucleus, Medulla, Locus Coeruleus, and others, in regulating sleep-wake cycles, arousal, and motor output. The lower brainstem functions as an integrated network linking sleep-wake regulation to the body's homeostatic demands, serving as a master coordinator for global brain states and peripheral bodily functions, which has implications for developing treatments for sleep disorders.
      Keywords: sleep-wake regulation, brainstem, body-brain integration, neural circuit
    • C5aR1+ microglia exacerbate neuroinflammation and cerebral edema in acute brain injury
      Zhou, Ma, Feng, Tian, Li, Guo, Shi, Cui, Dong, Hao, He, Xiao, Chen, Wu, Gao, Hu, Zhang, Wang, Zhou, Feng, Xue, Lin, Wang, Gao, Heng, Zhao, Luo, Tian, Zhang, Guo, Li, Liu, Ge, Liu, Qu | 2025
      The study by Zhou et al. discovers that a pro-inflammatory microglial subtype (C5aR1+) amplifies neuroinflammation, promotes harmful astrocyte polarization, and enhances neutrophil recruitment after traumatic brain injury (TBI) or intracerebral hemorrhage (ICH), exacerbating cerebral edema and neurological deficits. Pharmacological inhibition of C5aR1 reduces edema in preclinical models, suggesting its potential as a therapeutic target for TBI and ICH. The FDA-approved drug avacopan, a C5aR1 antagonist, shows promise in mitigating these harmful effects by suppressing pro-inflammatory cytokine expression, restoring microglial morphology, inhibiting AKT and ERK phosphorylation, reducing astrocyte polarization, decreasing infiltrated neutrophils, alleviating cerebral edema, and accelerating functional recovery after TBI and ICH. Further research is needed to confirm these findings and explore the full therapeutic potential of avacopan in these conditions.
    • Funding Distributions, Trends, Gaps, and Policy Implications for Spinal Cord Injury Research: A Systematic Analysis of U.S. Federal Funds
      Tucker Gillespie, Andrew Buxton, Bethany R. Kondiles, Miranda Leal-Garcia, Mia R. Pacheco, Ashley V. Tran, Katie Vo, Lucy Abu, James Barr, Tanya A. Barretto, Jason Biundo, Sam Duenwald, Abigail Evans, Timothy N. Friedman, Isabella Gadaleta, Saahas Ganesh, Bryson Gottschall, Peyton Green, Grant Lee, Lilian Liu, Raza N. Malik, Elizabeth J. Nava, Chiara Sorani, Vansh Tandon, Hannah Thomas, Kyndal Thomas, Chris Barr, Ian Burkhart, Dylan A. McCreedy, Peter Nowell, Heath Blackmon, Alexander G. Rabchevsky, Matthew Rodreick, Abel Torres-Espín, Jennifer N. Dulin | 2023
      The scholarly paper "Funding Distributions, Trends, Gaps, and Policy Implications for Spinal Cord Injury Research" analyzes U.S. federal funding for spinal cord injury (SCI) research between 1976 and 2021 to identify trends, distributions, gaps, and policy implications. The study found that the National Institutes of Health primarily supports basic and early-stage translational research, while the Department of Defense's CDMRP Spinal Cord Injury Research Program concentrates on applied and clinical research, particularly in pain, bladder function, and neuromodulatory device development. The Department of Veterans Affairs invests in rehabilitation-focused studies aimed at improving musculoskeletal and functional health outcomes. Critical gaps in funding were identified for high-priority areas like bowel/gastrointestinal health, cardiovascular function, and mental health. To address these issues, the authors propose developing a centralized, living research dashboard, forming working groups consisting of researchers, program staff, industry representatives, and individuals with lived SCI experience, improving standardization and transparency in grant award labeling and classification, and creating dynamic, publicly accessible platforms to track federally funded SCI research across agencies.
    • Linking spinal circuit reorganization to recovery after thoracic spinal cord injury
      Shevtsova, Lockhart, Rybak, Magnuson, Danner | 2025
      The study by Shevtsova et al., published in eLife, investigates the recovery of locomotor function following thoracic spinal cord injuries (hemisection or contusion) in rats using computational models. Key findings indicate that post-injury gait expression is significantly impacted by the drive to sublesional lumbar rhythm generators and lumbar commissural interneuron connectivity, with a critical balance between V0 and V3 connectivity for maintaining gait expression. The research offers insights into potential therapeutic targets for spinal cord injuries by restoring sublesional lumbar rhythm-generating circuits and commissural function tailored to injury symmetry.
    • Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance
      Kipnis, Benveniste, Eichmann, Thomas, Reich, Lewis, Tsai, Drieu, Bakker, Kelley, Lundgaard, Mestre, Zott, Eide, van Osch, van Veluw, Iliff, Yang, Santambrogio, Da Mesquita, Daneman, Rustenhoven, Goldman, Nedergaard
      The scientific paper "Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance" aims to advance our understanding of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) dynamics in brain waste clearance, focusing on periarterial spaces, glymphatic and meningeal lymphatic systems, their functional connections, and immune cell involvement. Key findings reveal primary CSF influx along periarterial spaces, solute clearance through multiple routes, and functional influence by vascular pulsation, neural activity, sleep, and immunity. The study highlights the role of immune cells at brain-border niches in monitoring solute efflux, linking brain clearance to neuroimmune interactions. The research aims to uncover new therapeutic avenues for various disorders such as autoimmune, neurodegenerative, neoplastic, and psychiatric diseases.
    • Respiratory modulation of neurophysiology and symptoms in athletes with sports-related concussion: a randomized crossover trial
      Wang, Zhu, Gao, Yu, Yan, Wang, Zhang | 2026
      The study "Slow Breathing Interventions for Athletes with Sport-Related Concussion: A Randomized Controlled Trial" finds that Resonance Breathing (RB) and Progressive Relaxation Breathing (PRB) reduce symptoms and improve cognitive functioning in athletes recovering from concussions by boosting prefrontal theta activity, enhancing attentional regulation, and potentially improving autonomic signaling coordination. However, the study's limitations include a small sample size, gender bias, exploratory correlation analyses, and lack of a sham control condition, suggesting that further research is needed to validate these findings as beneficial adjunctive approaches for concussion rehabilitation.
      Keywords: autonomic nervous system, electroencephalography, pelvic floor resonance breathing, resonance breathing, skin sympathetic nerve activity, sports-related concussion
    • Safety and feasibility of paired vagus nerve stimulation with rehabilitation for improving upper extremity function in people with cervical spinal cord injury: a pilot randomized controlled trial
      Korupolu, Yozbatiran, Miller, Shields, Tandon | 2026
      This scientific paper titled "Safety and feasibility of paired vagus nerve stimulation with rehabilitation for improving upper extremity function in people with cervical spinal cord injury: a pilot randomized controlled trial" aimed to evaluate the safety, feasibility, and therapeutic potential of pairing invasive Vagus Nerve Stimulation (VNS) with upper extremity rehabilitation in individuals with chronic cervical Spinal Cord Injury (SCI). The study demonstrated that the intervention was safe, well-tolerated, and feasible in SCI patients. At immediate post-intervention assessment, all participants receiving active VNS showed improvement in upper extremity motor function compared with baseline, whereas none of the participants in the control group showed improvement. However, these findings should be interpreted cautiously due to the small sample size and exploratory nature of the analysis. The study also found that transportation and access barriers affected recruitment and limited completion of in-clinic sessions, highlighting the need for more accessible and flexible rehabilitation delivery models. Further investigation is required with larger trials to determine the efficacy, durability, and optimal implementation of VNS paired with rehabilitation in spinal cord injury.
    • Test-retest reliability of threshold-tracking TMS and peripheral nerve testing in healthy adults and individuals with chronic cervical spinal cord injury
      Hrycyk, Gramm Kristensen, Tankisi, Spooren, Tedesco Triccas
      The study investigates the test-retest reliability of neuroexcitability measures using threshold-tracking methods in both healthy adults and individuals with chronic cervical spinal cord injury (SCI). It finds excellent relative reliability for TMS measures in both groups, high test-retest reliability for NET using the TROND protocol across all age groups, and the T-T RMT test being more efficient. Most parameters showed good to excellent reliability over time, but hyperpolarization slope may have lower reliability. The study concludes that threshold-tracking-based techniques are reliable and suitable for clinical research in populations with spasticity like those with stroke or SCI.
      Keywords: Transcranial magnetic stimulation (tms), Nerve excitability testing (net), Reliability, Spinal cord injury (SCI), Threshold tracking
    • The Rostral Ventrolateral Medulla Input to Central Amygdala Regulates Anxiety-­Like Behaviors in Mice for Chronic Light Exposure
      Jing, Tan, Xu, Yuan, Fan, Wang | 2026
      This Original Article, published in 2026 in CNS Neuroscience & Therapeutics, investigates the impact of chronic light exposure on anxiety-like behaviors in mice and the role of the rostral ventrolateral medulla (RVLM) input to the central amygdala (CeA). The study reveals that chronic light exposure enhances anxiety-like behaviors, which are regulated by the RVLM input to the CeA, suggesting a potential novel approach for treating anxiety disorders under conditions of prolonged light exposure. However, the research was limited by not addressing physical or pathological disconnection effects under light exposure conditions, being conducted exclusively in male mice, and not publicly releasing the data due to privacy or ethical restrictions. Future studies are necessary to validate the RVLM→CeA bidirectional feedback loop hypothesis and determine whether findings generalize to female subjects.
      Keywords: anxiety-­like behaviors, blood pressure, central amygdala, chronic light exposure, rostral ventrolateral medulla
    • Timed microglia depletion promotes functional network reorganization and motor recovery after ischaemic stroke
      Isla Cainzos, Quandt, Borggrewe, Altjohann, Magnus, Biskamp | 2026
      The study published in Brain Communications investigates the impact of short-term microglia depletion during the subacute phase following ischemic stroke on functional recovery and network dynamics in mice, using PLX5622. It found that this treatment significantly improved fine motor skills by day 7, as demonstrated by better performance in a sensorimotor test (SPR task), and revealed changes in sensorimotor network dynamics, including increased lower beta connectivity within the ipsilesional motor cortex during active periods, and a decrease in gamma connectivity in the contralesional sensorimotor system. The findings suggest that targeted suppression of activated microglia during the subacute phase may facilitate synaptic plasticity and network reorganization, enhancing functional recovery.
      Keywords: ischaemic stroke, CSF-1R inhibitor, microglial repopulation, sensorimotor networks, motor rehabilitation

      Wang, Zhu, Gao, Yu, Yan, Wang, Zhang | 2026

    • A projection atlas of excitatory and inhibitory inputs to the preB€otzinger complex: substrates for multimodal breathing control
      Reilly, Kadri, Arthurs, Sedano, Brezinski, Baertsch | 2021
      The research "A Projection Atlas of Excitatory and Inhibitory Inputs to the preBötzinger Complex" reveals that the preBötzinger complex, a critical region for rhythmic breathing in the brain, is modulated by diverse long-range excitatory and inhibitory inputs from various brain regions. The study identifies multiple sources of excitatory inputs converging on the preBötzinger complex, such as the caudal and rostral medullary regions, the rostral ventral respiratory group (rVRG), the lateral parafacial region/retrotrapezoid nucleus (PFl/RTN), and the spinal trigeminal nucleus caudalis (Sp5c). Inhibitory projections were predominantly ipsilateral to the injection site in the caudal medulla, while excitatory projections were found in the contralateral cVRG. This study provides a foundation for understanding the neural mechanisms underlying breathing regulation and could potentially lead to new treatments for respiratory disorders and sleep apnea.

    • Astrocytes: Orchestrators of brain gas exchange and oxygen homeostasis
      Christie | 2026
      Astrocytes play a critical role in brain oxygen sensing, cerebral perfusion, and cognition, with a higher sensitivity to changes in oxygen availability compared to glomus cells. They respond to hypoxia by releasing nitric oxide, regulating sympathetic drive, and cardiac output to maintain brain oxygenation. However, during ischemia, astrocytes can both protect neurons and exacerbate brain damage due to their involvement in neurotransmitter release, calcium signaling, and gap junction communication. Future research should explore therapeutic interventions targeting astrocytic functions for improving neurological disorders related to oxygen homeostasis, cognitive impairment, and dementia.
    • BDNF is necessary and sufficient for spinal respiratory plasticity following intermittent hypoxia
      Baker-Herman, Fuller, Bavis, Zabka, Golder, Doperalski, Johnson, Watters, Mitchell | 2003
      In the study, researchers found that brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) plays a crucial role in long-lasting phrenic facilitation (pLTF), a model of respiratory plasticity induced by intermittent hypoxia. The process involves serotonin-dependent BDNF synthesis near phrenic motoneurons, and BDNF administration elicits pLTF without hypoxia. Endogenous BDNF synthesis is necessary for pLTF, and the effect requires activation of a high affinity TrkB receptor. The findings could have implications for conditions such as Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS), obstructive sleep apnea, and respiratory failure following spinal cord injury.

    • Brain motion is driven by mechanical coupling with the abdomen
      Garborg, Ghitti, Zhang, Ricotta, Frank, Mueller, Greenawalt, Turner, Kedarasetti, Mostafa, Lee, Costanzo, Drew | 2026
      The study published in Nature Neuroscience investigates the motion of the dorsal cortex within the skull of awake mice, revealing that brain motion is primarily driven by locomotion rather than respiration or the cardiac cycle. Researchers found that forces generated by abdominal muscle contractions reach the brain via the vertebral venous plexus (VVP), a network of valveless veins connecting the abdomen and spinal canal, suggesting a potential hydraulic system driving cerebrospinal fluid flow.     • Breathing: Rhythmicity, Plasticity, Chemosensitivity

    • Decoding neuronal criticality firing patterns for large brain based EEG
      Beres, Ribeiro Rodrigues, Myers, Napoli
      This scientific study introduces a new method for decoding large-scale Electroencephalogram (EEG) neural recordings during cognitive tasks, focusing on demonstrating self-organization of neuronal networks indicative of neural criticality. By analyzing EEG data from 49 subjects and using hierarchical arrangements, the researchers discover power laws for multiple neuronal oscillatory groups, demonstrating scale-invariance through log–log plots, and characterizing criticality on the millisecond to seconds time scale relevant to cognitive tasks. This method could offer a significant advancement in understanding the dynamics of the neuronal network and its role in cognitive processes, potentially informing AI and machine learning developments.

    • Molecular mechanism of NMDA receptor potentiation by lactate
      Fiumelli, Herrera-López, Lemtiri-Chlieh, Mottier, Girgis, Ben-Adiba, Jourdain, Carrano, Mahmood, Ooi, Arold, Di Luca, Gardoni, Magistretti | 2026
      The research study published in The Journal of Physiology in 2026 investigates the role of lactate produced by astrocytes in enhancing neuronal NMDA receptor function, synaptic plasticity, and memory formation via a redox mechanism. This process involves lactate entering neurons through monocarboxylate transporters (MCTs), being oxidized to pyruvate, and generating NADH, which increases the intracellular NADH/NAD+ ratio. This redox shift promotes calcium release from ryanodine-sensitive stores (RyR) and recruits Ca2+/calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II (CaMKII) to GluN2B-containing NMDARs, leading to enhanced NMDAR currents and prolonged decay kinetics. This strengthened NMDAR–CaMKII signaling increases NMDAR presence at postsynaptic sites, facilitating the molecular processes underlying long-term synaptic plasticity and memory formation. The study was conducted by a team from various institutions and reveals a molecular pathway linking brain metabolism with learning and memory processes.

    • Regulation of sleep homeostasis mediator adenosine by basal forebrain glutamatergic neurons
      Peng*, Wu*, Song*, Zhang, Li, Xu†
      The research paper "GRABAdo1.0: A Novel Sensor for Extracellular Adenosine Detection and Its Role in Sleep Homeostasis" presents a study on glutamatergic neurons in the basal forebrain (BF), demonstrating their role as key regulators of sleep homeostasis through adenosine release. The study found higher adenosine levels during wakefulness and REM sleep compared to NREM sleep, and revealed a correlation between calcium activity in BF cholinergic neurons and adenosine levels. Optogenetic activation increased extracellular adenosine levels, while lesioning these glutamatergic neurons resulted in increased wakefulness during the active period, less recovery sleep after prolonged wakefulness, and impaired sleep rebound. The discussion section reviews existing studies on sleep regulation and neural activity in the basal forebrain, confirming adenosine's role in mediating homeostatic control of sleep in cholinergic BF neurons, and contributing new insights into how neural activity during wakefulness contributes to sleep pressure through the release of sleep-inducing factors by BF glutamatergic neurons.

    • Review: Neurologic mechanisms regulating cough in health and disease
      Mazzone, Pflaumer, Richards | 2026
      The 2026 review "Neurologic mechanisms regulating cough in health and disease" published by the American College of Allergy, Asthma & Immunology explores the neurology behind both acute and chronic cough, focusing on vagal sensory neurons and brainstem circuits. The study suggests that chronic cough is associated with peripheral sensory nerve fiber sensitivity and central neural mechanisms amplification, leading to excessive coughing in response to low levels of thermal, mechanical, or chemical exposure. Brain imaging studies suggest chronic cough may be a disorder of reduced inhibitory control, and therapies restoring sensorimotor balance could potentially control excessive cough. The review highlights the clinical significance of chronic cough and significant therapeutic advances in recent years for managing it, though uniformity in responsivity to trialed drugs indicates the need for therapies directed at specific endotypic mechanisms or responsive treatable traits for effective cough management. Funding was provided by the National Health and Medical Research Council of Australia, with some authors receiving grants and consulting fees from various pharmaceutical companies.

      Dempsey, McCrimmon
      The paper "CO2, V_CO2, and Breathing" argues against CO2/H+ chemoreception as the primary regulator of arterial partial pressure of carbon dioxide (PaCO2), particularly during non-rapid eye movement (NREM) sleep. Instead, it suggests that pulmonary CO2 exchange plays a crucial role in homeostatic ventilatory control by maintaining a near-proportional relationship between ventilation (V_A) and carbon dioxide production (V_CO2), with factors like body mass, food intake, exercise, and environmental changes playing a role. The research highlights the need for further investigation into potential mediators of these CO2 effects, such as the retrotrapezoid nucleus (RTN), to better understand the role of pulmonary CO2 exchange in PaCO2 homeostasis.
    • Ventral cervical EES in opioid-induced respiratory depression
      Huang, Zhou, Peyer, Galer, Patel, Madhavan, Le, Vinters, Salamon, Nuwer, Wu, Leiter, Lu | 2026
      The study found that mild to moderate intensity ventral cervical electrical stimulation at frequencies below 100 Hz did not significantly impact respiratory activity during opioid-induced respiratory depression in anesthetized humans undergoing anterior cervical spinal impingement surgeries, suggesting it may not directly access or indirectly activate ventral motor neurons during this condition.
    • Vip+ vagal neurons control allergen-induced responses
      Zhu, Su, Sun | 2026
      The study "Vip+ vagal neurons control allergen-induced responses" found that Vip+ vagal sensory neurons play a crucial role in asthma pathogenesis by mediating airway hyperreactivity and type 2 inflammation through the neurotrophin-NGFR signaling axis. These neurons, which are distinct from Tac1+ neurons, project extensively to the nTS region of the brainstem, revealing a neuroimmune mechanism along the body-brain axis contributing to asthma. The study highlights the functional importance of NGFR signaling in Vip+-neuron-mediated allergen responses, but further studies are needed to determine the role of VIP neuropeptide and its primary functional source in driving allergen responses.

    • Adenosine and ATP Link PCO2 to Cortical Excitability via pH
      Dulla, Dobelis, Pearson, Frenguelli, Staley, Masino | 2005
      This 2005 scientific paper published in Neuron investigates the impact of carbon dioxide (CO2) levels on neural function, particularly in the hippocampus, and finds that increased CO2 levels decrease neuronal excitability through adenosine A1 receptor activation, while decreased CO2 levels increase excitability. The study suggests that CO2 modulates neuronal excitability and epileptiform activity through its effects on adenosine and purines, which could have implications for the development of new therapies for epilepsy and other neurological disorders.
    • Adenosine, caffeine, and sleep–wake regulation: state of the science and perspectives
      Reichert, Deboer, Landolt | 2022

The study suggests potential clinical applications for improving sleep-related disorders and stresses the need for further research to understand the precise regulation and influence of these substances on sleep, particularly regarding circadian clock mechanisms.

    • Aquaporin-4 mis-localization slows glymphatic clearance of α-synuclein and promotes α-synuclein pathology and aggregate propagation
      Braun, Simon, Jang, Sanderson, Swierz, Sevao, Pincus, Schaser, Elliott, Lim, Unni, Schindler, Keene, Latimer, Iliff | 2024

This impairment promotes the aggregation of alpha-synuclein and potentially contributes to the development and propagation of synucleinopathies like Lewy Body Dementia and Parkinson's disease.     • Human forebrain neural synchronization and entrainment to breathing during wakefulness, sleep, and external mechanical ventilation
      Mowla, Rhone, Kumar, Kovach, Liu, Chan, Kawasaki, Mueller, Kuhn, Frede, Ciliberto, Czech, Thati Ganganna, Owens, Dabrowski, Sprigg, Granner, Simonyan, Nourski, Krause, Banks, Howard III, Davenport, Pattinson, Richerson, Wemmie, Dlouhy | 2026
      The research "Human Forebrain Neural Synchronization and Entrainment to Breathing During Wakefulness, Sleep, and External Mechanical Ventilation" found that the human forebrain synchronizes with breathing rhythms during various states, including wakefulness, sleep, and mechanical ventilation. The study discovered multiple afferent pathways for this synchronization, with possible unique changes in humans compared to rodents. Key brain regions like the insula, somatosensory cortex, anterior cingulate cortex, amygdala, and hippocampus showed synchronization during wakefulness, and the amygdala and hippocampus continued this synchronization during sleep. The findings could have implications for understanding sleep apnea, epilepsy, and other neurological disorders.
    • Regulation of sleep homeostasis mediator adenosine by basal forebrain glutamatergic neurons
      Peng*, Wu*, Song*, Zhang, Li, Xu†
      The research paper "GRABAdo1.0: A Novel Sensor for Extracellular Adenosine Detection and Its Role in Sleep Homeostasis" presents a study on glutamatergic neurons in the basal forebrain (BF), demonstrating their role as key regulators of sleep homeostasis through adenosine release. The study found higher adenosine levels during wakefulness and REM sleep compared to NREM sleep, and revealed a correlation between calcium activity in BF cholinergic neurons and adenosine levels. Optogenetic activation increased extracellular adenosine levels, while lesioning these glutamatergic neurons resulted in increased wakefulness during the active period, less recovery sleep after prolonged wakefulness, and impaired sleep rebound. The 'ion (38)' experiment showed statistically significant differences in NREM sleep time between the lesion group and control group. The discussion section reviews existing studies on sleep regulation and neural activity in the basal forebrain, confirming adenosine's role in mediating homeostatic control of sleep in cholinergic BF neurons, and contributing new insights into how neural activity during wakefulness contributes to sleep pressure through the release of sleep-inducing factors by BF glutamatergic neurons.
    • Role of circadian gene expression in the divergent effects of intermittent hypoxia and sleep fragmentation on blood pressure
      Chen, Yu, Xi | 2026
      The review article published in Clinical Science (2026) examines the impact of Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) on blood pressure regulation through disruption of circadian gene expression, focusing on the role of hypoxia-inducible factor 1α (HIF-1α). The research suggests that while conventional treatments like CPAP are effective for OSA, novel therapies targeting circadian clock genes could improve cardiovascular health, particularly in populations with disrupted sleep patterns or heightened salt sensitivity. Controlled moderate intermittent hypoxia is proposed to have potential benefits on the heart and vasculatures through modulation of specific gene expressions, but further research is needed for effective implementation of these novel therapies.
    • The Rostral Ventrolateral Medulla Input to Central Amygdala Regulates Anxiety-­Like Behaviors in Mice for Chronic Light Exposure
      Jing, Tan, Xu, Yuan, Fan, Wang | 2026
      This Original Article, published in 2026 in CNS Neuroscience & Therapeutics, investigates the impact of chronic light exposure on anxiety-like behaviors in mice and the role of the rostral ventrolateral medulla (RVLM) input to the central amygdala (CeA). The study reveals that chronic light exposure enhances anxiety-like behaviors, which are regulated by the RVLM input to the CeA, suggesting a potential novel approach for treating anxiety disorders under conditions of prolonged light exposure. However, the research was limited by not addressing physical or pathological disconnection effects under light exposure conditions, being conducted exclusively in male mice, and not publicly releasing the data due to privacy or ethical restrictions. Future studies are necessary to validate the RVLM→CeA bidirectional feedback loop hypothesis and determine whether findings generalize to female subjects.
      Keywords: anxiety-­like behaviors, blood pressure, central amygdala, chronic light exposure, rostral ventrolateral medulla
    • The Small Molecule Nobiletin Targets the Molecular Oscillator to Enhance Circadian Rhythms and Protect against Metabolic Syndrome
      He, Nohara, Park, Yong-Sung Park, Guillory, Zhao, Garcia, Koike, Chen Lee, Takahashi, Yoo, Chen | 2016
      This scientific paper investigates Nobiletin (NOB), a compound found in citrus peels, as a potential circadian clock amplitude enhancer that combats metabolic syndrome. The research finds that NOB functions as an agonist for Retinoid-related orphan receptors (RORs) in the circadian oscillator, improving circadian clock transcript oscillations and restoring clock protein rhythms in the liver of diet-induced obese mice. When administered to these mice, NOB counters metabolic syndrome by augmenting energy expenditure and locomotor activity, offering potential benefits for treating diseases like metabolic disorders, mood and sleep disorders, and aging-related conditions with dampened circadian amplitude. The research was supported by various organizations and accepted on March 14, 2016.
    • The neuroscience of cancer
      Mancusi1, Monje1,2 | 2023
      The scientific paper investigates complex interactions between the nervous system, cancer cells, and immune cells in various types of cancers, such as glioma, gastrointestinal, oral squamous cell carcinomas, and pancreatic cancer. It reveals that neuronal activity promotes cancer progression, co-opted by cancer cells to enhance metastasis potential. Key findings suggest new treatment strategies like preoperative β-blockade, serotonin attenuation, and vagus nerve stimulation for cancer management.

    • A neuronal architecture underlying autonomic dysreflexia
      Soriano, Hudelle, Mahe, Gautier, Teo, Skinnider, Laskaratos, Ceto, Kathe, Hutson, Charbonneau, Girgis, Casha, Rimok, Tso, Larkin-Kaiser, Gandhi, Amir, Kang, Vyza, Martin-Moraud, Lacour, Demesmaeker, Asboth, Barraud, Anderson, Bloch, Squair, Phillips, Courtine | 2025
      The scientific paper explores the neuronal architecture responsible for autonomic dysreflexia (AD) in mice with spinal cord injury, focusing on maladaptive connections between lower lumbosacral and lower thoracic spinal cord after SCI. Key findings suggest vGLUT2ON excitatory neurons are activated during AD, with Vsx2ON being the most transcriptionally responsive subpopulation. The study also discusses an implantable system designed to restore hemodynamic stability in individuals with spinal cord injuries, featuring epidural electrodes, a brain-computer interface, and a closed-loop control system, which showed improved results compared to the control group in preclinical models and one human participant.     • A neuroprosthesis for restoring hand movement and sensation in a person with complete tetraplegia
      Bouton | 2026
      The scientific paper presents a hybrid Dual Neuromodulation (DNB) system that combines an intracortical brain-computer interface, transcutaneous spinal cord stimulation, and peripheral stimuli for restoring motor and sensory functions in individuals with spinal cord injuries. The study on one participant showed significant improvements in motor control and sensory recovery, with potential for home use. Two related studies were discussed, both focusing on neural modulation in response to tactile stimuli and emphasizing the importance of understanding brain activity associated with different body parts and various types of stimuli.     • Expert-level probabilistic breathing event detector informs phenotyping of sleep apnea
      Kjaer, Hanif, Brink-Kjaer, Olsen, Sum-Ping, Carrillo, Sands, Redline, Stone, Jennum, Mignot | 2026
      This study introduces an expert-level probabilistic breathing event detector (ABED), a deep learning model trained on over 5000 polysomnographies, to enhance sleep apnea phenotyping by providing accurate identification of various types of sleep-disordered breathing events and offering insights into their etiology. The model can outperform human experts in large-scale studies and shows potential benefits for increased diagnosis rates and more consistent scoring of apnea events. However, its robustness to low signal quality is limited for EEG and thoracic modalities.
    • Galvanin (TMEM154) is an electric-field sensor for directed cell migration
      Belliveau, Footer, Platenkamp, Rodriguez, Kim, Prinz, van Loon, Lin, Eustis, Chan, Cohen, Theriot | 2026
      The research published in Cell (2026) by Belliveau et al. identifies Galvanin as a crucial electric-field sensor essential for directed migration in various rapidly moving cells such as immune and epithelial cells. Upon exposure to electric fields, Galvanin localizes at the front and rear of cells, changing spatial patterns, exhibiting an exponential decay with a net charge of -18e, and regulating subsequent movement responses. The study suggests that a highly negatively charged construct can rescue directed migration in knockout cell lines, indicating a high net negative ectodomain charge is sufficient for Galvanin-mediated electrotaxis. Further studies are needed to understand the molecular mechanisms underlying Galvanin's role in electrotaxis and its implications for cell signaling and other biological processes.

(2026) explores the feasibility of human V2a spinal interneurons derived from optogenetically engineered human induced pluripotent stem cells as a potential treatment for repairing spinal cord injuries in rats, demonstrating their ability to survive, integrate anatomically and synaptically connect with denervated host networks. However, further research is needed to optimize transplantation techniques, assess efficacy in larger animal models or clinical trials, and address limitations like ectopic migration, variability in cell survival, and the influence of the local injury environment on differentiation or maturation of transplanted cells.

    • A projection atlas of excitatory and inhibitory inputs to the preB€otzinger complex: substrates for multimodal breathing control
      Reilly, Kadri, Arthurs, Sedano, Brezinski, Baertsch | 2021
      The research "A Projection Atlas of Excitatory and Inhibitory Inputs to the preBötzinger Complex" reveals that the preBötzinger complex, a critical region for rhythmic breathing in the brain, is modulated by diverse long-range excitatory and inhibitory inputs from various brain regions. The study identifies multiple sources of excitatory inputs converging on the preBötzinger complex, such as the caudal and rostral medullary regions, the rostral ventral respiratory group (rVRG), the lateral parafacial region/retrotrapezoid nucleus (PFl/RTN), and the spinal trigeminal nucleus caudalis (Sp5c). Inhibitory projections were predominantly ipsilateral to the injection site in the caudal medulla, while excitatory projections were found in the contralateral cVRG. This study provides a foundation for understanding the neural mechanisms underlying breathing regulation and could potentially lead to new treatments for respiratory disorders and sleep apnea.
      Keywords: breathing, inputs, preB€otzinger complex, projections, respiratory circuits

    • Brain motion is driven by mechanical coupling with the abdomen

      The study published in Nature Neuroscience investigates the motion of the dorsal cortex within the skull of awake mice, revealing that brain motion is primarily driven by locomotion rather than respiration or the cardiac cycle. Researchers found that forces generated by abdominal muscle contractions reach the brain via the vertebral venous plexus (VVP), a network of valveless veins connecting the abdomen and spinal canal, suggesting a potential hydraulic system driving cerebrospinal fluid flow. The findings suggest a mechanical link between the brain and the abdominal compartment, with implications for understanding pathologies such as intracranial hypertension, meningioma, Chiari malformations, and conditions like obesity that elevate intra-abdominal pressure.
    • Bringing the genetically minimal cell to life on a computer in 4D
      Thornburg, Maytin, Kwon, Brier, Gilbert, Fu, Gao, Quenneville, Wu, Li, Long, Pezeshkian, Sun, Matias de C. Bittencourt, Glass, Mehta, Ha, Luthey-Schulten | 2026
      The text presents a four-dimensional whole-cell model (4DWCM) that simulates the entire cell cycle of JCVI-syn3A, a minimal bacterium with a synthetic genome, accounting for genetic information processes, metabolism, growth, and cell division. The model integrates hybrid computational methods to account for morphological transformations during growth, allowing for predictions not only for average behavior but also the heterogeneity among cells. The 4DWCM recovers experimental measurements and accurately predicts various cellular properties such as doubling times, mRNA half-lives, protein distributions, and ribosome counts, providing a significant advancement in understanding fundamental behaviors in cellular life and potential applications in targeted therapy design.

    • Molecular mechanism of NMDA receptor potentiation by lactate
      Fiumelli, Herrera-López, Lemtiri-Chlieh, Mottier, Girgis, Ben-Adiba, Jourdain, Carrano, Mahmood, Ooi, Arold, Di Luca, Gardoni, Magistretti | 2026
      The research study published in The Journal of Physiology in 2026 investigates the role of lactate produced by astrocytes in enhancing neuronal NMDA receptor function, synaptic plasticity, and memory formation via a redox mechanism. This process involves lactate entering neurons through monocarboxylate transporters (MCTs), being oxidized to pyruvate, and generating NADH, which increases the intracellular NADH/NAD+ ratio. This redox shift promotes calcium release from ryanodine-sensitive stores (RyR) and recruits Ca2+/calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II (CaMKII) to GluN2B-containing NMDARs, leading to enhanced NMDAR currents and prolonged decay kinetics. This strengthened NMDAR–CaMKII signaling increases NMDAR presence at postsynaptic sites, facilitating the molecular processes underlying long-term synaptic plasticity and memory formation. The study was conducted by a team from various institutions and reveals a molecular pathway linking brain metabolism with learning and memory processes.
    • Resistance training-induced adaptations in the neuromuscular system: Physiological mechanisms and implications for human performance
      Lecce, Amoruso, Felici, Bazzucchi | 2026
      This topical review published in The Journal of Physiology by Edoardo Lecce's team from the University of Rome 'Foro Italico' explores neuromuscular adaptations resulting from resistance training (RT). They find increased excitability, improved synaptic input transmission, and decreased synaptic noise in the brain and spinal cord, leading to a more stable neural drive. At the spinal level, intrinsic motoneuron excitability, neuromodulatory input, and spinal circuitry are modified, resulting in enhanced motor unit (MU) excitability due to attenuated inhibitory pathways. Skeletal muscle adaptations involve contractile protein synthesis, structural repair by satellite cells, and endurance capacity improvement through mitochondrial biogenesis and capillarization. These changes lead to increased strength, improved physical performance, and alterations in muscle fiber type composition, energy metabolism, glucose uptake, and hormone levels, with practical applications for healthy individuals and those affected by chronic disorders.
    • Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance

      The scientific paper "Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance" aims to advance our understanding of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) dynamics in brain waste clearance, focusing on periarterial spaces, glymphatic and meningeal lymphatic systems, their functional connections, and immune cell involvement. The research aims to uncover new therapeutic avenues for various disorders such as autoimmune, neurodegenerative, neoplastic, and psychiatric diseases.
    • Slowed canonical progress in large fields of science
      Chu, Evans | 2021
      The study by Johan S. G. Chua and James A. Evans suggests that an abundance of scientific publications in large fields can lead to stagnation due to cognitive overload and competition among many new ideas, causing established papers to maintain their citations while new ideas struggle to gain traction. The findings support this theory, revealing that as fields grow, top-cited papers become durably dominant, and policy measures such as establishing clear journal hierarchies, rewarding novel contributions, adopting a measure of novelty, and revising graduate training might help encourage innovation in science.

      Esvald, Avarlaid, Koppel, Tuvikene, Timmusk | 2026
      This scientific paper explores the complex regulation of Brain-Derived Neurotrophic Factor (BDNF), a protein essential for brain development, learning, and memory, focusing on various transcription factors and four enhancer regions within the BDNF gene. The research also emphasizes post-transcriptional regulation of BDNF, underscoring the roles of distinct 5' and 3' UTRs in controlling subcellular localization and translation. The study highlights the need for further investigation into the regulatory landscape, non-neural tissue expression, and physiological significance of 3' UTRs in regulating cell type-specific BDNF expression to better understand its intricate regulatory processes and implications for brain health and disease.
    • V_ CO2: the essential underpinning to homeostatic control of breathing
      Dempsey, McCrimmon
      The paper "CO2, V_CO2, and Breathing" argues against CO2/H+ chemoreception as the primary regulator of arterial partial pressure of carbon dioxide (PaCO2), particularly during non-rapid eye movement (NREM) sleep. Instead, it suggests that pulmonary CO2 exchange plays a crucial role in homeostatic ventilatory control by maintaining a near-proportional relationship between ventilation (V_A) and carbon dioxide production (V_CO2), with factors like body mass, food intake, exercise, and environmental changes playing a role. The research highlights the need for further investigation into potential mediators of these CO2 effects, such as the retrotrapezoid nucleus (RTN), to better understand the role of pulmonary CO2 exchange in PaCO2 homeostasis.

    • Adenosine and ATP Link PCO2 to Cortical Excitability via pH

      This 2005 scientific paper published in Neuron investigates the impact of carbon dioxide (CO2) levels on neural function, particularly in the hippocampus, and finds that increased CO2 levels decrease neuronal excitability through adenosine A1 receptor activation, while decreased CO2 levels increase excitability. The study suggests that CO2 modulates neuronal excitability and epileptiform activity through its effects on adenosine and purines, which could have implications for the development of new therapies for epilepsy and other neurological disorders.
    • Adenosine signalling drives antidepressant actions of ketamine and ECT
      Yue1,2,3,4,5,12, Wang2,6,12, Zhai7,8, Yuan1,2,4, Cui1,2,4, Quan1,2,4, Zhou1,2,4, Fan2, Wang7, Wu9, Mi1,2, Ge1,2, Li10,11, Wang7,8, Luo1,2,3,4,11 | 2026
      This research investigates the role of adenosine in ketamine's antidepressant effects, revealing that ketamine increases adenosine levels in the mouse brain, particularly in the prefrontal cortex and hippocampus. The study suggests DCK (2C-DCK) and 3′-Cl-ketamine as promising new antidepressants due to their stronger and longer-lasting adenosine surges than ketamine.     • Amino acid modulation of carotid body function
      Gold, Pauza, Felippe, Shen, Paton | 2026
      This scientific paper published in The Journal of Physiology investigates the role of amino acids, particularly glutamate, in modulating carotid body function for arterial chemoreception and respiratory regulation. The study finds that during hypoxia, glutamate stimulates postsynaptic petrosal neurons and adjacent glomus cells, while GABA inhibits excitation of neighboring glomus cells, creating an 'accelerator-brake' mechanism that regulates the sensitivity of the carotid body to hypoxia. The study suggests that amino acids such as glutamate and GABA play significant roles in regulating respiratory control through carotid body function, particularly contributing to long-term adaptations seen after repeated hypoxia, potentially explaining heightened chemoreflex sensitivity observed in human conditions involving intermittent hypoxia, such as sleep apnea. The study has experimental limitations, including using potassium cyanide instead of physiological hypoxia and relying on pharmacological approaches to investigate glutamatergic and GABAergic signaling.
    • An optimized 'hypoxia in a pill' regimen reverses neurodegenerative disease phenotypes in multiple preclinical models
      Wang1,2,3, Marutani4, Zazzeron4, Menard4, Volpicelli-Daley5, Ichinose4, Mootha1,2,3
      This study investigates a novel drug combination (GBT601/PT2399) as a potential treatment for neurodegenerative diseases such as Leigh syndrome, Friedreich's ataxia, Parkinson's disease, and Huntington's disease. Preclinical results reveal that the drug combo significantly extends median lifespan in the Leigh syndrome model, improves motor functions in Parkinson's and Huntington's disease models, and reduces brain tissue hyperoxia without inducing weight loss or pulmonary hypertension. The findings suggest a promising therapeutic approach for neurodegenerative diseases, with future research focusing on clinical trials to evaluate safety and efficacy.

      The scientific paper "Artificial Hibernation Reveals Synaptic Engram Architecture Associated with Memory Retention" reveals that memories can remain stable despite widespread synapse loss during hibernation, and identifies clustered synaptic connections among memory-encoding neurons as a structural feature associated with memory retention.     • Astrocyte activation in the ventrolateral medulla modulates breathing and arousal states Luiz Marcelo Oliveira
      Oliveira, Miranda, Lim, Severs, Takakura, Moreira, Li, Kalume, Ramirez
      This scientific study investigates the role of astrocytes, brain cells that regulate physiological functions, in modulating breathing and arousal states during sigh generation in alert mice, specifically focusing on those located in the ventral respiratory column (VRC). Activating a specific subset of Aldh1l1 cells within the VRC through chemogenetic or optogenetic methods increased the probability of inducing arousals accompanied by sighs, especially during hypoxia.     • Astrocytes connect specific brain regions through plastic networks
      Cooper, Selles, Cammer, Redd, Gildea, Sall, Chiurri, Cheung, Wheeler, Saab, Liddelow, Chao | 2026
      This scientific paper explores the structural plasticity of astrocyte networks in mice brains following whisker trimming, and the role of connexin43 (Cx43) gap junctions in these networks. Key findings suggest that long-range astrocyte networks exhibit substantial deviations post-whisker trimming, and deleting Cx43 results in enhanced locomotor activity and accelerated hippocampal spreading depression. The study contributes to a better understanding of the role of astrocytes in neural signaling and various neurological disorders, emphasizing the importance of gap junctions like Cx43 for inter-astrocytic communication and their crucial roles during various neurological conditions.
    • Astrocytes: Orchestrators of brain gas exchange and oxygen homeostasis
      Christie | 2026
      Astrocytes play a critical role in brain oxygen sensing, cerebral perfusion, and cognition, with a higher sensitivity to changes in oxygen availability compared to glomus cells. They respond to hypoxia by releasing nitric oxide, regulating sympathetic drive, and cardiac output to maintain brain oxygenation. However, during ischemia, astrocytes can both protect neurons and exacerbate brain damage due to their involvement in neurotransmitter release, calcium signaling, and gap junction communication. Future research should explore therapeutic interventions targeting astrocytic functions for improving neurological disorders related to oxygen homeostasis, cognitive impairment, and dementia.
    • BDNF is necessary and sufficient for spinal respiratory plasticity following intermittent hypoxia
      Baker-Herman, Fuller, Bavis, Zabka, Golder, Doperalski, Johnson, Watters, Mitchell | 2003
      In the study, researchers found that brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) plays a crucial role in long-lasting phrenic facilitation (pLTF), a model of respiratory plasticity induced by intermittent hypoxia. The process involves serotonin-dependent BDNF synthesis near phrenic motoneurons, and BDNF administration elicits pLTF without hypoxia. Endogenous BDNF synthesis is necessary for pLTF, and the effect requires activation of a high affinity TrkB receptor. The findings could have implications for conditions such as Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS), obstructive sleep apnea, and respiratory failure following spinal cord injury.

    • Brain motion is driven by mechanical coupling with the abdomen
      Garborg, Ghitti, Zhang, Ricotta, Frank, Mueller, Greenawalt, Turner, Kedarasetti, Mostafa, Lee, Costanzo, Drew | 2026
      The study published in Nature Neuroscience investigates the motion of the dorsal cortex within the skull of awake mice, revealing that brain motion is primarily driven by locomotion rather than respiration or the cardiac cycle. Researchers found that forces generated by abdominal muscle contractions reach the brain via the vertebral venous plexus (VVP), a network of valveless veins connecting the abdomen and spinal canal, suggesting a potential hydraulic system driving cerebrospinal fluid flow. The findings suggest a mechanical link between the brain and the abdominal compartment, with implications for understanding pathologies such as intracranial hypertension, meningioma, Chiari malformations, and conditions like obesity that elevate intra-abdominal pressure.
    • Breathing: Rhythmicity, Plasticity, Chemosensitivity
      Feldman, Mitchell, Nattie | 2003
      The paper reviews recent advancements in understanding the neural mechanisms that generate rhythmic breathing patterns (rhythmicity), adaptive changes in breathing patterns (plasticity), and the role of chemoreceptors in regulating respiratory drive based on blood gas changes (chemosensitivity). Key findings include identifying the preBötzinger Complex as a key site for generating the respiratory rhythm, discussing serotonin-dependent long-term facilitation during intermittent hypoxia, and highlighting neurons with chemosensitivity spread throughout the brainstem. These studies could lead to more effective therapeutic strategies for various respiratory control disorders like Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS), sleep-disordered breathing or sleep apnea, Congenital Central Hypoventilation Syndrome, and respiratory insufficiency in spinally injured patients.

    • Decoding neuronal criticality firing patterns for large brain based EEG
      Beres, Ribeiro Rodrigues, Myers, Napoli
      This scientific study introduces a new method for decoding large-scale Electroencephalogram (EEG) neural recordings during cognitive tasks, focusing on demonstrating self-organization of neuronal networks indicative of neural criticality. By analyzing EEG data from 49 subjects and using hierarchical arrangements, the researchers discover power laws for multiple neuronal oscillatory groups, demonstrating scale-invariance through log–log plots, and characterizing criticality on the millisecond to seconds time scale relevant to cognitive tasks. This method could offer a significant advancement in understanding the dynamics of the neuronal network and its role in cognitive processes, potentially informing AI and machine learning developments.
      Keywords: Electroencephalography (EEG), Neural oscillations, Neural criticality, Critical brain hypothesis, Newell’s time scale, Scale invariance, Power law, Cognitive impairment, Hypoxia
