    • Exercise and hypoxia independently stimulate platelet liberation of brain-derived neurotrophic factor
      Thomas, Caldwell, Macleod, Abraham, Carr, Gasho, Brewster, Bruce, Monteleone, Duffy, Howe, Tremblay, Jenkins, Talbot, Stembridge, Ainslie, Gibbons | 2025
      This study published in 'The Journal of Physiology' investigates the role of Brain-Derived Neurotrophic Factor (BDNF) and Platelet Factor 4 (PF4) during exercise at sea level and high altitude, revealing that maximal exercise doubles free BDNF concentration, with platelets playing a role in liberating BDNF and potentially contributing to the neuroprotective effects of exercise. At high altitude, hypoxia promotes net cerebral release of BDNF, and there is a correlation between platelet loss during exercise and PF4 release into venous circulation.
    • GABAergic Hub Neurons Orchestrate Synchrony in Developing Hippocampal Networks
      Bonifazi, Goldin, Picardo, Jorquera, Cattani, Bianconi, Represa, Ben-Ari, Cossart
      The scientific paper investigates the significant role of high connectivity GABAergic interneurons, or hub neurons, in regulating network dynamics during hippocampus development, particularly their impact on Giant Depolarizing Potentials (GDPs). Key findings show that a third of targeted hub neurons significantly affect network dynamics compared to minimal impact from low connectivity neurons. Hub neurons are predominantly found in specific layers like the stratum oriens and lucidum, and activation of these neurons before synchrony contributes to network synchronization in the developing CA3 region. The study also highlights differences between hub and low connectivity interneurons in terms of electrophysiological properties and references notable authors' works to provide insights into physiological and pathological network oscillations.

      This Nature Metabolism study investigates the role of hyperglycosylation, specifically N-glycosylation, in Alzheimer's disease (AD). The research suggests that excessive N-glycosylation contributes to the conserved phenotype of brain hyperglycosylation in AD, and reducing this excess improves social recognition memory in AD mouse models.     • Intermittent Hypoxia Induces Reversible Epigenetic Age Acceleration in Old Mice

      The study reveals that brief periods of low oxygen exposure (intermittent hypoxia) can make cells appear older in old mice but this effect is reversible.     • MINI-REVIEW Opioids and Respiratory Depression: The role of carotid bodies in opioid-induced respiratory depression
      Spiller, Pedro F., Amorim, Mateus R., Polotsky, Vsevolod Y. | 2023
      This mini-review discusses Carotid Bodies as a potential target to mitigate Opioid-Induced Respiratory Depression, a significant public health concern in the U.S., causing over 130 deaths daily. The review suggests that activating opioid receptors (μ, d, or κ) in Carotid Bodies may protect against respiratory depression and reduce its effects without compromising analgesic properties of opioids.     • MINIREVIEW Intermittent Hypoxia: Cause of or Therapy for Systemic Hypertension?
      Serebrovskaya, Manukhina, Smith, Downey, Mallet | 2008
      This mini-review discusses the conflicting effects of Intermittent Hypoxia (IH) on systemic hypertension, particularly its potential as a treatment due to its ability to lower blood pressure in individuals with hypertension. The review highlights research conducted primarily in the former Soviet Union, focusing on mechanisms such as enhanced sarcoplasmic reticular Ca2+ sequestration and increased antioxidant defenses that contribute to IH's effects on preventing endothelial dysfunction and high blood pressure in rats with various forms of experimental hypertension. The article also distinguishes Intermittent Hypoxia Treatment (IHT) from Obstructive Sleep Apnea, emphasizing IHT's potential benefits over traditional drug therapies due to its minimal side effects and possible stimulation of an organism's general resistance. The paper also provides references related to nitric oxide, intermittent hypoxia, and cardiovascular physiology.

    • Noradrenergic neuromodulation produces a NMDAR-dependent network state of respiratory rhythmogenesis in the preBötzinger Complex

      This preprint study investigates the effects of Norepinephrine (NE) and Nitric Oxide (NO) on the preBotanic Complex (preBötC), a critical brainstem region for respiratory rhythm generation, in rodents. The research finds that NE increases action potential frequency while decreasing the average action potential number during network bursts, and NO attenuates synchrony by activating soluble guanylyl cyclase and cGMP-dependent protein kinase (PKG).     • Novel oxygen sensing mechanism in the spinal cord involved in cardiorespiratory responses to hypoxia
      Barioni, Derakhshan, Tenorio Lopes, Onimaru, Roy, McDonald, Scheibli, Baghdadwala, Heidari, Bharadia, Ikeda, Yazawa, Okada, Harris, Dutschmann, Wilson | 2022
      The study by Barioni et al., published in Science Advances, identifies a novel oxygen sensing mechanism in the spinal cord's sympathetic preganglionic neurons (SSPNs), which contributes to cardiorespiratory responses during hypoxia. This mechanism involves nitric oxide synthase 1 (NOS1) and nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADPH) oxidase (NOX2). The research has potential clinical implications for various respiratory and sympathetic conditions, such as obesity, atherosclerosis, diabetes, asthma, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, sleep apnea, acute mountain sickness, subdued carotid body activity, intermittent hypoxia-induced cardiorespiratory plasticity, and severe asphyxia-like conditions.
    • Physiological Differences Underlying Divergent Hypoxia
      Burtscher, Mallet, Sah, Gassmann, Burtscher, Iturriaga | 2025
      This review paper, published in 2025 by Johannes Burtscher et al., compares the physiological differences underlying altitude adaptations and responses to hypoxia among humans, rats, and mice. The authors find that humans and rodents have distinct reactions: humans boost ventilation, oxygen transport, and anaerobic metabolism during hypoxia, while rodents initially lower their metabolic rate. Rodents demonstrate higher-altitude tolerance due to factors like larger lung volume, efficient gas exchange, and hypoxic hypometabolism. However, rats are more susceptible to hypoxia than mice or humans, with myocardial injury, excessive erythropoiesis, and significant pulmonary vascular remodeling in response. The authors propose combining rodent studies with large animal models, focusing on comparative physiology and molecular biology, developing valid in vitro systems for systemically relevant hypoxia responses, using biomarkers for back-translation, and considering dosing windows of intermittent/continuous hypoxia protocols. They emphasize that no single animal model is ideal for pre-clinical hypoxia research due to the species' distinct adaptations.
      Keywords: chronic continuous hypoxia, erythropoietin, high altitude adaptation, human physiology, hypoxia inducible factor, intermittent hypoxia conditioning, mouse, rat, red blood cells, species differences
    • Regulation of sleep homeostasis mediator adenosine by basal forebrain glutamatergic neurons
      Peng*, Wu*, Song*, Zhang, Li, Xu†
      The research paper "GRABAdo1.0: A Novel Sensor for Extracellular Adenosine Detection and Its Role in Sleep Homeostasis" presents a study on glutamatergic neurons in the basal forebrain (BF), demonstrating their role as key regulators of sleep homeostasis through adenosine release. The study found higher adenosine levels during wakefulness and REM sleep compared to NREM sleep, and revealed a correlation between calcium activity in BF cholinergic neurons and adenosine levels. Optogenetic activation increased extracellular adenosine levels, while lesioning these glutamatergic neurons resulted in increased wakefulness during the active period, less recovery sleep after prolonged wakefulness, and impaired sleep rebound. The 'ion (38)' experiment showed statistically significant differences in NREM sleep time between the lesion group and control group. The discussion section reviews existing studies on sleep regulation and neural activity in the basal forebrain, confirming adenosine's role in mediating homeostatic control of sleep in cholinergic BF neurons, and contributing new insights into how neural activity during wakefulness contributes to sleep pressure through the release of sleep-inducing factors by BF glutamatergic neurons.
    • Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance
      Kipnis, Benveniste, Eichmann, Thomas, Reich, Lewis, Tsai, Drieu, Bakker, Kelley, Lundgaard, Mestre, Zott, Eide, van Osch, van Veluw, Iliff, Yang, Santambrogio, Da Mesquita, Daneman, Rustenhoven, Goldman, Nedergaard
      The scientific paper "Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance" aims to advance our understanding of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) dynamics in brain waste clearance, focusing on periarterial spaces, glymphatic and meningeal lymphatic systems, their functional connections, and immune cell involvement. Key findings reveal primary CSF influx along periarterial spaces, solute clearance through multiple routes, and functional influence by vascular pulsation, neural activity, sleep, and immunity. The study highlights the role of immune cells at brain-border niches in monitoring solute efflux, linking brain clearance to neuroimmune interactions. The research aims to uncover new therapeutic avenues for various disorders such as autoimmune, neurodegenerative, neoplastic, and psychiatric diseases.
    • Review: Cell-type-specific APOE4 cascade across the Alzheimer’s disease continuum
      Shostak, Liang, Huang | 2026
      The review "Cell-type-specific APOE4 cascade across the Alzheimer’s disease continuum" highlights Apolipoprotein E4 (APOE4) as the leading genetic risk factor for Alzheimer's disease (AD), causing early neuronal deficits in preclinical AD stages through age-associated stress. This neuronal damage triggers a harmful glial response, amplifying neurodegeneration and accelerating the onset of prodromal and dementia AD stages. The proposed model suggests potential therapeutic targets like HMGB1 and MHC class I for treating neuroinflammation but emphasizes the need for further investigation into their broader physiological impact and safety, with the ultimate goal of preventing APOE4's harmful effects on the aging brain before cognitive decline begins.
    • Role of circadian gene expression in the divergent effects of intermittent hypoxia and sleep fragmentation on blood pressure
      Chen, Yu, Xi | 2026
      The review article published in Clinical Science (2026) examines the impact of Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) on blood pressure regulation through disruption of circadian gene expression, focusing on the role of hypoxia-inducible factor 1α (HIF-1α). The research suggests that while conventional treatments like CPAP are effective for OSA, novel therapies targeting circadian clock genes could improve cardiovascular health, particularly in populations with disrupted sleep patterns or heightened salt sensitivity. Controlled moderate intermittent hypoxia is proposed to have potential benefits on the heart and vasculatures through modulation of specific gene expressions, but further research is needed for effective implementation of these novel therapies.
    • Sleep Disorders: Cholinergic premotor modulation of hypoglossal motor activity identified from combined ‘opto-dialysis’ in vivo
      Amin Ladha, Raina, da Silveira Scarpellini, Carolina, Liu, Wen-Ying, Hattie Liu, Liu, Kevin Patrick Grace, Kevin, Montandon, Gaspard, Horner, Richard L. | 2026
      The research paper, led by R. Amin Ladha and R. L. Horner, explores the role of cholinergic neurons in controlling tongue movements during sleep using a combined optogenetics (optical manipulation of neurons) and dialysis (substance removal from living organisms) approach.     • Tanycytic degeneration impairs tau clearance and contributes to Alzheimer’s disease pathology
      Sauve´, Martinez-Go´ mez, Mbouamboua, Ternier, Nampoothiri, Dupre´, Garcia, Couralet, Dewisme, Lebouvier, Danis, Rasika, Dhenain, Kim, Ciofi, Bue´ e, Landrieu, Pasquier, Lilamand, Paquet, Giacobini, Barbry, Maurage, Nogueiras, Schwaninger, Prevot | 2026
      The research paper published by Prevot et al. investigates the critical role tanycytes play in clearing tau proteins from the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) to the blood, which may be impaired in Alzheimer's disease (AD). Findings suggest that these cells exhibit fragmentation and clearance deficits in AD patients, potentially contributing to increased tau accumulation in the brain. The implications of this research include a better understanding of tanycytic dysfunction in human pathology and evidence for a brain-to-blood tanycytic shuttle as potential therapeutic targets for treating Alzheimer's disease.
    • The Rostral Ventrolateral Medulla Input to Central Amygdala Regulates Anxiety-­Like Behaviors in Mice for Chronic Light Exposure
      Jing, Tan, Xu, Yuan, Fan, Wang | 2026
      This Original Article, published in 2026 in CNS Neuroscience & Therapeutics, investigates the impact of chronic light exposure on anxiety-like behaviors in mice and the role of the rostral ventrolateral medulla (RVLM) input to the central amygdala (CeA). The study reveals that chronic light exposure enhances anxiety-like behaviors, which are regulated by the RVLM input to the CeA, suggesting a potential novel approach for treating anxiety disorders under conditions of prolonged light exposure. However, the research was limited by not addressing physical or pathological disconnection effects under light exposure conditions, being conducted exclusively in male mice, and not publicly releasing the data due to privacy or ethical restrictions. Future studies are necessary to validate the RVLM→CeA bidirectional feedback loop hypothesis and determine whether findings generalize to female subjects.
      Keywords: anxiety-­like behaviors, blood pressure, central amygdala, chronic light exposure, rostral ventrolateral medulla
    • The hypoxic ventilatory response and arousal burden predict the magnitude of ventilatory long-term facilitation in humans with obstructive sleep apnoea
      Mateika, Hammo, Kissane, Azarbarzin | 2025
      In a 2025 study, researchers led by Jason H. Mateika found that hypoxic ventilatory response (HVR) and arousal burden are significant predictors of progressive augmentation (PA) and ventilatory long-term facilitation (VLTF) in individuals with obstructive sleep apnea (OSA). Arousal burden might be a more accurate predictor of the magnitude of PA or VLTF than hypoxic burden. The study was funded by several institutions, including the United States Department of Veterans Affairs and the American Academy of Sleep Medicine Foundation.
      Keywords: arousal, intermittent hypoxia, obstructive sleep apnoea, respiratory plasticity
    • The influence of intermittent hypercapnia on cerebrospinal fluid flow and clearance in Parkinson’s disease and healthy older adults
      Erhardt1, Mayer2, Lin3,4, Richardson3,5, Shaff2, Vakhtin2, Caprihan2, van der Horn2,6, Hoffman2, Phillips2, Grigg-Damberger5, Wang2, Gough2, Hobson7, Deligtisch5, Suarez Cedeno5, Sugar5, Ryman2,5 | 2025
      This study investigates the influence of intermittent hypercapnia on cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) flow and clearance in Parkinson's disease (PD) patients and healthy older adults, evaluating if it can stimulate glymphatic clearance, a process that removes waste products from the brain. The study found that intermittent hypercapnia elicited large-amplitude cerebrovascular activity followed by a large-amplitude CSF inflow signal in both groups, but PD patients exhibited significantly reduced CSF inflow response to intermittent hypercapnia compared to healthy controls. The study also revealed that intermittent hypercapnia increased the appearance of several brain byproducts in the plasma of both groups, suggesting potential clearance from the brain, and proposes future work to compare protein and peptide clearance in a larger cohort of PD patients relative to healthy controls, determine optimal CO2 concentrations or targeted changes in ETCO2, and evaluate individual differences in physiological responses.
    • The neuroscience of cancer
      Mancusi1, Monje1,2 | 2023
      The scientific paper investigates complex interactions between the nervous system, cancer cells, and immune cells in various types of cancers, such as glioma, gastrointestinal, oral squamous cell carcinomas, and pancreatic cancer. It reveals that neuronal activity promotes cancer progression, co-opted by cancer cells to enhance metastasis potential. Key findings suggest new treatment strategies like preoperative β-blockade, serotonin attenuation, and vagus nerve stimulation for cancer management.

    • Adenosine and ATP Link PCO2 to Cortical Excitability via pH
      Dulla, Dobelis, Pearson, Frenguelli, Staley, Masino | 2005
      This 2005 scientific paper published in Neuron investigates the impact of carbon dioxide (CO2) levels on neural function, particularly in the hippocampus, and finds that increased CO2 levels decrease neuronal excitability through adenosine A1 receptor activation, while decreased CO2 levels increase excitability. The study suggests that CO2 modulates neuronal excitability and epileptiform activity through its effects on adenosine and purines, which could have implications for the development of new therapies for epilepsy and other neurological disorders.
    • Aquaporin-4 mis-localization slows glymphatic clearance of α-synuclein and promotes α-synuclein pathology and aggregate propagation
      Braun, Simon, Jang, Sanderson, Swierz, Sevao, Pincus, Schaser, Elliott, Lim, Unni, Schindler, Keene, Latimer, Iliff | 2024
      This research paper suggests that mislocalization of AQP4, a water channel protein in the perivascular region, impairs glymphatic function, which is responsible for clearing alpha-synuclein from brain tissue. This impairment promotes the aggregation of alpha-synuclein and potentially contributes to the development and propagation of synucleinopathies like Lewy Body Dementia and Parkinson's disease. The findings are based on post-mortem human brain tissue analysis and experiments using transgenic mice, and suggest that glymphatic impairment may contribute to the development and progression of synucleinopathies.
      Keywords: Glymphatic, aquaporin-4, AQP4, Astrocytes, Perivascular endfeet, α-syntrophin, α-synuclein, propagation, PFFs, Lewy body dementia
    • Astrocytes connect specific brain regions through plastic networks
      Cooper, Selles, Cammer, Redd, Gildea, Sall, Chiurri, Cheung, Wheeler, Saab, Liddelow, Chao | 2026
      This scientific paper explores the structural plasticity of astrocyte networks in mice brains following whisker trimming, and the role of connexin43 (Cx43) gap junctions in these networks. Key findings suggest that long-range astrocyte networks exhibit substantial deviations post-whisker trimming, and deleting Cx43 results in enhanced locomotor activity and accelerated hippocampal spreading depression. The study contributes to a better understanding of the role of astrocytes in neural signaling and various neurological disorders, emphasizing the importance of gap junctions like Cx43 for inter-astrocytic communication and their crucial roles during various neurological conditions.

    • Body-Brain Integration: The Lower Brainstem in Sleep-Wake Regulation
      Yao, Dan | 2026
      This review paper published in Annual Review of Neuroscience (2026) emphasizes the role of the lower brainstem, specifically key regions such as the Nucleus of the Solitary Tract, Parabrachial Nucleus, Medulla, Locus Coeruleus, and others, in regulating sleep-wake cycles, arousal, and motor output.     • Brain motion is driven by mechanical coupling with the abdomen
      Garborg, Ghitti, Zhang, Ricotta, Frank, Mueller, Greenawalt, Turner, Kedarasetti, Mostafa, Lee, Costanzo, Drew | 2026
      The study published in Nature Neuroscience investigates the motion of the dorsal cortex within the skull of awake mice, revealing that brain motion is primarily driven by locomotion rather than respiration or the cardiac cycle. Researchers found that forces generated by abdominal muscle contractions reach the brain via the vertebral venous plexus (VVP), a network of valveless veins connecting the abdomen and spinal canal, suggesting a potential hydraulic system driving cerebrospinal fluid flow. The findings suggest a mechanical link between the brain and the abdominal compartment, with implications for understanding pathologies such as intracranial hypertension, meningioma, Chiari malformations, and conditions like obesity that elevate intra-abdominal pressure.
    • C5aR1+ microglia exacerbate neuroinflammation and cerebral edema in acute brain injury
      Zhou, Ma, Feng, Tian, Li, Guo, Shi, Cui, Dong, Hao, He, Xiao, Chen, Wu, Gao, Hu, Zhang, Wang, Zhou, Feng, Xue, Lin, Wang, Gao, Heng, Zhao, Luo, Tian, Zhang, Guo, Li, Liu, Ge, Liu, Qu | 2025
      The study by Zhou et al. discovers that a pro-inflammatory microglial subtype (C5aR1+) amplifies neuroinflammation, promotes harmful astrocyte polarization, and enhances neutrophil recruitment after traumatic brain injury (TBI) or intracerebral hemorrhage (ICH), exacerbating cerebral edema and neurological deficits. Pharmacological inhibition of C5aR1 reduces edema in preclinical models, suggesting its potential as a therapeutic target for TBI and ICH. The FDA-approved drug avacopan, a C5aR1 antagonist, shows promise in mitigating these harmful effects by suppressing pro-inflammatory cytokine expression, restoring microglial morphology, inhibiting AKT and ERK phosphorylation, reducing astrocyte polarization, decreasing infiltrated neutrophils, alleviating cerebral edema, and accelerating functional recovery after TBI and ICH. Further research is needed to confirm these findings and explore the full therapeutic potential of avacopan in these conditions.
    • Comparative Physiology: Adaptation across an extreme elevational gradient in Andean leaf-­eared mice, the world’s highest-­dwelling mammal
      Liphardt, Bautista | 2026
      The summarized texts discuss multiple scientific studies focusing on identifying genetic adaptations in rodents (Phyllotis vaccarum) and insects (Phyllotreta vaccarum) living in extreme high-altitude environments, providing insights into their unique traits like enhanced thermogenesis, mitochondrial capacity, and tolerance for toxic plants. The research uses various bioinformatics tools and genetic analysis methods to uncover these adaptations, contributing significantly to the broader field of evolutionary biology.
    • Evidence for acid-sensing ion channel 3 (ASIC3) involvement in cough resulting from aspiration of gastric fluid
      Chou, Mori, Pavelkova, Liu, Liptak, Meeker, Halicka, Undem, Kollarik, Canning | 2026
      This study, published in J Physiol 604.3 (2026), investigates the role of ASICs in coughing triggered by aspirated gastric fluid using an animal model. Key findings suggest that ASIC3-dependent coughing is initiated by gastric fluid aspiration and can be inhibited by the ASIC3 blocker diclofenac. The research implies potential implications for identifying patients at risk for aspiration pneumonia and developing therapeutic strategies for cough hypersensitivity.
    • Exercise and hypoxia independently stimulate platelet liberation of brain-derived neurotrophic factor
      Thomas, Caldwell, Macleod, Abraham, Carr, Gasho, Brewster, Bruce, Monteleone, Duffy, Howe, Tremblay, Jenkins, Talbot, Stembridge, Ainslie, Gibbons | 2025
      This study published in 'The Journal of Physiology' investigates the role of Brain-Derived Neurotrophic Factor (BDNF) and Platelet Factor 4 (PF4) during exercise at sea level and high altitude, revealing that maximal exercise doubles free BDNF concentration, with platelets playing a role in liberating BDNF and potentially contributing to the neuroprotective effects of exercise. At high altitude, hypoxia promotes net cerebral release of BDNF, and there is a correlation between platelet loss during exercise and PF4 release into venous circulation.
    • How Your Brain’s Nightly Cleanse Keeps It Healthy
      Denworth, Mukerjee, Martincic | 2025
      The scientific paper investigates the glymphatic system, a nocturnal waste removal mechanism in the brain essential for clearing toxic proteins linked to Alzheimer's disease, focusing on the role of norepinephrine and anesthetics' impact on its functionality. Key findings reveal that certain anesthetics can either suppress or enhance glymphatic function, with impairments linked to various conditions such as chronic pain, psychiatric disorders, and neurodegenerative diseases. The research suggests potential for non-invasive diagnostic tools like a "glymphogram" and treatments for slowing or preventing neurodegenerative diseases.
    • Hyperglycosylation is a metabolic driver of Alzheimer’s disease
      Liu, Ribas, Medina, Nielsen, Clarke, Ma, Mueller, Plasencia, Sheer, Simpson, Soto, Sudderth, Cai, Cantrell, Colpaert, Shedlock, Wu, Young, Kooser, Chen, Huang, He, Alonge, Guo, Bian, Vander Kooi, Gentry, Sun, Quinones, Son, Azadi, Deberardinis, Prokop, Allison, Yang, Chen, Huang, He, Guo, Gao, Kooi, Gentry, & Sun | 2026
      This Nature Metabolism study investigates the role of hyperglycosylation, specifically N-glycosylation, in Alzheimer's disease (AD). The research suggests that excessive N-glycosylation contributes to the conserved phenotype of brain hyperglycosylation in AD, and reducing this excess improves social recognition memory in AD mouse models. Genetic knockdown of glycan biosynthetic enzymes showed improved cognitive outcomes, while glucosamine supplementation worsened behavior in these mice. A retrospective analysis of electronic health records revealed that glucosamine supplementation was associated with accelerated AD progression and reduced survival in patients with mild cognitive impairment (MCI) and dementia, highlighting the need for further investigation into cell-type-specific spatial information and pharmacological interventions targeting the hexosamine pathway in animal models.
    • Inverted U-shaped relationship between sleep duration and phenotypic age in US adults: a population-based study
      You, Chen, Liu, Zhang, Wang, Yang, Liu, Ma | 2024
      This study investigates the relationship between sleep duration, exercise, and biological aging in US adults using data from the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey (NHANES) between 2005–2020. The research finds a declining proportion of adults with insufficient or deficient sleep since 2015-2016. Both extreme short (<7 hours) and long (>9 hours) sleep durations are associated with increased phenotypic age, suggesting an inverted U-shaped relationship. Regular exercise benefits those with extended sleep durations but may exacerbate aging effects for individuals with shorter sleep durations. The study uses self-reported sleep data, and future research should employ objective measures of sleep to establish causality more accurately.
      Keywords: Sleep, Phenotypic age, Exercise, US population, Cross-sectional study
    • MINI-REVIEW Opioids and Respiratory Depression: The role of carotid bodies in opioid-induced respiratory depression
      Spiller, Pedro F., Amorim, Mateus R., Polotsky, Vsevolod Y. | 2023
      This mini-review discusses Carotid Bodies as a potential target to mitigate Opioid-Induced Respiratory Depression, a significant public health concern in the U.S., causing over 130 deaths daily. The review suggests that activating opioid receptors (μ, d, or κ) in Carotid Bodies may protect against respiratory depression and reduce its effects without compromising analgesic properties of opioids. However, further research is required to confirm the presence of κ opioid receptors in human Carotid Bodies and to evaluate the effectiveness of κ agonists in blocking OIRD with higher doses of fentanyl. The authors propose that activating Carotid Bodies may offer a therapeutic intervention for Opioid-Induced Respiratory Depression, minimizing risks associated with respiratory depression while preserving opioids' analgesic benefits.
      Keywords: carotid body, opioid receptor, respiratory depression
    • Mu opioid receptors gate the locus coeruleus pain generator
      Kuo, Norris, Dunn, Becker, Kim, Vazquez, Borges, Thang, O’Brien, Parker, McCall | 2026
      The study by Kuo et al. identifies the locus coeruleus as a key player in chronic pain regulation, transforming from an endogenous analgesia source to a pain generator following nerve injury. Mu opioid receptors (MOR) on distinct projections within the LC were found to robustly inhibit it and drive antinociception. The research suggests that restoring LC opioid signaling could potentially reverse neuropathic hypersensitivity, offering potential therapeutic targets for pain management, although with limitations such as global NE-conditional MOR knockout and focus on specific time points post-neuropathic injury.

    • Respiratory control dysfunction and vulnerability in Parkinson’s disease
      Macedo, Matos, Damasceno-Silva, Oliveira, Franco, Ferreira, Cabral, Aquino, Moreira, Takakura | 2021
      The study "Respiratory Control in Parkinson's Disease" by Tainá O. Macedo et al., funded by research grants, investigates how Parkinson's disease affects central neural circuits like the RTN and preBötC, causing oxidative stress, neuroinflammation, mitochondrial dysfunction, and neuronal vulnerability. Peripherally, PD impacts sleep architecture, altering rapid eye movement (REM) and non-REM sleep stages through glutamatergic neurons in the PPN. Respiratory dysfunction, a common but underdiagnosed feature of PD, is observed in decreased inspiratory drive, increased expiratory flow limitation, and changes in response to hypercapnia and hypoxia. Pharmacological interventions have been explored for restoring respiratory function, while the authors recommend standardizing methods for assessing respiratory function in clinical settings and designing clinical trials targeting respiratory outcomes. The study provides a list of references related to sleep, arousal systems, cardiorespiratory regulation, Parkinson's disease, and central chemoreceptors.

    • The cellular inflammatory response in human spinal cords after injury
      Fleming, Norenberg, Ramsay, Dekaban, Marcillo, Saenz, Pasquale-Styles, Dietrich, Weaver | 2006
      This study by Fleming et al. (2006) investigates the cellular inflammatory response in human spinal cords after injury, focusing on neutrophils, monocytes/macrophages, lymphocytes, oxidative enzymes, and proteolytic enzymes. The research found that neutrophils dominate immediately after injury but decrease over time, while macrophages increase. Activated microglia express gp91phox and contribute to both neurological recovery and tissue damage. Lymphocytes, particularly CD8+ T-cells, are present among macrophages, with their role in secondary degenerative processes and wound healing unclear. The study highlights the need for limiting neutrophil entry as a potential neuroprotective therapy.
      Keywords: inflammation, macrophage, neutrophil, oxidative activity, SCI

    • Vip+ vagal neurons control allergen-induced responses
      Zhu, Su, Sun | 2026
      The study "Vip+ vagal neurons control allergen-induced responses" found that Vip+ vagal sensory neurons play a crucial role in asthma pathogenesis by mediating airway hyperreactivity and type 2 inflammation through the neurotrophin-NGFR signaling axis. These neurons, which are distinct from Tac1+ neurons, project extensively to the nTS region of the brainstem, revealing a neuroimmune mechanism along the body-brain axis contributing to asthma. The study highlights the functional importance of NGFR signaling in Vip+-neuron-mediated allergen responses, but further studies are needed to determine the role of VIP neuropeptide and its primary functional source in driving allergen responses.

    kinesiology

    • 4-Aminopyridine-sensitive outward currents in preBötzinger complex neurons influence respiratory rhythm generation in neonatal mice
      Hayes, Mendenhall, Brush, Del Negro | 2008
      The 2008 study by Hayes et al. investigates the role of a transient outward potassium current, IA, in shaping inspiratory bursts during respiratory activity within the preBötzinger complex (preBötC) in neonatal mice. The researchers found that more than half of inspiratory neurons express IA and that its maximum conductance positively correlates with membrane capacitance and negatively with resistance, with a time constant of inactivation between 20-80ms. 4-aminopyridine (4-AP), an IA blocker, was used to study its role in preBc neurons and caused disorganized XII output and noisy preBötC rhythms that fluctuate in amplitude and period, suggesting that IA plays a crucial role in the regularity of the cycle period and drive potential magnitude in preBötC neurons. The study proposes that further research is needed to clarify the specific contributions of different Kv subunits to preBötC IA and its functional significance in respiration.
    • A neuroprosthesis for restoring hand movement and sensation in a person with complete tetraplegia
      Bouton | 2026
      The scientific paper presents a hybrid Dual Neuromodulation (DNB) system that combines an intracortical brain-computer interface, transcutaneous spinal cord stimulation, and peripheral stimuli for restoring motor and sensory functions in individuals with spinal cord injuries. The study on one participant showed significant improvements in motor control and sensory recovery, with potential for home use. Two related studies were discussed, both focusing on neural modulation in response to tactile stimuli and emphasizing the importance of understanding brain activity associated with different body parts and various types of stimuli. Challenges remain in optimizing stimulation parameters, adapting to interindividual variability, and streamlining identification of effective stimulation patterns for the DNB system.
    • A new sense for electrical fields
      Riedl, Sixt | 2026
      The Cell-published study by Belliveau et al. confirms TMEM154/Galvanin as a cellular antenna that senses electrical gradients, facilitating cell migration towards the cathode. They found that Galvanin is essential for electrotaxis in human granulocytes and can transform non-electrotactic cell types into electrotactic ones, suggesting an electrophoretic process where Galvanin redistributes towards the anodal pole of the cell in electrical fields.
    • A projection atlas of excitatory and inhibitory inputs to the preB€otzinger complex: substrates for multimodal breathing control
      Reilly, Kadri, Arthurs, Sedano, Brezinski, Baertsch | 2021
      The research "A Projection Atlas of Excitatory and Inhibitory Inputs to the preBötzinger Complex" reveals that the preBötzinger complex, a critical region for rhythmic breathing in the brain, is modulated by diverse long-range excitatory and inhibitory inputs from various brain regions. The study identifies multiple sources of excitatory inputs converging on the preBötzinger complex, such as the caudal and rostral medullary regions, the rostral ventral respiratory group (rVRG), the lateral parafacial region/retrotrapezoid nucleus (PFl/RTN), and the spinal trigeminal nucleus caudalis (Sp5c). This study provides a foundation for understanding the neural mechanisms underlying breathing regulation and could potentially lead to new treatments for respiratory disorders and sleep apnea.

    • Brain motion is driven by mechanical coupling with the abdomen
      Garborg, Ghitti, Zhang, Ricotta, Frank, Mueller, Greenawalt, Turner, Kedarasetti, Mostafa, Lee, Costanzo, Drew | 2026
      The study published in Nature Neuroscience investigates the motion of the dorsal cortex within the skull of awake mice, revealing that brain motion is primarily driven by locomotion rather than respiration or the cardiac cycle. Researchers found that forces generated by abdominal muscle contractions reach the brain via the vertebral venous plexus (VVP), a network of valveless veins connecting the abdomen and spinal canal, suggesting a potential hydraulic system driving cerebrospinal fluid flow.     • Challenging conventional wisdom: A fresh look at the litter effect in rats
      Huxtable, Flanigan, Frazure, Wollman, Sun, Pandey, Restifo, Fregosi | 2026
      The scientific paper led by Dr. Adrianne G. Huxtable challenges the traditional belief that physiological variance among neonatal Sprague–Dawley rat litters is greater between litters than within litters. Using intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC) analysis, the study found that a significant portion of total variation in body weight and breathing frequency originated within litters rather than between them. The findings suggest a need for more accurate experimental designs, taking into account specific research questions, outcome variables, rat genetics, raw variance both within and between litters, and minimizing animal use in research. The authors propose using multiple pups per litter and applying mixed-model ANOVA with litter as a random effect for more reliable results. However, limitations include the study's applicability to other lab rodents and small sample sizes in some cohorts.

    • GABAA receptor mediated facilitation of proprioceptive afferent transmission to motoneurons is facilitated by TRPM8 channels on C-fibres that are gated by serotonin
      Black, Li, Hari, Lin, Stephens, Fouad, Fenrich, Lucas-Osma, Gorassini, Bennett | 2026
      The study conducted at the University of Alberta investigates how serotonin modulates sensory transmission within the monosynaptic stretch reflex from proprioceptive Ia afferents to motoneurons, using zolmitriptan as an example. It finds that serotonin, via its main receptor subtype 5-HT1D on nociceptive C-fibers, induces a long-lasting tonic depolarization in Ia afferents through increased GABAergic input onto them, promoting sodium spike initiation. Activation of 5-HT1D receptors with zolmitriptan inhibits C-fiber activity and reduces the GABAA receptor-mediated tonic depolarization on Ia afferents, decreasing spike propagation to motoneurons. The research suggests that brainstem-derived serotonin not only inhibits ongoing nociceptive C-fibers activity but also reduces GABAergic neuron activity and sensory transmission in large proprioceptive afferents, leading to decreased motoneuron activity. This could have implications for pain studies and challenges traditional concepts of sensory gating.
    • GABAergic Hub Neurons Orchestrate Synchrony in Developing Hippocampal Networks
      Bonifazi, Goldin, Picardo, Jorquera, Cattani, Bianconi, Represa, Ben-Ari, Cossart
      The scientific paper investigates the significant role of high connectivity GABAergic interneurons, or hub neurons, in regulating network dynamics during hippocampus development, particularly their impact on Giant Depolarizing Potentials (GDPs). Key findings show that a third of targeted hub neurons significantly affect network dynamics compared to minimal impact from low connectivity neurons. Hub neurons are predominantly found in specific layers like the stratum oriens and lucidum, and activation of these neurons before synchrony contributes to network synchronization in the developing CA3 region. The study also highlights differences between hub and low connectivity interneurons in terms of electrophysiological properties and references notable authors' works to provide insights into physiological and pathological network oscillations.
    • Galvanin (TMEM154) is an electric-field sensor for directed cell migration
      Belliveau, Footer, Platenkamp, Rodriguez, Kim, Prinz, van Loon, Lin, Eustis, Chan, Cohen, Theriot | 2026
      The research published in Cell (2026) by Belliveau et al. identifies Galvanin as a crucial electric-field sensor essential for directed migration in various rapidly moving cells such as immune and epithelial cells. Upon exposure to electric fields, Galvanin localizes at the front and rear of cells, changing spatial patterns, exhibiting an exponential decay with a net charge of -18e, and regulating subsequent movement responses. The study suggests that a highly negatively charged construct can rescue directed migration in knockout cell lines, indicating a high net negative ectodomain charge is sufficient for Galvanin-mediated electrotaxis. Further studies are needed to understand the molecular mechanisms underlying Galvanin's role in electrotaxis and its implications for cell signaling and other biological processes.

    • Regulation of sleep homeostasis mediator adenosine by basal forebrain glutamatergic neurons
      Peng*, Wu*, Song*, Zhang, Li, Xu†
      The research paper "GRABAdo1.0: A Novel Sensor for Extracellular Adenosine Detection and Its Role in Sleep Homeostasis" presents a study on glutamatergic neurons in the basal forebrain (BF), demonstrating their role as key regulators of sleep homeostasis through adenosine release. The study found higher adenosine levels during wakefulness and REM sleep compared to NREM sleep, and revealed a correlation between calcium activity in BF cholinergic neurons and adenosine levels. Optogenetic activation increased extracellular adenosine levels, while lesioning these glutamatergic neurons resulted in increased wakefulness during the active period, less recovery sleep after prolonged wakefulness, and impaired sleep rebound. The 'ion (38)' experiment showed statistically significant differences in NREM sleep time between the lesion group and control group. The discussion section reviews existing studies on sleep regulation and neural activity in the basal forebrain, confirming adenosine's role in mediating homeostatic control of sleep in cholinergic BF neurons, and contributing new insights into how neural activity during wakefulness contributes to sleep pressure through the release of sleep-inducing factors by BF glutamatergic neurons.
    • Resistance training-induced adaptations in the neuromuscular system: Physiological mechanisms and implications for human performance
      Lecce, Amoruso, Felici, Bazzucchi | 2026
      This topical review published in The Journal of Physiology by Edoardo Lecce's team from the University of Rome 'Foro Italico' explores neuromuscular adaptations resulting from resistance training (RT). They find increased excitability, improved synaptic input transmission, and decreased synaptic noise in the brain and spinal cord, leading to a more stable neural drive. At the spinal level, intrinsic motoneuron excitability, neuromodulatory input, and spinal circuitry are modified, resulting in enhanced motor unit (MU) excitability due to attenuated inhibitory pathways. Skeletal muscle adaptations involve contractile protein synthesis, structural repair by satellite cells, and endurance capacity improvement through mitochondrial biogenesis and capillarization. These changes lead to increased strength, improved physical performance, and alterations in muscle fiber type composition, energy metabolism, glucose uptake, and hormone levels, with practical applications for healthy individuals and those affected by chronic disorders.

    • Safety and feasibility of paired vagus nerve stimulation with rehabilitation for improving upper extremity function in people with cervical spinal cord injury: a pilot randomized controlled trial
      Korupolu, Yozbatiran, Miller, Shields, Tandon | 2026
      This scientific paper titled "Safety and feasibility of paired vagus nerve stimulation with rehabilitation for improving upper extremity function in people with cervical spinal cord injury: a pilot randomized controlled trial" aimed to evaluate the safety, feasibility, and therapeutic potential of pairing invasive Vagus Nerve Stimulation (VNS) with upper extremity rehabilitation in individuals with chronic cervical Spinal Cord Injury (SCI). The study demonstrated that the intervention was safe, well-tolerated, and feasible in SCI patients. At immediate post-intervention assessment, all participants receiving active VNS showed improvement in upper extremity motor function compared with baseline, whereas none of the participants in the control group showed improvement. However, these findings should be interpreted cautiously due to the small sample size and exploratory nature of the analysis. The study also found that transportation and access barriers affected recruitment and limited completion of in-clinic sessions, highlighting the need for more accessible and flexible rehabilitation delivery models. Further investigation is required with larger trials to determine the efficacy, durability, and optimal implementation of VNS paired with rehabilitation in spinal cord injury.

      This Original Article, published in 2026 in CNS Neuroscience & Therapeutics, investigates the impact of chronic light exposure on anxiety-like behaviors in mice and the role of the rostral ventrolateral medulla (RVLM) input to the central amygdala (CeA). The study reveals that chronic light exposure enhances anxiety-like behaviors, which are regulated by the RVLM input to the CeA, suggesting a potential novel approach for treating anxiety disorders under conditions of prolonged light exposure. However, the research was limited by not addressing physical or pathological disconnection effects under light exposure conditions, being conducted exclusively in male mice, and not publicly releasing the data due to privacy or ethical restrictions. Future studies are necessary to validate the RVLM→CeA bidirectional feedback loop hypothesis and determine whether findings generalize to female subjects.
      Keywords: anxiety-­like behaviors, blood pressure, central amygdala, chronic light exposure, rostral ventrolateral medulla
    • The cellular inflammatory response in human spinal cords after injury
      Fleming, Norenberg, Ramsay, Dekaban, Marcillo, Saenz, Pasquale-Styles, Dietrich, Weaver | 2006
      This study by Fleming et al. (2006) investigates the cellular inflammatory response in human spinal cords after injury, focusing on neutrophils, monocytes/macrophages, lymphocytes, oxidative enzymes, and proteolytic enzymes. The research found that neutrophils dominate immediately after injury but decrease over time, while macrophages increase. Activated microglia express gp91phox and contribute to both neurological recovery and tissue damage. Lymphocytes, particularly CD8+ T-cells, are present among macrophages, with their role in secondary degenerative processes and wound healing unclear. The study highlights the need for limiting neutrophil entry as a potential neuroprotective therapy.
      Keywords: inflammation, macrophage, neutrophil, oxidative activity, SCI
    • The composition of motor recovery revealed by syllable-level behavioral analysis after spinal cord injury
      Abraira, Eisdorfer, Lin, Thackray, Theis, Vivinetto, Oputa, Nacht, Martinez, Tschang, Mahmood, Tucker, Ruven, Pusuloori, Zymoro, Phu, Birch, Kumar, Schachner, Sahni, Popovich, Ferguson, McTigue, Tysseling, Dulin, Hollis II, Datta | 2026
      The multi-institutional study investigates motor recovery in mice and rats following spinal cord injury using a unique syllable-level behavioral analysis approach, revealing heterogeneity in recovery across subjects and changes shaped by injury severity and individual identity. The findings suggest that recovery after SCI is composed of repertoire-level changes under a disrupted brain-spinal cord axis, and the researchers propose that a more comprehensive understanding of post-SCI recovery can be gained by analyzing various behaviors and biomechanical signatures rather than relying solely on traditional methods like the BMS scale.
    • Timed microglia depletion promotes functional network reorganization and motor recovery after ischaemic stroke
      Isla Cainzos, Quandt, Borggrewe, Altjohann, Magnus, Biskamp | 2026
      The study published in Brain Communications investigates the impact of short-term microglia depletion during the subacute phase following ischemic stroke on functional recovery and network dynamics in mice, using PLX5622. It found that this treatment significantly improved fine motor skills by day 7, as demonstrated by better performance in a sensorimotor test (SPR task), and revealed changes in sensorimotor network dynamics, including increased lower beta connectivity within the ipsilesional motor cortex during active periods, and a decrease in gamma connectivity in the contralesional sensorimotor system. The findings suggest that targeted suppression of activated microglia during the subacute phase may facilitate synaptic plasticity and network reorganization, enhancing functional recovery.
      Keywords: ischaemic stroke, CSF-1R inhibitor, microglial repopulation, sensorimotor networks, motor rehabilitation
    • Transcriptome of neonatal preBötzinger complex neurones in Dbx1 reporter mice

      This 2017 Nature Scientific Reports study investigates the unique characteristics of Dbx1 and non-Dbx1 neurons within the preBötC, a respiratory control center in the brain, through gene expression analysis. Key findings include Dbx1 neurons being primary excitatory, glutamatergic neurons, with some exhibiting chloride-mediated synaptic inhibition, and differences in the expression of transcription factors and Synaptotagmin-10 between these neuron types.     • Ventral cervical EES in opioid-induced respiratory depression

      The study found that mild to moderate intensity ventral cervical electrical stimulation at frequencies below 100 Hz did not significantly impact respiratory activity during opioid-induced respiratory depression in anesthetized humans undergoing anterior cervical spinal impingement surgeries, suggesting it may not directly access or indirectly activate ventral motor neurons during this condition.

    • Adenosine and ATP Link PCO2 to Cortical Excitability via pH
      Dulla, Dobelis, Pearson, Frenguelli, Staley, Masino | 2005
      This 2005 scientific paper published in Neuron investigates the impact of carbon dioxide (CO2) levels on neural function, particularly in the hippocampus, and finds that increased CO2 levels decrease neuronal excitability through adenosine A1 receptor activation, while decreased CO2 levels increase excitability. The study suggests that CO2 modulates neuronal excitability and epileptiform activity through its effects on adenosine and purines, which could have implications for the development of new therapies for epilepsy and other neurological disorders.

    • Brain motion is driven by mechanical coupling with the abdomen
      Garborg, Ghitti, Zhang, Ricotta, Frank, Mueller, Greenawalt, Turner, Kedarasetti, Mostafa, Lee, Costanzo, Drew | 2026
      The study published in Nature Neuroscience investigates the motion of the dorsal cortex within the skull of awake mice, revealing that brain motion is primarily driven by locomotion rather than respiration or the cardiac cycle. Researchers found that forces generated by abdominal muscle contractions reach the brain via the vertebral venous plexus (VVP), a network of valveless veins connecting the abdomen and spinal canal, suggesting a potential hydraulic system driving cerebrospinal fluid flow. The findings suggest a mechanical link between the brain and the abdominal compartment, with implications for understanding pathologies such as intracranial hypertension, meningioma, Chiari malformations, and conditions like obesity that elevate intra-abdominal pressure.
    • Clinical Application of Near Infrared Blood Oxygen Monitoring in Cervical Spinal Cord Injury

The research, conducted on 37 patients, found an improvement in regional SO2 after laminectomy and durotomy, suggesting that NIR-SO2 is safe, feasible, and could potentially predict recovery and guide treatment choices for such patients.

    • Hyperglycosylation is a metabolic driver of Alzheimer’s disease
      Liu, Ribas, Medina, Nielsen, Clarke, Ma, Mueller, Plasencia, Sheer, Simpson, Soto, Sudderth, Cai, Cantrell, Colpaert, Shedlock, Wu, Young, Kooser, Chen, Huang, He, Alonge, Guo, Bian, Vander Kooi, Gentry, Sun, Quinones, Son, Azadi, Deberardinis, Prokop, Allison, Yang, Chen, Huang, He, Guo, Gao, Kooi, Gentry, & Sun | 2026
      This Nature Metabolism study investigates the role of hyperglycosylation, specifically N-glycosylation, in Alzheimer's disease (AD). The research suggests that excessive N-glycosylation contributes to the conserved phenotype of brain hyperglycosylation in AD, and reducing this excess improves social recognition memory in AD mouse models. Genetic knockdown of glycan biosynthetic enzymes showed improved cognitive outcomes, while glucosamine supplementation worsened behavior in these mice. A retrospective analysis of electronic health records revealed that glucosamine supplementation was associated with accelerated AD progression and reduced survival in patients with mild cognitive impairment (MCI) and dementia, highlighting the need for further investigation into cell-type-specific spatial information and pharmacological interventions targeting the hexosamine pathway in animal models.
    • Monitoring Spinal Cord Tissue Oxygen in Patients With Acute, Severe Traumatic Spinal Cord Injuries
      Visagan, Hogg, Gallagher, Kearney, Zoumprouli, Papadopoulos, Saadoun | 2022
      The study published in Critical Care Medicine investigates the use of an intraspinal pressure catheter and tissue oxygen monitor to assess cord tissue oxygen levels at the site of spinal cord injury in 26 acute spinal cord injury patients. Key findings include associations between tissue oxygen levels and Fio2, Pao2, intraspinal pressure, spinal cord perfusion pressure, sedation level, glucose, lactate/pyruvate ratio, fever, blood transfusions, cerebrospinal fluid drainage, and motor-complete outcomes. Monitoring tissue oxygen could potentially provide valuable information for managing patients with acute spinal cord injuries, but further research is needed to confirm these findings and determine clinical utility. Limitations include a small sample size and complications such as cerebrospinal fluid leaks.
      Keywords: Licox, microdialysis, monitoring, perfusion pressure, spinal cord injury, tissue oxygen
    • Neuromodulation for the treatment of moderate to severe asthma – a pilot first-in-human clinical study
      Gehlsen, Carrasco, Hooghius, Pyles | 2025
      The July 2025 study published in the Journal of Asthma presents two pilot clinical trials investigating neuromodulation as a potential treatment for moderate to severe asthma. One trial used a non-invasive device to stimulate the vagus nerve, showing significant improvements in pulmonary function tests and asthma symptom scores after six weeks for 10 participants. The other study involved SCS implantation in 11 patients with chronic pain, resulting in complete elimination of asthma symptoms within days, improved asthma control, reduced medication usage, and enhanced quality of life. Future research will focus on larger controlled clinical studies to further establish the safety and efficacy of these neuromodulation techniques as treatment options for asthma patients.
    • Obstructive Sleep Apnea, Positive Airway Pressure, and Implications of Early Treatment in Parkinson Disease
      Lee, Neilson, Montaño, May, Sicard, Cho, Iliff, Elliott, Lim, Scott | 2025
      The scientific paper 'Obstructive Sleep Apnea, Positive Airway Pressure, and Implications of Early Treatment in Parkinson Disease' suggests that Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) is a modifiable midlife risk factor for developing Parkinson's disease (PD), especially among women. The study found a higher incidence of PD among veterans with OSA compared to those without, and early treatment with CPAP significantly reduced the number of PD cases and associated risks such as falls, fractures, and mortality. However, limitations include an inability to assess CPAP adherence beyond administrative codes and a need for further prospective studies to confirm and extend these findings.
    • Safety and feasibility of paired vagus nerve stimulation with rehabilitation for improving upper extremity function in people with cervical spinal cord injury: a pilot randomized controlled trial
      Korupolu, Yozbatiran, Miller, Shields, Tandon | 2026
      This scientific paper titled "Safety and feasibility of paired vagus nerve stimulation with rehabilitation for improving upper extremity function in people with cervical spinal cord injury: a pilot randomized controlled trial" aimed to evaluate the safety, feasibility, and therapeutic potential of pairing invasive Vagus Nerve Stimulation (VNS) with upper extremity rehabilitation in individuals with chronic cervical Spinal Cord Injury (SCI). The study demonstrated that the intervention was safe, well-tolerated, and feasible in SCI patients. At immediate post-intervention assessment, all participants receiving active VNS showed improvement in upper extremity motor function compared with baseline, whereas none of the participants in the control group showed improvement. However, these findings should be interpreted cautiously due to the small sample size and exploratory nature of the analysis. The study also found that transportation and access barriers affected recruitment and limited completion of in-clinic sessions, highlighting the need for more accessible and flexible rehabilitation delivery models. Further investigation is required with larger trials to determine the efficacy, durability, and optimal implementation of VNS paired with rehabilitation in spinal cord injury.
    • Spinal cord stimulation for upper limb motor function in people with chronic post-stroke hemiparesis: a feasibility trial
      2026
      The Nature Medicine study investigates the feasibility of cervical epidural spinal cord stimulation (SCS) for improving upper limb motor function in individuals with chronic post-stroke hemiparesis over a four-week period. Significant improvements were observed in strength, reduced spasticity, and increased Fugl-Meyer Assessment points. The study suggests that cervical SCS is safe, feasible, and effective for chronic post-stroke hemiparesis, but further research is needed to determine long-term effectiveness and safety, as well as the determinants of responsiveness to SCS, particularly spared sensory function.

    • A neuronal architecture underlying autonomic dysreflexia
      Soriano, Hudelle, Mahe, Gautier, Teo, Skinnider, Laskaratos, Ceto, Kathe, Hutson, Charbonneau, Girgis, Casha, Rimok, Tso, Larkin-Kaiser, Gandhi, Amir, Kang, Vyza, Martin-Moraud, Lacour, Demesmaeker, Asboth, Barraud, Anderson, Bloch, Squair, Phillips, Courtine | 2025
      The scientific paper explores the neuronal architecture responsible for autonomic dysreflexia (AD) in mice with spinal cord injury, focusing on maladaptive connections between lower lumbosacral and lower thoracic spinal cord after SCI. Key findings suggest vGLUT2ON excitatory neurons are activated during AD, with Vsx2ON being the most transcriptionally responsive subpopulation.     • A neuroprosthesis for restoring hand movement and sensation in a person with complete tetraplegia
      Bouton | 2026
      The scientific paper presents a hybrid Dual Neuromodulation (DNB) system that combines an intracortical brain-computer interface, transcutaneous spinal cord stimulation, and peripheral stimuli for restoring motor and sensory functions in individuals with spinal cord injuries. The study on one participant showed significant improvements in motor control and sensory recovery, with potential for home use. Two related studies were discussed, both focusing on neural modulation in response to tactile stimuli and emphasizing the importance of understanding brain activity associated with different body parts and various types of stimuli.       The scientific paper "Artificial Hibernation Reveals Synaptic Engram Architecture Associated with Memory Retention" reveals that memories can remain stable despite widespread synapse loss during hibernation, and identifies clustered synaptic connections among memory-encoding neurons as a structural feature associated with memory retention.       This study investigates the role of connexin43 (Cx43) gap junctions in astrocyte networks within the mouse brain, revealing their significance in maintaining network connectivity, brain functioning, and potential implications for neurodegenerative diseases.     • Astrocytes connect specific brain regions through plastic networks
      Cooper, Selles, Cammer, Redd, Gildea, Sall, Chiurri, Cheung, Wheeler, Saab, Liddelow, Chao | 2026
      This scientific paper explores the structural plasticity of astrocyte networks in mice brains following whisker trimming, and the role of connexin43 (Cx43) gap junctions in these networks. Key findings suggest that long-range astrocyte networks exhibit substantial deviations post-whisker trimming, and deleting Cx43 results in enhanced locomotor activity and accelerated hippocampal spreading depression.     • Bringing the genetically minimal cell to life on a computer in 4D
      Thornburg, Maytin, Kwon, Brier, Gilbert, Fu, Gao, Quenneville, Wu, Li, Long, Pezeshkian, Sun, Matias de C. Bittencourt, Glass, Mehta, Ha, Luthey-Schulten | 2026
      The text presents a four-dimensional whole-cell model (4DWCM) that simulates the entire cell cycle of JCVI-syn3A, a minimal bacterium with a synthetic genome, accounting for genetic information processes, metabolism, growth, and cell division. The model integrates hybrid computational methods to account for morphological transformations during growth, allowing for predictions not only for average behavior but also the heterogeneity among cells. The 4DWCM recovers experimental measurements and accurately predicts various cellular properties such as doubling times, mRNA half-lives, protein distributions, and ribosome counts, providing a significant advancement in understanding fundamental behaviors in cellular life and potential applications in targeted therapy design.

    • Decoding neuronal criticality firing patterns for large brain based EEG
      Beres, Ribeiro Rodrigues, Myers, Napoli
      This scientific study introduces a new method for decoding large-scale Electroencephalogram (EEG) neural recordings during cognitive tasks, focusing on demonstrating self-organization of neuronal networks indicative of neural criticality. By analyzing EEG data from 49 subjects and using hierarchical arrangements, the researchers discover power laws for multiple neuronal oscillatory groups, demonstrating scale-invariance through log–log plots, and characterizing criticality on the millisecond to seconds time scale relevant to cognitive tasks. This method could offer a significant advancement in understanding the dynamics of the neuronal network and its role in cognitive processes, potentially informing AI and machine learning developments.
      Keywords: Electroencephalography (EEG), Neural oscillations, Neural criticality, Critical brain hypothesis, Newell’s time scale, Scale invariance, Power law, Cognitive impairment, Hypoxia

    • Galvanin (TMEM154) is an electric-field sensor for directed cell migration
      Belliveau, Footer, Platenkamp, Rodriguez, Kim, Prinz, van Loon, Lin, Eustis, Chan, Cohen, Theriot | 2026
      The research published in Cell (2026) by Belliveau et al. identifies Galvanin as a crucial electric-field sensor essential for directed migration in various rapidly moving cells such as immune and epithelial cells. Upon exposure to electric fields, Galvanin localizes at the front and rear of cells, changing spatial patterns, exhibiting an exponential decay with a net charge of -18e, and regulating subsequent movement responses. The study suggests that a highly negatively charged construct can rescue directed migration in knockout cell lines, indicating a high net negative ectodomain charge is sufficient for Galvanin-mediated electrotaxis. Further studies are needed to understand the molecular mechanisms underlying Galvanin's role in electrotaxis and its implications for cell signaling and other biological processes.

    • Linking spinal circuit reorganization to recovery after thoracic spinal cord injury
      Shevtsova, Lockhart, Rybak, Magnuson, Danner | 2025

    • Molecular mechanism of NMDA receptor potentiation by lactate
      Fiumelli, Herrera-López, Lemtiri-Chlieh, Mottier, Girgis, Ben-Adiba, Jourdain, Carrano, Mahmood, Ooi, Arold, Di Luca, Gardoni, Magistretti | 2026
      The research study published in The Journal of Physiology in 2026 investigates the role of lactate produced by astrocytes in enhancing neuronal NMDA receptor function, synaptic plasticity, and memory formation via a redox mechanism. This process involves lactate entering neurons through monocarboxylate transporters (MCTs), being oxidized to pyruvate, and generating NADH, which increases the intracellular NADH/NAD+ ratio. This redox shift promotes calcium release from ryanodine-sensitive stores (RyR) and recruits Ca2+/calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II (CaMKII) to GluN2B-containing NMDARs, leading to enhanced NMDAR currents and prolonged decay kinetics. This strengthened NMDAR–CaMKII signaling increases NMDAR presence at postsynaptic sites, facilitating the molecular processes underlying long-term synaptic plasticity and memory formation. The study was conducted by a team from various institutions and reveals a molecular pathway linking brain metabolism with learning and memory processes.
    • Mu opioid receptors gate the locus coeruleus pain generator
      Kuo, Norris, Dunn, Becker, Kim, Vazquez, Borges, Thang, O’Brien, Parker, McCall | 2026
      The study by Kuo et al. identifies the locus coeruleus as a key player in chronic pain regulation, transforming from an endogenous analgesia source to a pain generator following nerve injury. Mu opioid receptors (MOR) on distinct projections within the LC were found to robustly inhibit it and drive antinociception. The research suggests that restoring LC opioid signaling could potentially reverse neuropathic hypersensitivity, offering potential therapeutic targets for pain management, although with limitations such as global NE-conditional MOR knockout and focus on specific time points post-neuropathic injury.
    • Noradrenergic neuromodulation produces a NMDAR-dependent network state of respiratory rhythmogenesis in the preBötzinger Complex
      Tryba, Viemari, Wang, Garcia | 2026
      This preprint study investigates the effects of Norepinephrine (NE) and Nitric Oxide (NO) on the preBotanic Complex (preBötC), a critical brainstem region for respiratory rhythm generation, in rodents. The research finds that NE increases action potential frequency while decreasing the average action potential number during network bursts, and NO attenuates synchrony by activating soluble guanylyl cyclase and cGMP-dependent protein kinase (PKG). Combined application of NA and NO leads to complex interactions with overall effects dependent on their relative concentrations. The study suggests potential roles for NE and NO modulation in abnormal respiratory patterns associated with chronic hypoxia, offering insights for new therapeutic strategies for treating hypoxia-related respiratory disorders. However, further peer review is necessary as this is a preprint.
    • Protocol for head-restrained drug self-administration in mice
      Doncheck, Clarke, Gordon, Boquiren, Manusky, Baek, Paniccia, Dinu, Dereschewitz, Grant, Stuber, Otis | 2026
      This protocol article describes a low-cost, standardized method for training head-fixed mice to self-administer drugs using 3D-printed equipment and open-source software (REACHER/Labrynth). The aim is to facilitate research on drug reward and motivation in rodents using head fixation systems.     • Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance
      Kipnis, Benveniste, Eichmann, Thomas, Reich, Lewis, Tsai, Drieu, Bakker, Kelley, Lundgaard, Mestre, Zott, Eide, van Osch, van Veluw, Iliff, Yang, Santambrogio, Da Mesquita, Daneman, Rustenhoven, Goldman, Nedergaard
      The scientific paper "Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance" aims to advance our understanding of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) dynamics in brain waste clearance, focusing on periarterial spaces, glymphatic and meningeal lymphatic systems, their functional connections, and immune cell involvement. Key findings reveal primary CSF influx along periarterial spaces, solute clearance through multiple routes, and functional influence by vascular pulsation, neural activity, sleep, and immunity. The study highlights the role of immune cells at brain-border niches in monitoring solute efflux, linking brain clearance to neuroimmune interactions. The research aims to uncover new therapeutic avenues for various disorders such as autoimmune, neurodegenerative, neoplastic, and psychiatric diseases.
    • Review on Extracellular Spike Waveforms: Morphology, Biophysics, and Classification Strategies
      Shi Hai Sun, Ibbotson
      This scientific paper focuses on the exploration of extracellular spike waveforms and their classifications in neuroscience research, emphasizing their importance in understanding neuron morphology, connectivity, and identity. The study employs three common waveform classification techniques: negative waveform classification, decision-tree classifications, and automated clustering algorithms. Key findings reveal distinct phases in various spike types, with challenges in machine learning methods for spike classification due to data size requirements, reduced interpretability, and reproducibility issues. The paper concludes that improved spike waveform classification techniques can unlock further insights into neuronal functions and connections, contributing to a better comprehension of brain mechanisms as recording technologies advance.
      Keywords: biophysics, electrophysiology, extracellular recording, neuronal classification, spike waveforms
    • Robust network oscillations during mammalian respiratory rhythm generation driven by synaptic dynamics
      Guerriera, Hayes, Fortin, Holcman | 2015

    • The Rostral Ventrolateral Medulla Input to Central Amygdala Regulates Anxiety-­Like Behaviors in Mice for Chronic Light Exposure
      Jing, Tan, Xu, Yuan, Fan, Wang | 2026
      This Original Article, published in 2026 in CNS Neuroscience & Therapeutics, investigates the impact of chronic light exposure on anxiety-like behaviors in mice and the role of the rostral ventrolateral medulla (RVLM) input to the central amygdala (CeA). The study reveals that chronic light exposure enhances anxiety-like behaviors, which are regulated by the RVLM input to the CeA, suggesting a potential novel approach for treating anxiety disorders under conditions of prolonged light exposure. However, the research was limited by not addressing physical or pathological disconnection effects under light exposure conditions, being conducted exclusively in male mice, and not publicly releasing the data due to privacy or ethical restrictions. Future studies are necessary to validate the RVLM→CeA bidirectional feedback loop hypothesis and determine whether findings generalize to female subjects.
      Keywords: anxiety-­like behaviors, blood pressure, central amygdala, chronic light exposure, rostral ventrolateral medulla

    • Finding the Groove in Neural Space
      Bhatt, Sheets, Jordan, Downey, Merchant, Greenspon | 2026
      The study "Finding the Groove in Neural Space" investigates how the human brain processes rhythmic patterns by examining neural signatures during rhythmic tapping tasks in two participants with spinal cord injuries. Findings suggest that motor cortex encodes position, velocity, and tempo-dependent differences simultaneously and a continuous tempo axis exists in neural space.     • GABAA receptor mediated facilitation of proprioceptive afferent transmission to motoneurons is facilitated by TRPM8 channels on C-fibres that are gated by serotonin
      Black, Li, Hari, Lin, Stephens, Fouad, Fenrich, Lucas-Osma, Gorassini, Bennett | 2026
      The study conducted at the University of Alberta investigates how serotonin modulates sensory transmission within the monosynaptic stretch reflex from proprioceptive Ia afferents to motoneurons, using zolmitriptan as an example. It finds that serotonin, via its main receptor subtype 5-HT1D on nociceptive C-fibers, induces a long-lasting tonic depolarization in Ia afferents through increased GABAergic input onto them, promoting sodium spike initiation. Activation of 5-HT1D receptors with zolmitriptan inhibits C-fiber activity and reduces the GABAA receptor-mediated tonic depolarization on Ia afferents, decreasing spike propagation to motoneurons. The research suggests that brainstem-derived serotonin not only inhibits ongoing nociceptive C-fibers activity but also reduces GABAergic neuron activity and sensory transmission in large proprioceptive afferents, leading to decreased motoneuron activity. This could have implications for pain studies and challenges traditional concepts of sensory gating.
    • In vivo multiplex imaging of dynamic neurochemical networks with designed far-­red dopamine sensors
      Zheng, Cai, Wang, Zhang, Zhuo, Dong, Zhang, Wang, Deng, Ji, Cui, Fang, Xinzhang, Huang, Kecheng Zhang, Jinxu Wang, Grimm, Johnsson, Schreiter, Lavis, Chen, Mu, Li
      The scientific paper presents HaloDA1.0, a new genetically encoded sensor for real-time monitoring of dopamine dynamics in various biological systems.     • Molecular mechanism of NMDA receptor potentiation by lactate
      Fiumelli, Herrera-López, Lemtiri-Chlieh, Mottier, Girgis, Ben-Adiba, Jourdain, Carrano, Mahmood, Ooi, Arold, Di Luca, Gardoni, Magistretti | 2026
      The research study published in The Journal of Physiology in 2026 investigates the role of lactate produced by astrocytes in enhancing neuronal NMDA receptor function, synaptic plasticity, and memory formation via a redox mechanism. This process involves lactate entering neurons through monocarboxylate transporters (MCTs), being oxidized to pyruvate, and generating NADH, which increases the intracellular NADH/NAD+ ratio. This redox shift promotes calcium release from ryanodine-sensitive stores (RyR) and recruits Ca2+/calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II (CaMKII) to GluN2B-containing NMDARs, leading to enhanced NMDAR currents and prolonged decay kinetics. This strengthened NMDAR–CaMKII signaling increases NMDAR presence at postsynaptic sites, facilitating the molecular processes underlying long-term synaptic plasticity and memory formation. The study was conducted by a team from various institutions and reveals a molecular pathway linking brain metabolism with learning and memory processes.

      The study published in Journal of Neurochemistry reveals that smaller motor neurons in adult rats express more Tropomyosin-Related Kinase Receptor B (TRKB) mRNA than larger ones, which could have implications for neurotrophic dependence and functional adaptability of different types of motor units in the diaphragm muscle.     • Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation
      Balaguer, Prat-Ortega, Ostrowski, Borda, Verma, Yadav, Sorensen, de Freitas, Ensel, Donadio, Liang, Ho, Damiani, Grigsby, Fields, Gonzalez-Martinez, Gerszten, Fisher, Weber, Pirondini, Capogrosso | 2025
      In the study "Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation," Balaguer et al. (2025) reveal that residual brain inputs do not facilitate SCS-mediated motor control but rather transform subthreshold SCS-generated excitatory postsynaptic potentials into action potentials, enabling brain regulation and volitional rate control during spinal cord stimulation in individuals with spinal cord injury and stroke. The study also highlights the limitation of SCS in severe injury cases due to a restricted set of functional stimulation parameters, while demonstrating fine control over force output by participants and improving the ability to modulate motor neuron firing rates through incorporating supraspinal inhibitory drive.
    • Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation
      Balaguer, Prat-Ortega, Ostrowski, Borda, Verma, Yadav, Sorensen, de Freitas, Ensel, Donadio, Liang, Ho, Damiani, Grigsby, Fields, Gonzalez-Martinez, Gerszten, Fisher, Weber, Pirondini, Capogrosso | 2025
      In the study 'Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation' (Balaguer et al., 2025), researchers discovered that residual brain inputs modulate motoneuron membrane potentials, allowing subthreshold SCS pulses to transform into action potentials and entrain motoneuron activity to the stimulation. The study found that only a subset of SCS parameters enables volitional motor control, and this functional parameter space shrinks with lesion severity. The findings have potential implications for treating neurological disorders like spinal cord injuries, where SCS could help restore motor function by allowing participants to leverage residual supraspinal inhibition to control motoneuron firing rate. However, the study's limitations include focusing on immediate SCS effects and not accounting for long-term changes due to physical training.
    • Potentiation of active locomotor state by spinal-projecting serotonergic neurons
      Fenstermacher, Vonasek, Cavanagh, Gattuso, Chaimowitz, Jessell, Dymecki, Dasen | 2025
      The scientific paper by Fenstermacher et al. investigates the roles of spinal-projecting serotonergic (5-HT) neurons in locomotor behavior using optogenetic techniques on mice. Activation of these neurons increases the speed and duration of ongoing locomotion, with key findings showing an increase in individual run bouts' length, fewer overall run bouts, and a shift towards faster speeds during locomotion. The study suggests that serotonergic neurons can potentiate motor output over extended time scales, enhancing locomotor output and modulating the temporal dynamics of motor circuits. The research has potential implications for understanding the function of serotonin in motor behavior and developing innovative approaches to control locomotion in neuroprosthetics.
