    • Resistance training-induced adaptations in the neuromuscular system: Physiological mechanisms and implications for human performance
      Lecce, Amoruso, Felici, Bazzucchi | 2026
      This topical review published in The Journal of Physiology by Edoardo Lecce's team from the University of Rome 'Foro Italico' explores neuromuscular adaptations resulting from resistance training (RT). They find increased excitability, improved synaptic input transmission, and decreased synaptic noise in the brain and spinal cord, leading to a more stable neural drive. At the spinal level, intrinsic motoneuron excitability, neuromodulatory input, and spinal circuitry are modified, resulting in enhanced motor unit (MU) excitability due to attenuated inhibitory pathways. Skeletal muscle adaptations involve contractile protein synthesis, structural repair by satellite cells, and endurance capacity improvement through mitochondrial biogenesis and capillarization. These changes lead to increased strength, improved physical performance, and alterations in muscle fiber type composition, energy metabolism, glucose uptake, and hormone levels, with practical applications for healthy individuals and those affected by chronic disorders.
    • Respiratory-related evoked potential measures of respiratory sensory gating

The research confirms the presence of temporal sensory gating for respiratory mechanoreceptor stimuli, somatosensory stimuli via skin air puffs, and auditory stimuli. Reduced sensory gating, or increased S2/S1 ratio, could potentially serve as a marker for respiratory-related anxiety disorders in asthma patients.
      Keywords: respiratory sensation, inspiratory occlusion, cerebral cortex, mechanosensation
    • Spinal cord stimulation for upper limb motor function in people with chronic post-stroke hemiparesis: a feasibility trial

    • Test-retest reliability of threshold-tracking TMS and peripheral nerve testing in healthy adults and individuals with chronic cervical spinal cord injury
      Hrycyk, Gramm Kristensen, Tankisi, Spooren, Tedesco Triccas
      The study investigates the test-retest reliability of neuroexcitability measures using threshold-tracking methods in both healthy adults and individuals with chronic cervical spinal cord injury (SCI). It finds excellent relative reliability for TMS measures in both groups, high test-retest reliability for NET using the TROND protocol across all age groups, and the T-T RMT test being more efficient.     • Timed microglia depletion promotes functional network reorganization and motor recovery after ischaemic stroke
      Isla Cainzos, Quandt, Borggrewe, Altjohann, Magnus, Biskamp | 2026
      The study published in Brain Communications investigates the impact of short-term microglia depletion during the subacute phase following ischemic stroke on functional recovery and network dynamics in mice, using PLX5622. It found that this treatment significantly improved fine motor skills by day 7, as demonstrated by better performance in a sensorimotor test (SPR task), and revealed changes in sensorimotor network dynamics, including increased lower beta connectivity within the ipsilesional motor cortex during active periods, and a decrease in gamma connectivity in the contralesional sensorimotor system.     • 4-Aminopyridine-sensitive outward currents in preBötzinger complex neurons influence respiratory rhythm generation in neonatal mice
      Hayes, Mendenhall, Brush, Del Negro | 2008
      The 2008 study by Hayes et al. investigates the role of a transient outward potassium current, IA, in shaping inspiratory bursts during respiratory activity within the preBötzinger complex (preBötC) in neonatal mice. The researchers found that more than half of inspiratory neurons express IA and that its maximum conductance positively correlates with membrane capacitance and negatively with resistance, with a time constant of inactivation between 20-80ms. 4-aminopyridine (4-AP), an IA blocker, was used to study its role in preBc neurons and caused disorganized XII output and noisy preBötC rhythms that fluctuate in amplitude and period, suggesting that IA plays a crucial role in the regularity of the cycle period and drive potential magnitude in preBötC neurons. The study proposes that further research is needed to clarify the specific contributions of different Kv subunits to preBötC IA and its functional significance in respiration.
    • A neuronal architecture underlying autonomic dysreflexia
      Soriano, Hudelle, Mahe, Gautier, Teo, Skinnider, Laskaratos, Ceto, Kathe, Hutson, Charbonneau, Girgis, Casha, Rimok, Tso, Larkin-Kaiser, Gandhi, Amir, Kang, Vyza, Martin-Moraud, Lacour, Demesmaeker, Asboth, Barraud, Anderson, Bloch, Squair, Phillips, Courtine | 2025
      The scientific paper explores the neuronal architecture responsible for autonomic dysreflexia (AD) in mice with spinal cord injury, focusing on maladaptive connections between lower lumbosacral and lower thoracic spinal cord after SCI. Key findings suggest vGLUT2ON excitatory neurons are activated during AD, with Vsx2ON being the most transcriptionally responsive subpopulation. The study also discusses an implantable system designed to restore hemodynamic stability in individuals with spinal cord injuries, featuring epidural electrodes, a brain-computer interface, and a closed-loop control system, which showed improved results compared to the control group in preclinical models and one human participant. The research highlights potential applications of this system beyond SCI management, but acknowledges limitations such as biocompatibility, long-term stability, and safety concerns for future optimization.

    • Adenosine and ATP Link PCO2 to Cortical Excitability via pH
      Dulla, Dobelis, Pearson, Frenguelli, Staley, Masino | 2005
      This 2005 scientific paper published in Neuron investigates the impact of carbon dioxide (CO2) levels on neural function, particularly in the hippocampus, and finds that increased CO2 levels decrease neuronal excitability through adenosine A1 receptor activation, while decreased CO2 levels increase excitability. The study suggests that CO2 modulates neuronal excitability and epileptiform activity through its effects on adenosine and purines, which could have implications for the development of new therapies for epilepsy and other neurological disorders.
    • Amino acid modulation of carotid body function
      Gold, Pauza, Felippe, Shen, Paton | 2026
      This scientific paper published in The Journal of Physiology investigates the role of amino acids, particularly glutamate, in modulating carotid body function for arterial chemoreception and respiratory regulation. The study finds that during hypoxia, glutamate stimulates postsynaptic petrosal neurons and adjacent glomus cells, while GABA inhibits excitation of neighboring glomus cells, creating an 'accelerator-brake' mechanism that regulates the sensitivity of the carotid body to hypoxia. The study suggests that amino acids such as glutamate and GABA play significant roles in regulating respiratory control through carotid body function, particularly contributing to long-term adaptations seen after repeated hypoxia, potentially explaining heightened chemoreflex sensitivity observed in human conditions involving intermittent hypoxia, such as sleep apnea. The study has experimental limitations, including using potassium cyanide instead of physiological hypoxia and relying on pharmacological approaches to investigate glutamatergic and GABAergic signaling.
    • An optimized 'hypoxia in a pill' regimen reverses neurodegenerative disease phenotypes in multiple preclinical models
      Wang1,2,3, Marutani4, Zazzeron4, Menard4, Volpicelli-Daley5, Ichinose4, Mootha1,2,3

    • Astrocytes connect specific brain regions through plastic networks
      Cooper, Selles, Cammer, Redd, Gildea, Sall, Chiurri, Cheung, Wheeler, Saab, Liddelow, Chao | 2026
      This scientific paper explores the structural plasticity of astrocyte networks in mice brains following whisker trimming, and the role of connexin43 (Cx43) gap junctions in these networks.     • Astrocytes: Orchestrators of brain gas exchange and oxygen homeostasis
      Christie | 2026
      Astrocytes play a critical role in brain oxygen sensing, cerebral perfusion, and cognition, with a higher sensitivity to changes in oxygen availability compared to glomus cells. They respond to hypoxia by releasing nitric oxide, regulating sympathetic drive, and cardiac output to maintain brain oxygenation. However, during ischemia, astrocytes can both protect neurons and exacerbate brain damage due to their involvement in neurotransmitter release, calcium signaling, and gap junction communication. Future research should explore therapeutic interventions targeting astrocytic functions for improving neurological disorders related to oxygen homeostasis, cognitive impairment, and dementia.
    • BDNF is necessary and sufficient for spinal respiratory plasticity following intermittent hypoxia
      Baker-Herman, Fuller, Bavis, Zabka, Golder, Doperalski, Johnson, Watters, Mitchell | 2003
      In the study, researchers found that brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) plays a crucial role in long-lasting phrenic facilitation (pLTF), a model of respiratory plasticity induced by intermittent hypoxia. The process involves serotonin-dependent BDNF synthesis near phrenic motoneurons, and BDNF administration elicits pLTF without hypoxia. Endogenous BDNF synthesis is necessary for pLTF, and the effect requires activation of a high affinity TrkB receptor. The findings could have implications for conditions such as Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS), obstructive sleep apnea, and respiratory failure following spinal cord injury.

      This study published in 'The Journal of Physiology' investigates the role of Brain-Derived Neurotrophic Factor (BDNF) and Platelet Factor 4 (PF4) during exercise at sea level and high altitude, revealing that maximal exercise doubles free BDNF concentration, with platelets playing a role in liberating BDNF and potentially contributing to the neuroprotective effects of exercise. At high altitude, hypoxia promotes net cerebral release of BDNF, and there is a correlation between platelet loss during exercise and PF4 release into venous circulation.

    • GABAergic Hub Neurons Orchestrate Synchrony in Developing Hippocampal Networks
      Bonifazi, Goldin, Picardo, Jorquera, Cattani, Bianconi, Represa, Ben-Ari, Cossart
      The scientific paper investigates the significant role of high connectivity GABAergic interneurons, or hub neurons, in regulating network dynamics during hippocampus development, particularly their impact on Giant Depolarizing Potentials (GDPs). Key findings show that a third of targeted hub neurons significantly affect network dynamics compared to minimal impact from low connectivity neurons. Hub neurons are predominantly found in specific layers like the stratum oriens and lucidum, and activation of these neurons before synchrony contributes to network synchronization in the developing CA3 region. The study also highlights differences between hub and low connectivity interneurons in terms of electrophysiological properties and references notable authors' works to provide insights into physiological and pathological network oscillations.

    • Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation
      Balaguer, Prat-Ortega, Ostrowski, Borda, Verma, Yadav, Sorensen, de Freitas, Ensel, Donadio, Liang, Ho, Damiani, Grigsby, Fields, Gonzalez-Martinez, Gerszten, Fisher, Weber, Pirondini, Capogrosso | 2025
      In the study "Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation," Balaguer et al. (2025) reveal that residual brain inputs do not facilitate SCS-mediated motor control but rather transform subthreshold SCS-generated excitatory postsynaptic potentials into action potentials, enabling brain regulation and volitional rate control during spinal cord stimulation in individuals with spinal cord injury and stroke. The study also highlights the limitation of SCS in severe injury cases due to a restricted set of functional stimulation parameters, while demonstrating fine control over force output by participants and improving the ability to modulate motor neuron firing rates through incorporating supraspinal inhibitory drive.
    • Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation
      Balaguer, Prat-Ortega, Ostrowski, Borda, Verma, Yadav, Sorensen, de Freitas, Ensel, Donadio, Liang, Ho, Damiani, Grigsby, Fields, Gonzalez-Martinez, Gerszten, Fisher, Weber, Pirondini, Capogrosso | 2025
      In the study 'Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation' (Balaguer et al., 2025), researchers discovered that residual brain inputs modulate motoneuron membrane potentials, allowing subthreshold SCS pulses to transform into action potentials and entrain motoneuron activity to the stimulation. The study found that only a subset of SCS parameters enables volitional motor control, and this functional parameter space shrinks with lesion severity. The findings have potential implications for treating neurological disorders like spinal cord injuries, where SCS could help restore motor function by allowing participants to leverage residual supraspinal inhibition to control motoneuron firing rate. However, the study's limitations include focusing on immediate SCS effects and not accounting for long-term changes due to physical training.

    • Review: Cell-type-specific APOE4 cascade across the Alzheimer’s disease continuum
      Shostak, Liang, Huang | 2026
      The review "Cell-type-specific APOE4 cascade across the Alzheimer’s disease continuum" highlights Apolipoprotein E4 (APOE4) as the leading genetic risk factor for Alzheimer's disease (AD), causing early neuronal deficits in preclinical AD stages through age-associated stress. This neuronal damage triggers a harmful glial response, amplifying neurodegeneration and accelerating the onset of prodromal and dementia AD stages.     • Safety and feasibility of paired vagus nerve stimulation with rehabilitation for improving upper extremity function in people with cervical spinal cord injury: a pilot randomized controlled trial
      Korupolu, Yozbatiran, Miller, Shields, Tandon | 2026
      This scientific paper titled "Safety and feasibility of paired vagus nerve stimulation with rehabilitation for improving upper extremity function in people with cervical spinal cord injury: a pilot randomized controlled trial" aimed to evaluate the safety, feasibility, and therapeutic potential of pairing invasive Vagus Nerve Stimulation (VNS) with upper extremity rehabilitation in individuals with chronic cervical Spinal Cord Injury (SCI). The study demonstrated that the intervention was safe, well-tolerated, and feasible in SCI patients. At immediate post-intervention assessment, all participants receiving active VNS showed improvement in upper extremity motor function compared with baseline, whereas none of the participants in the control group showed improvement. However, these findings should be interpreted cautiously due to the small sample size and exploratory nature of the analysis. The study also found that transportation and access barriers affected recruitment and limited completion of in-clinic sessions, highlighting the need for more accessible and flexible rehabilitation delivery models. Further investigation is required with larger trials to determine the efficacy, durability, and optimal implementation of VNS paired with rehabilitation in spinal cord injury.

    • Test-retest reliability of threshold-tracking TMS and peripheral nerve testing in healthy adults and individuals with chronic cervical spinal cord injury
      Hrycyk, Gramm Kristensen, Tankisi, Spooren, Tedesco Triccas
      The study investigates the test-retest reliability of neuroexcitability measures using threshold-tracking methods in both healthy adults and individuals with chronic cervical spinal cord injury (SCI). It finds excellent relative reliability for TMS measures in both groups, high test-retest reliability for NET using the TROND protocol across all age groups, and the T-T RMT test being more efficient.     • The cellular inflammatory response in human spinal cords after injury

      This study by Fleming et al. (2006) investigates the cellular inflammatory response in human spinal cords after injury, focusing on neutrophils, monocytes/macrophages, lymphocytes, oxidative enzymes, and proteolytic enzymes.     • The influence of intermittent hypercapnia on cerebrospinal fluid flow and clearance in Parkinson’s disease and healthy older adults

      This study investigates the influence of intermittent hypercapnia on cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) flow and clearance in Parkinson's disease (PD) patients and healthy older adults, evaluating if it can stimulate glymphatic clearance, a process that removes waste products from the brain. The study found that intermittent hypercapnia elicited large-amplitude cerebrovascular activity followed by a large-amplitude CSF inflow signal in both groups, but PD patients exhibited significantly reduced CSF inflow response to intermittent hypercapnia compared to healthy controls.       Hayes, Mendenhall, Brush, Del Negro | 2008
      The 2008 study by Hayes et al. investigates the role of a transient outward potassium current, IA, in shaping inspiratory bursts during respiratory activity within the preBötzinger complex (preBötC) in neonatal mice. The researchers found that more than half of inspiratory neurons express IA and that its maximum conductance positively correlates with membrane capacitance and negatively with resistance, with a time constant of inactivation between 20-80ms. 4-aminopyridine (4-AP), an IA blocker, was used to study its role in preBc neurons and caused disorganized XII output and noisy preBötC rhythms that fluctuate in amplitude and period, suggesting that IA plays a crucial role in the regularity of the cycle period and drive potential magnitude in preBötC neurons. The study proposes that further research is needed to clarify the specific contributions of different Kv subunits to preBötC IA and its functional significance in respiration.

      The scientific paper explores the neuronal architecture responsible for autonomic dysreflexia (AD) in mice with spinal cord injury, focusing on maladaptive connections between lower lumbosacral and lower thoracic spinal cord after SCI.     • A projection atlas of excitatory and inhibitory inputs to the preB€otzinger complex: substrates for multimodal breathing control
      Reilly, Kadri, Arthurs, Sedano, Brezinski, Baertsch | 2021
      The research "A Projection Atlas of Excitatory and Inhibitory Inputs to the preBötzinger Complex" reveals that the preBötzinger complex, a critical region for rhythmic breathing in the brain, is modulated by diverse long-range excitatory and inhibitory inputs from various brain regions. The study identifies multiple sources of excitatory inputs converging on the preBötzinger complex, such as the caudal and rostral medullary regions, the rostral ventral respiratory group (rVRG), the lateral parafacial region/retrotrapezoid nucleus (PFl/RTN), and the spinal trigeminal nucleus caudalis (Sp5c). Inhibitory projections were predominantly ipsilateral to the injection site in the caudal medulla, while excitatory projections were found in the contralateral cVRG. This study provides a foundation for understanding the neural mechanisms underlying breathing regulation and could potentially lead to new treatments for respiratory disorders and sleep apnea.
      Keywords: breathing, inputs, preB€otzinger complex, projections, respiratory circuits

    • Advances in the Regulation of Neural Function by Infrared Light
      Song, Wang, Peng | 2024
      The research paper "Advances in the Regulation of Neural Function by Infrared Light" investigates the potential use of infrared light as a replacement for traditional neural regulation methods due to its high spatial resolution, safety, and neural sensitivity.     • Amino acid modulation of carotid body function
      Gold, Pauza, Felippe, Shen, Paton | 2026
      This scientific paper published in The Journal of Physiology investigates the role of amino acids, particularly glutamate, in modulating carotid body function for arterial chemoreception and respiratory regulation. The study finds that during hypoxia, glutamate stimulates postsynaptic petrosal neurons and adjacent glomus cells, while GABA inhibits excitation of neighboring glomus cells, creating an 'accelerator-brake' mechanism that regulates the sensitivity of the carotid body to hypoxia. The study suggests that amino acids such as glutamate and GABA play significant roles in regulating respiratory control through carotid body function, particularly contributing to long-term adaptations seen after repeated hypoxia, potentially explaining heightened chemoreflex sensitivity observed in human conditions involving intermittent hypoxia, such as sleep apnea. The study has experimental limitations, including using potassium cyanide instead of physiological hypoxia and relying on pharmacological approaches to investigate glutamatergic and GABAergic signaling.

    • Astrocytes: Orchestrators of brain gas exchange and oxygen homeostasis
      Christie | 2026
      Astrocytes play a critical role in brain oxygen sensing, cerebral perfusion, and cognition, with a higher sensitivity to changes in oxygen availability compared to glomus cells. They respond to hypoxia by releasing nitric oxide, regulating sympathetic drive, and cardiac output to maintain brain oxygenation. However, during ischemia, astrocytes can both protect neurons and exacerbate brain damage due to their involvement in neurotransmitter release, calcium signaling, and gap junction communication. Future research should explore therapeutic interventions targeting astrocytic functions for improving neurological disorders related to oxygen homeostasis, cognitive impairment, and dementia.

    • BDNF is necessary and sufficient for spinal respiratory plasticity following intermittent hypoxia
      Baker-Herman, Fuller, Bavis, Zabka, Golder, Doperalski, Johnson, Watters, Mitchell | 2003
      In the study, researchers found that brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) plays a crucial role in long-lasting phrenic facilitation (pLTF), a model of respiratory plasticity induced by intermittent hypoxia. The process involves serotonin-dependent BDNF synthesis near phrenic motoneurons, and BDNF administration elicits pLTF without hypoxia. Endogenous BDNF synthesis is necessary for pLTF, and the effect requires activation of a high affinity TrkB receptor. The findings could have implications for conditions such as Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS), obstructive sleep apnea, and respiratory failure following spinal cord injury.

      The study published in Nature Neuroscience investigates the motion of the dorsal cortex within the skull of awake mice, revealing that brain motion is primarily driven by locomotion rather than respiration or the cardiac cycle. Researchers found that forces generated by abdominal muscle contractions reach the brain via the vertebral venous plexus (VVP), a network of valveless veins connecting the abdomen and spinal canal, suggesting a potential hydraulic system driving cerebrospinal fluid flow.       The study by Zhou et al. discovers that a pro-inflammatory microglial subtype (C5aR1+) amplifies neuroinflammation, promotes harmful astrocyte polarization, and enhances neutrophil recruitment after traumatic brain injury (TBI) or intracerebral hemorrhage (ICH), exacerbating cerebral edema and neurological deficits.     • Challenging conventional wisdom: A fresh look at the litter effect in rats
      Huxtable, Flanigan, Frazure, Wollman, Sun, Pandey, Restifo, Fregosi | 2026
      The scientific paper led by Dr. Adrianne G. Huxtable challenges the traditional belief that physiological variance among neonatal Sprague–Dawley rat litters is greater between litters than within litters. Using intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC) analysis, the study found that a significant portion of total variation in body weight and breathing frequency originated within litters rather than between them. The findings suggest a need for more accurate experimental designs, taking into account specific research questions, outcome variables, rat genetics, raw variance both within and between litters, and minimizing animal use in research. The authors propose using multiple pups per litter and applying mixed-model ANOVA with litter as a random effect for more reliable results. However, limitations include the study's applicability to other lab rodents and small sample sizes in some cohorts.

      The scientific paper investigates the complex interplay between various organs and the brain, revealing how conditions like heart failure, non-alcoholic fatty liver disease, and splenectomy can lead to cognitive decline or neurological diseases such as Alzheimer's disease. Conversely, brain disorders affect peripheral organ functions. The study emphasizes the potential benefits of physical exercise in improving these organ-brain interactions and managing neurological disorders like AD and depression by engaging neural and endocrine pathways, regulating lipid metabolism, and secreting bioactive factors like lactate that promote neuroplasticity and cognitive function.     • Expert-level probabilistic breathing event detector informs phenotyping of sleep apnea
      Kjaer, Hanif, Brink-Kjaer, Olsen, Sum-Ping, Carrillo, Sands, Redline, Stone, Jennum, Mignot | 2026
      This study introduces an expert-level probabilistic breathing event detector (ABED), a deep learning model trained on over 5000 polysomnographies, to enhance sleep apnea phenotyping by providing accurate identification of various types of sleep-disordered breathing events and offering insights into their etiology. The model can outperform human experts in large-scale studies and shows potential benefits for increased diagnosis rates and more consistent scoring of apnea events.     • Finding the Groove in Neural Space
      Bhatt, Sheets, Jordan, Downey, Merchant, Greenspon | 2026
      The study "Finding the Groove in Neural Space" investigates how the human brain processes rhythmic patterns by examining neural signatures during rhythmic tapping tasks in two participants with spinal cord injuries. Findings suggest that motor cortex encodes position, velocity, and tempo-dependent differences simultaneously and a continuous tempo axis exists in neural space. The research reveals a continuum of kinematic strategies during rhythmic tapping tasks, indicating shifts from reactive to anticipatory timing mechanisms at lower and higher tempos respectively. The study has implications for developing more effective brain-computer interfaces (BCIs) and understanding human brain function.
    • Funding Distributions, Trends, Gaps, and Policy Implications for Spinal Cord Injury Research: A Systematic Analysis of U.S. Federal Funds
      Tucker Gillespie, Andrew Buxton, Bethany R. Kondiles, Miranda Leal-Garcia, Mia R. Pacheco, Ashley V. Tran, Katie Vo, Lucy Abu, James Barr, Tanya A. Barretto, Jason Biundo, Sam Duenwald, Abigail Evans, Timothy N. Friedman, Isabella Gadaleta, Saahas Ganesh, Bryson Gottschall, Peyton Green, Grant Lee, Lilian Liu, Raza N. Malik, Elizabeth J. Nava, Chiara Sorani, Vansh Tandon, Hannah Thomas, Kyndal Thomas, Chris Barr, Ian Burkhart, Dylan A. McCreedy, Peter Nowell, Heath Blackmon, Alexander G. Rabchevsky, Matthew Rodreick, Abel Torres-Espín, Jennifer N. Dulin | 2023
      The scholarly paper "Funding Distributions, Trends, Gaps, and Policy Implications for Spinal Cord Injury Research" analyzes U.S. federal funding for spinal cord injury (SCI) research between 1976 and 2021 to identify trends, distributions, gaps, and policy implications. The study found that the National Institutes of Health primarily supports basic and early-stage translational research, while the Department of Defense's CDMRP Spinal Cord Injury Research Program concentrates on applied and clinical research, particularly in pain, bladder function, and neuromodulatory device development. The Department of Veterans Affairs invests in rehabilitation-focused studies aimed at improving musculoskeletal and functional health outcomes. Critical gaps in funding were identified for high-priority areas like bowel/gastrointestinal health, cardiovascular function, and mental health. To address these issues, the authors propose developing a centralized, living research dashboard, forming working groups consisting of researchers, program staff, industry representatives, and individuals with lived SCI experience, improving standardization and transparency in grant award labeling and classification, and creating dynamic, publicly accessible platforms to track federally funded SCI research across agencies.
    • GABAA receptor mediated facilitation of proprioceptive afferent transmission to motoneurons is facilitated by TRPM8 channels on C-fibres that are gated by serotonin
      Black, Li, Hari, Lin, Stephens, Fouad, Fenrich, Lucas-Osma, Gorassini, Bennett | 2026
      The study conducted at the University of Alberta investigates how serotonin modulates sensory transmission within the monosynaptic stretch reflex from proprioceptive Ia afferents to motoneurons, using zolmitriptan as an example. It finds that serotonin, via its main receptor subtype 5-HT1D on nociceptive C-fibers, induces a long-lasting tonic depolarization in Ia afferents through increased GABAergic input onto them, promoting sodium spike initiation. Activation of 5-HT1D receptors with zolmitriptan inhibits C-fiber activity and reduces the GABAA receptor-mediated tonic depolarization on Ia afferents, decreasing spike propagation to motoneurons. The research suggests that brainstem-derived serotonin not only inhibits ongoing nociceptive C-fibers activity but also reduces GABAergic neuron activity and sensory transmission in large proprioceptive afferents, leading to decreased motoneuron activity. This could have implications for pain studies and challenges traditional concepts of sensory gating.
    • GABAergic Hub Neurons Orchestrate Synchrony in Developing Hippocampal Networks
      Bonifazi, Goldin, Picardo, Jorquera, Cattani, Bianconi, Represa, Ben-Ari, Cossart
      The scientific paper investigates the significant role of high connectivity GABAergic interneurons, or hub neurons, in regulating network dynamics during hippocampus development, particularly their impact on Giant Depolarizing Potentials (GDPs). Key findings show that a third of targeted hub neurons significantly affect network dynamics compared to minimal impact from low connectivity neurons. Hub neurons are predominantly found in specific layers like the stratum oriens and lucidum, and activation of these neurons before synchrony contributes to network synchronization in the developing CA3 region. The study also highlights differences between hub and low connectivity interneurons in terms of electrophysiological properties and references notable authors' works to provide insights into physiological and pathological network oscillations.
    • GABAergic neurons in the ventrolateral periaqueductal gray mediate fentanyl withdrawal and self-administration in mice
      Chen1, Cheng2, Cao1, Xiao1, Chubykin2,3,4,5, Kimbrough1,4,5,6 | 2026
      This study investigates the role of GABAergic neurons in the ventrolateral periaqueductal gray (vlPAG) during fentanyl withdrawal and self-administration in mice, revealing an increased activity and enhanced inhibitory synaptic transmission within this region during fentanyl use and withdrawal. Chemogenetic inhibition of these neurons alleviated withdrawal-induced hyperalgesia in male mice, while no comparable effect was observed in female mice.     • Galvanin (TMEM154) is an electric-field sensor for directed cell migration
      Belliveau, Footer, Platenkamp, Rodriguez, Kim, Prinz, van Loon, Lin, Eustis, Chan, Cohen, Theriot | 2026
      The research published in Cell (2026) by Belliveau et al. identifies Galvanin as a crucial electric-field sensor essential for directed migration in various rapidly moving cells such as immune and epithelial cells. Upon exposure to electric fields, Galvanin localizes at the front and rear of cells, changing spatial patterns, exhibiting an exponential decay with a net charge of -18e, and regulating subsequent movement responses. The study suggests that a highly negatively charged construct can rescue directed migration in knockout cell lines, indicating a high net negative ectodomain charge is sufficient for Galvanin-mediated electrotaxis. Further studies are needed to understand the molecular mechanisms underlying Galvanin's role in electrotaxis and its implications for cell signaling and other biological processes.

    • Hyperglycosylation is a metabolic driver of Alzheimer’s disease
      Liu, Ribas, Medina, Nielsen, Clarke, Ma, Mueller, Plasencia, Sheer, Simpson, Soto, Sudderth, Cai, Cantrell, Colpaert, Shedlock, Wu, Young, Kooser, Chen, Huang, He, Alonge, Guo, Bian, Vander Kooi, Gentry, Sun, Quinones, Son, Azadi, Deberardinis, Prokop, Allison, Yang, Chen, Huang, He, Guo, Gao, Kooi, Gentry, & Sun | 2026
      This Nature Metabolism study investigates the role of hyperglycosylation, specifically N-glycosylation, in Alzheimer's disease (AD). The research suggests that excessive N-glycosylation contributes to the conserved phenotype of brain hyperglycosylation in AD, and reducing this excess improves social recognition memory in AD mouse models.     • Intermittent Hypoxia Induces Reversible Epigenetic Age Acceleration in Old Mice

      The study reveals that brief periods of low oxygen exposure (intermittent hypoxia) can make cells appear older in old mice but this effect is reversible.     • MINI-REVIEW Opioids and Respiratory Depression: The role of carotid bodies in opioid-induced respiratory depression
      Spiller, Pedro F., Amorim, Mateus R., Polotsky, Vsevolod Y. | 2023
      This mini-review discusses Carotid Bodies as a potential target to mitigate Opioid-Induced Respiratory Depression, a significant public health concern in the U.S., causing over 130 deaths daily. The review suggests that activating opioid receptors (μ, d, or κ) in Carotid Bodies may protect against respiratory depression and reduce its effects without compromising analgesic properties of opioids. However, further research is required to confirm the presence of κ opioid receptors in human Carotid Bodies and to evaluate the effectiveness of κ agonists in blocking OIRD with higher doses of fentanyl.     • MINIREVIEW Intermittent Hypoxia: Cause of or Therapy for Systemic Hypertension?
      Serebrovskaya, Manukhina, Smith, Downey, Mallet | 2008
      This mini-review discusses the conflicting effects of Intermittent Hypoxia (IH) on systemic hypertension, particularly its potential as a treatment due to its ability to lower blood pressure in individuals with hypertension. The review highlights research conducted primarily in the former Soviet Union, focusing on mechanisms such as enhanced sarcoplasmic reticular Ca2+ sequestration and increased antioxidant defenses that contribute to IH's effects on preventing endothelial dysfunction and high blood pressure in rats with various forms of experimental hypertension. The article also distinguishes Intermittent Hypoxia Treatment (IHT) from Obstructive Sleep Apnea, emphasizing IHT's potential benefits over traditional drug therapies due to its minimal side effects and possible stimulation of an organism's general resistance. The paper also provides references related to nitric oxide, intermittent hypoxia, and cardiovascular physiology.

    • Molecular mechanism of NMDA receptor potentiation by lactate
      Fiumelli, Herrera-López, Lemtiri-Chlieh, Mottier, Girgis, Ben-Adiba, Jourdain, Carrano, Mahmood, Ooi, Arold, Di Luca, Gardoni, Magistretti | 2026
      The research study published in The Journal of Physiology in 2026 investigates the role of lactate produced by astrocytes in enhancing neuronal NMDA receptor function, synaptic plasticity, and memory formation via a redox mechanism. This process involves lactate entering neurons through monocarboxylate transporters (MCTs), being oxidized to pyruvate, and generating NADH, which increases the intracellular NADH/NAD+ ratio. This redox shift promotes calcium release from ryanodine-sensitive stores (RyR) and recruits Ca2+/calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II (CaMKII) to GluN2B-containing NMDARs, leading to enhanced NMDAR currents and prolonged decay kinetics. This strengthened NMDAR–CaMKII signaling increases NMDAR presence at postsynaptic sites, facilitating the molecular processes underlying long-term synaptic plasticity and memory formation. The study was conducted by a team from various institutions and reveals a molecular pathway linking brain metabolism with learning and memory processes.

      Keywords: Licox, microdialysis, monitoring, perfusion pressure, spinal cord injury, tissue oxygen

    • Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation
      Balaguer, Prat-Ortega, Ostrowski, Borda, Verma, Yadav, Sorensen, de Freitas, Ensel, Donadio, Liang, Ho, Damiani, Grigsby, Fields, Gonzalez-Martinez, Gerszten, Fisher, Weber, Pirondini, Capogrosso | 2025
      In the study "Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation," Balaguer et al. (2025) reveal that residual brain inputs do not facilitate SCS-mediated motor control but rather transform subthreshold SCS-generated excitatory postsynaptic potentials into action potentials, enabling brain regulation and volitional rate control during spinal cord stimulation in individuals with spinal cord injury and stroke. The study also highlights the limitation of SCS in severe injury cases due to a restricted set of functional stimulation parameters, while demonstrating fine control over force output by participants and improving the ability to modulate motor neuron firing rates through incorporating supraspinal inhibitory drive.
    • Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation
      Balaguer, Prat-Ortega, Ostrowski, Borda, Verma, Yadav, Sorensen, de Freitas, Ensel, Donadio, Liang, Ho, Damiani, Grigsby, Fields, Gonzalez-Martinez, Gerszten, Fisher, Weber, Pirondini, Capogrosso | 2025
      In the study 'Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation' (Balaguer et al., 2025), researchers discovered that residual brain inputs modulate motoneuron membrane potentials, allowing subthreshold SCS pulses to transform into action potentials and entrain motoneuron activity to the stimulation. The study found that only a subset of SCS parameters enables volitional motor control, and this functional parameter space shrinks with lesion severity. The findings have potential implications for treating neurological disorders like spinal cord injuries, where SCS could help restore motor function by allowing participants to leverage residual supraspinal inhibition to control motoneuron firing rate. However, the study's limitations include focusing on immediate SCS effects and not accounting for long-term changes due to physical training.
    • Neurokinin receptor-expressing pre-Bo¨tzinger complex neurons in neonatal mice studied in vitro

    • Neuromodulation for the treatment of moderate to severe asthma – a pilot first-in-human clinical study
      Gehlsen, Carrasco, Hooghius, Pyles | 2025
      The July 2025 study published in the Journal of Asthma presents two pilot clinical trials investigating neuromodulation as a potential treatment for moderate to severe asthma. One trial used a non-invasive device to stimulate the vagus nerve, showing significant improvements in pulmonary function tests and asthma symptom scores after six weeks for 10 participants. The other study involved SCS implantation in 11 patients with chronic pain, resulting in complete elimination of asthma symptoms within days, improved asthma control, reduced medication usage, and enhanced quality of life. Future research will focus on larger controlled clinical studies to further establish the safety and efficacy of these neuromodulation techniques as treatment options for asthma patients.
    • Noradrenergic neuromodulation produces a NMDAR-dependent network state of respiratory rhythmogenesis in the preBötzinger Complex
      Tryba, Viemari, Wang, Garcia | 2026
      This preprint study investigates the effects of Norepinephrine (NE) and Nitric Oxide (NO) on the preBotanic Complex (preBötC), a critical brainstem region for respiratory rhythm generation, in rodents. The research finds that NE increases action potential frequency while decreasing the average action potential number during network bursts, and NO attenuates synchrony by activating soluble guanylyl cyclase and cGMP-dependent protein kinase (PKG). Combined application of NA and NO leads to complex interactions with overall effects dependent on their relative concentrations. The study suggests potential roles for NE and NO modulation in abnormal respiratory patterns associated with chronic hypoxia, offering insights for new therapeutic strategies for treating hypoxia-related respiratory disorders. However, further peer review is necessary as this is a preprint.

    • Potentiation of active locomotor state by spinal-projecting serotonergic neurons
      Fenstermacher, Vonasek, Cavanagh, Gattuso, Chaimowitz, Jessell, Dymecki, Dasen | 2025
      The scientific paper by Fenstermacher et al. investigates the roles of spinal-projecting serotonergic (5-HT) neurons in locomotor behavior using optogenetic techniques on mice. Activation of these neurons increases the speed and duration of ongoing locomotion, with key findings showing an increase in individual run bouts' length, fewer overall run bouts, and a shift towards faster speeds during locomotion. The research has potential implications for understanding the function of serotonin in motor behavior and developing innovative approaches to control locomotion in neuroprosthetics.

    • Pulmonary and carotid body afferents in OIRD

    • Regulation of sleep homeostasis mediator adenosine by basal forebrain glutamatergic neurons
      Peng*, Wu*, Song*, Zhang, Li, Xu†
      The research paper "GRABAdo1.0: A Novel Sensor for Extracellular Adenosine Detection and Its Role in Sleep Homeostasis" presents a study on glutamatergic neurons in the basal forebrain (BF), demonstrating their role as key regulators of sleep homeostasis through adenosine release. The study found higher adenosine levels during wakefulness and REM sleep compared to NREM sleep, and revealed a correlation between calcium activity in BF cholinergic neurons and adenosine levels. Optogenetic activation increased extracellular adenosine levels, while lesioning these glutamatergic neurons resulted in increased wakefulness during the active period, less recovery sleep after prolonged wakefulness, and impaired sleep rebound. The 'ion (38)' experiment showed statistically significant differences in NREM sleep time between the lesion group and control group. The discussion section reviews existing studies on sleep regulation and neural activity in the basal forebrain, confirming adenosine's role in mediating homeostatic control of sleep in cholinergic BF neurons, and contributing new insights into how neural activity during wakefulness contributes to sleep pressure through the release of sleep-inducing factors by BF glutamatergic neurons.
    • Resistance training-induced adaptations in the neuromuscular system: Physiological mechanisms and implications for human performance
      Lecce, Amoruso, Felici, Bazzucchi | 2026
      This topical review published in The Journal of Physiology by Edoardo Lecce's team from the University of Rome 'Foro Italico' explores neuromuscular adaptations resulting from resistance training (RT). They find increased excitability, improved synaptic input transmission, and decreased synaptic noise in the brain and spinal cord, leading to a more stable neural drive. At the spinal level, intrinsic motoneuron excitability, neuromodulatory input, and spinal circuitry are modified, resulting in enhanced motor unit (MU) excitability due to attenuated inhibitory pathways. Skeletal muscle adaptations involve contractile protein synthesis, structural repair by satellite cells, and endurance capacity improvement through mitochondrial biogenesis and capillarization. These changes lead to increased strength, improved physical performance, and alterations in muscle fiber type composition, energy metabolism, glucose uptake, and hormone levels, with practical applications for healthy individuals and those affected by chronic disorders.
    • Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance
      Kipnis, Benveniste, Eichmann, Thomas, Reich, Lewis, Tsai, Drieu, Bakker, Kelley, Lundgaard, Mestre, Zott, Eide, van Osch, van Veluw, Iliff, Yang, Santambrogio, Da Mesquita, Daneman, Rustenhoven, Goldman, Nedergaard
      The scientific paper "Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance" aims to advance our understanding of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) dynamics in brain waste clearance, focusing on periarterial spaces, glymphatic and meningeal lymphatic systems, their functional connections, and immune cell involvement. Key findings reveal primary CSF influx along periarterial spaces, solute clearance through multiple routes, and functional influence by vascular pulsation, neural activity, sleep, and immunity.     • Respiratory control dysfunction and vulnerability in Parkinson’s disease
      Macedo, Matos, Damasceno-Silva, Oliveira, Franco, Ferreira, Cabral, Aquino, Moreira, Takakura | 2021
      The study "Respiratory Control in Parkinson's Disease" by Tainá O. Macedo et al., funded by research grants, investigates how Parkinson's disease affects central neural circuits like the RTN and preBötC, causing oxidative stress, neuroinflammation, mitochondrial dysfunction, and neuronal vulnerability. Peripherally, PD impacts sleep architecture, altering rapid eye movement (REM) and non-REM sleep stages through glutamatergic neurons in the PPN. Respiratory dysfunction, a common but underdiagnosed feature of PD, is observed in decreased inspiratory drive, increased expiratory flow limitation, and changes in response to hypercapnia and hypoxia. Pharmacological interventions have been explored for restoring respiratory function, while the authors recommend standardizing methods for assessing respiratory function in clinical settings and designing clinical trials targeting respiratory outcomes. The study provides a list of references related to sleep, arousal systems, cardiorespiratory regulation, Parkinson's disease, and central chemoreceptors.
      Keywords: Respiratory control, chemosensitivity, Parkinson’s disease, brainstem circuits
    • Respiratory modulation of neurophysiology and symptoms in athletes with sports-related concussion: a randomized crossover trial

    • Review on Extracellular Spike Waveforms: Morphology, Biophysics, and Classification Strategies
      Shi Hai Sun, Ibbotson
      This scientific paper focuses on the exploration of extracellular spike waveforms and their classifications in neuroscience research, emphasizing their importance in understanding neuron morphology, connectivity, and identity.     • Review: Cell-type-specific APOE4 cascade across the Alzheimer’s disease continuum
      Shostak, Liang, Huang | 2026
      The review "Cell-type-specific APOE4 cascade across the Alzheimer’s disease continuum" highlights Apolipoprotein E4 (APOE4) as the leading genetic risk factor for Alzheimer's disease (AD), causing early neuronal deficits in preclinical AD stages through age-associated stress. This neuronal damage triggers a harmful glial response, amplifying neurodegeneration and accelerating the onset of prodromal and dementia AD stages. The proposed model suggests potential therapeutic targets like HMGB1 and MHC class I for treating neuroinflammation but emphasizes the need for further investigation into their broader physiological impact and safety, with the ultimate goal of preventing APOE4's harmful effects on the aging brain before cognitive decline begins.
    • Review: Neurologic mechanisms regulating cough in health and disease
      Mazzone, Pflaumer, Richards | 2026
      The 2026 review "Neurologic mechanisms regulating cough in health and disease" published by the American College of Allergy, Asthma & Immunology explores the neurology behind both acute and chronic cough, focusing on vagal sensory neurons and brainstem circuits. The study suggests that chronic cough is associated with peripheral sensory nerve fiber sensitivity and central neural mechanisms amplification, leading to excessive coughing in response to low levels of thermal, mechanical, or chemical exposure. Brain imaging studies suggest chronic cough may be a disorder of reduced inhibitory control, and therapies restoring sensorimotor balance could potentially control excessive cough. The review highlights the clinical significance of chronic cough and significant therapeutic advances in recent years for managing it, though uniformity in responsivity to trialed drugs indicates the need for therapies directed at specific endotypic mechanisms or responsive treatable traits for effective cough management. Funding was provided by the National Health and Medical Research Council of Australia, with some authors receiving grants and consulting fees from various pharmaceutical companies.

    • Safety and feasibility of paired vagus nerve stimulation with rehabilitation for improving upper extremity function in people with cervical spinal cord injury: a pilot randomized controlled trial
      Korupolu, Yozbatiran, Miller, Shields, Tandon | 2026
      This scientific paper titled "Safety and feasibility of paired vagus nerve stimulation with rehabilitation for improving upper extremity function in people with cervical spinal cord injury: a pilot randomized controlled trial" aimed to evaluate the safety, feasibility, and therapeutic potential of pairing invasive Vagus Nerve Stimulation (VNS) with upper extremity rehabilitation in individuals with chronic cervical Spinal Cord Injury (SCI). The study demonstrated that the intervention was safe, well-tolerated, and feasible in SCI patients. At immediate post-intervention assessment, all participants receiving active VNS showed improvement in upper extremity motor function compared with baseline, whereas none of the participants in the control group showed improvement. However, these findings should be interpreted cautiously due to the small sample size and exploratory nature of the analysis. The study also found that transportation and access barriers affected recruitment and limited completion of in-clinic sessions, highlighting the need for more accessible and flexible rehabilitation delivery models. Further investigation is required with larger trials to determine the efficacy, durability, and optimal implementation of VNS paired with rehabilitation in spinal cord injury.

    • Test-retest reliability of threshold-tracking TMS and peripheral nerve testing in healthy adults and individuals with chronic cervical spinal cord injury
      Hrycyk, Gramm Kristensen, Tankisi, Spooren, Tedesco Triccas
      The study investigates the test-retest reliability of neuroexcitability measures using threshold-tracking methods in both healthy adults and individuals with chronic cervical spinal cord injury (SCI). It finds excellent relative reliability for TMS measures in both groups, high test-retest reliability for NET using the TROND protocol across all age groups, and the T-T RMT test being more efficient.       This Original Article, published in 2026 in CNS Neuroscience & Therapeutics, investigates the impact of chronic light exposure on anxiety-like behaviors in mice and the role of the rostral ventrolateral medulla (RVLM) input to the central amygdala (CeA).       This scientific paper investigates Nobiletin (NOB), a compound found in citrus peels, as a potential circadian clock amplitude enhancer that combats metabolic syndrome.     • The cellular inflammatory response in human spinal cords after injury
      Fleming, Norenberg, Ramsay, Dekaban, Marcillo, Saenz, Pasquale-Styles, Dietrich, Weaver | 2006
      This study by Fleming et al. (2006) investigates the cellular inflammatory response in human spinal cords after injury, focusing on neutrophils, monocytes/macrophages, lymphocytes, oxidative enzymes, and proteolytic enzymes. The research found that neutrophils dominate immediately after injury but decrease over time, while macrophages increase. Activated microglia express gp91phox and contribute to both neurological recovery and tissue damage. Lymphocytes, particularly CD8+ T-cells, are present among macrophages, with their role in secondary degenerative processes and wound healing unclear. The study highlights the need for limiting neutrophil entry as a potential neuroprotective therapy.

    • The influence of intermittent hypercapnia on cerebrospinal fluid flow and clearance in Parkinson’s disease and healthy older adults

      This study investigates the influence of intermittent hypercapnia on cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) flow and clearance in Parkinson's disease (PD) patients and healthy older adults, evaluating if it can stimulate glymphatic clearance, a process that removes waste products from the brain. The study found that μOR agonist DAMGO increases passive potassium conductance in glutamatergic neurons via membrane-delimited modulation of Kir3 channels. Researchers propose targeting signaling molecules downstream from the μ-opioid receptor and attenuating Gβγ–Kir3 interactions as a potential strategy to reverse OIRD, with promising approaches including gallein, nanobodies, and short peptides that mimic Gβγ-binding sites on Kir3.
      Keywords: μ-Opioid receptor, Kir3 currents, glutamatergic preBötzinger complex

    • A neuronal architecture underlying autonomic dysreflexia
      Soriano, Hudelle, Mahe, Gautier, Teo, Skinnider, Laskaratos, Ceto, Kathe, Hutson, Charbonneau, Girgis, Casha, Rimok, Tso, Larkin-Kaiser, Gandhi, Amir, Kang, Vyza, Martin-Moraud, Lacour, Demesmaeker, Asboth, Barraud, Anderson, Bloch, Squair, Phillips, Courtine | 2025
      The scientific paper explores the neuronal architecture responsible for autonomic dysreflexia (AD) in mice with spinal cord injury, focusing on maladaptive connections between lower lumbosacral and lower thoracic spinal cord after SCI. Key findings suggest vGLUT2ON excitatory neurons are activated during AD, with Vsx2ON being the most transcriptionally responsive subpopulation. The study also discusses an implantable system designed to restore hemodynamic stability in individuals with spinal cord injuries, featuring epidural electrodes, a brain-computer interface, and a closed-loop control system, which showed improved results compared to the control group in preclinical models and one human participant.     • A µ-opioid receptor superagonist analgesic with minimal adverse effects
      Gomez, Ventriglia, Frangos, Sulima, Robertson, Sacco, Budinich, Giosan, Xie, Solis, Tischer, Bossert, Caldwell, Bonbrest, Essmann, Garçon-Poca, Choi, Noya, Limiac, Arce, Glatfelter, Robinson, Chen, Mullarkey, Brademan, Enten, Dunne, Quiroz, Schoenborn, Lee, Rais, Holt, Dannals, Shi, Hüttenhain, Ferré, Kiyatkin, Bonaventura, Shaham, Zachariou, Baumann, Skiniotis, Rice, Michaelides | 2026
      This scientific paper compares FNZ (fentanyl N-benzyl) and its metabolite DFNZ in terms of brain penetration, pharmacokinetics, MOR occupancy, dopamine neurotransmission, and potential for abuse. The research employs various techniques such as Caco-2 permeability assays, molecular docking, cryo-electron microscopy, and behavioral tests to demonstrate that FNZ has higher brain penetration than DFNZ, exhibits stronger MOR occupancy, and causes more potent dopamine neurotransmission effects. The study also highlights the critical role of VTA MOR-GAL1 heteromers in these effects and suggests potential implications for pain management, addiction, and neurological disorders. Further studies are recommended to explore the role of P-glycoprotein in drug transport across the blood-brain barrier.
    • Advances in the Regulation of Neural Function by Infrared Light
      Song, Wang, Peng | 2024
      The research paper "Advances in the Regulation of Neural Function by Infrared Light" investigates the potential use of infrared light as a replacement for traditional neural regulation methods due to its high spatial resolution, safety, and neural sensitivity. A novel image segmentation algorithm (NISA) was employed to analyze medical images against four established techniques, demonstrating superior performance with accuracy of 93.6%, precision of 92.7%, F1-score of 93.2%, and DICE coefficient of 94.0%. However, NISA has slower processing speed and requires advanced hardware for efficient implementation due to its increased computational complexity, but offers a promising solution for disease diagnosis and treatment by enabling precise identification of affected regions in medical imagery.
      Keywords: infrared light, regulation, nerve function, mechanism
    • Artificial hibernation reveals synaptic engram architecture associated with memory retention
      Lin, Takahashi-Nakazato, Tsutsumi, Takahashi, Mercier, Ashitomi, Chiang, Haberl, Uytiepo, Maximov, Makino, Nemoto, Enoki, Hirano, Soga, Looprasertkul, Ohno, Kubota, Sakurai, Tanaka | 2026
      The scientific paper "Artificial Hibernation Reveals Synaptic Engram Architecture Associated with Memory Retention" reveals that memories can remain stable despite widespread synapse loss during hibernation, and identifies clustered synaptic connections among memory-encoding neurons as a structural feature associated with memory retention. A separate study suggests that Quinine-induced hypothermia (QIH) may not significantly affect spatial memory or synaptic plasticity in mice but reveals a unique neuronal architecture spared during QIH.       The review article published in Rev Assoc Med Bras (2023) examines auricular vagus nerve stimulation (aVNS) as a potential novel treatment for managing inflammation related to COVID-19, suggesting it could modulate the immune response and reduce inflammatory markers, with more research required to confirm its efficacy and optimize usage.
    • C5aR1+ microglia exacerbate neuroinflammation and cerebral edema in acute brain injury
      Zhou, Ma, Feng, Tian, Li, Guo, Shi, Cui, Dong, Hao, He, Xiao, Chen, Wu, Gao, Hu, Zhang, Wang, Zhou, Feng, Xue, Lin, Wang, Gao, Heng, Zhao, Luo, Tian, Zhang, Guo, Li, Liu, Ge, Liu, Qu | 2025
      The study by Zhou et al. discovers that a pro-inflammatory microglial subtype (C5aR1+) amplifies neuroinflammation, promotes harmful astrocyte polarization, and enhances neutrophil recruitment after traumatic brain injury (TBI) or intracerebral hemorrhage (ICH), exacerbating cerebral edema and neurological deficits. Pharmacological inhibition of C5aR1 reduces edema in preclinical models, suggesting its potential as a therapeutic target for TBI and ICH. The FDA-approved drug avacopan, a C5aR1 antagonist, shows promise in mitigating these harmful effects by suppressing pro-inflammatory cytokine expression, restoring microglial morphology, inhibiting AKT and ERK phosphorylation, reducing astrocyte polarization, decreasing infiltrated neutrophils, alleviating cerebral edema, and accelerating functional recovery after TBI and ICH.     • Clinical Application of Near Infrared Blood Oxygen Monitoring in Cervical Spinal Cord Injury

    • GABAA receptor mediated facilitation of proprioceptive afferent transmission to motoneurons is facilitated by TRPM8 channels on C-fibres that are gated by serotonin
      Black, Li, Hari, Lin, Stephens, Fouad, Fenrich, Lucas-Osma, Gorassini, Bennett | 2026
      The study conducted at the University of Alberta investigates how serotonin modulates sensory transmission within the monosynaptic stretch reflex from proprioceptive Ia afferents to motoneurons, using zolmitriptan as an example. It finds that serotonin, via its main receptor subtype 5-HT1D on nociceptive C-fibers, induces a long-lasting tonic depolarization in Ia afferents through increased GABAergic input onto them, promoting sodium spike initiation. Activation of 5-HT1D receptors with zolmitriptan inhibits C-fiber activity and reduces the GABAA receptor-mediated tonic depolarization on Ia afferents, decreasing spike propagation to motoneurons. The research suggests that brainstem-derived serotonin not only inhibits ongoing nociceptive C-fibers activity but also reduces GABAergic neuron activity and sensory transmission in large proprioceptive afferents, leading to decreased motoneuron activity. This could have implications for pain studies and challenges traditional concepts of sensory gating.
    • GABAergic neurons in the ventrolateral periaqueductal gray mediate fentanyl withdrawal and self-administration in mice
      Chen1, Cheng2, Cao1, Xiao1, Chubykin2,3,4,5, Kimbrough1,4,5,6 | 2026
      This study investigates the role of GABAergic neurons in the ventrolateral periaqueductal gray (vlPAG) during fentanyl withdrawal and self-administration in mice, revealing an increased activity and enhanced inhibitory synaptic transmission within this region during fentanyl use and withdrawal. Chemogenetic inhibition of these neurons alleviated withdrawal-induced hyperalgesia in male mice, while no comparable effect was observed in female mice. The findings suggest that targeting inhibitory signaling within the vlPAG could potentially alleviate withdrawal-associated hyperalgesia and limit fentanyl use.
    • Hyperglycosylation is a metabolic driver of Alzheimer’s disease
      Liu, Ribas, Medina, Nielsen, Clarke, Ma, Mueller, Plasencia, Sheer, Simpson, Soto, Sudderth, Cai, Cantrell, Colpaert, Shedlock, Wu, Young, Kooser, Chen, Huang, He, Alonge, Guo, Bian, Vander Kooi, Gentry, Sun, Quinones, Son, Azadi, Deberardinis, Prokop, Allison, Yang, Chen, Huang, He, Guo, Gao, Kooi, Gentry, & Sun | 2026
      This Nature Metabolism study investigates the role of hyperglycosylation, specifically N-glycosylation, in Alzheimer's disease (AD). The research suggests that excessive N-glycosylation contributes to the conserved phenotype of brain hyperglycosylation in AD, and reducing this excess improves social recognition memory in AD mouse models. Genetic knockdown of glycan biosynthetic enzymes showed improved cognitive outcomes, while glucosamine supplementation worsened behavior in these mice. A retrospective analysis of electronic health records revealed that glucosamine supplementation was associated with accelerated AD progression and reduced survival in patients with mild cognitive impairment (MCI) and dementia, highlighting the need for further investigation into cell-type-specific spatial information and pharmacological interventions targeting the hexosamine pathway in animal models.

      Arakawa, Hiroyuki, Arakawa, Keiko, Levitt, Erica S. | Not specified
      This scientific paper by Arakawa et al. investigates the role of hypothalamic orexin (ORX) and glutamatergic neurons in counteracting fentanyl-induced respiratory depression (FIRD) in mice, focusing on the lateral hypothalamus (LH) and paraventricular nucleus (PVN). The study found that LH-ORX/glutamatergic projections to the medulla drive respiratory activity, reducing FIRD. Activation of PVN glutamatergic neurons had minimal effects on respiratory parameters.     • In vivo multiplex imaging of dynamic neurochemical networks with designed far-­red dopamine sensors
      Zheng, Cai, Wang, Zhang, Zhuo, Dong, Zhang, Wang, Deng, Ji, Cui, Fang, Xinzhang, Huang, Kecheng Zhang, Jinxu Wang, Grimm, Johnsson, Schreiter, Lavis, Chen, Mu, Li
      The scientific paper presents HaloDA1.0, a new genetically encoded sensor for real-time monitoring of dopamine dynamics in various biological systems. It demonstrates rapid response to DA release and potential for multi-color imaging, reducing autofluorescence interference. In vivo experiments reveal simultaneous monitoring of DA, acetylcholine, and cyclic adenosine monophosphate dynamics in the mouse nucleus accumbens, and the effects of addictive drugs like cocaine on this regulatory mechanism are explored.     • Intracellular calcium release activates carotid body afferents by fentanyl
      Peng, Kabil, Wang, Xie, Fox, Prabhakar | 2025
      The research reveals that fentanyl activates carotid body afferents through a KOR-Gaq-PLC-IP3R-Ca2+ signaling pathway, which increases intracellular calcium levels in glomus cells and counteracts opioid-induced respiratory depression. This newly discovered mechanism may help reduce opioid-related respiratory complications and enhance opioid safety.

    • MINI-REVIEW Opioids and Respiratory Depression: The role of carotid bodies in opioid-induced respiratory depression
      Spiller, Pedro F., Amorim, Mateus R., Polotsky, Vsevolod Y. | 2023
      This mini-review discusses Carotid Bodies as a potential target to mitigate Opioid-Induced Respiratory Depression, a significant public health concern in the U.S., causing over 130 deaths daily. The review suggests that activating opioid receptors (μ, d, or κ) in Carotid Bodies may protect against respiratory depression and reduce its effects without compromising analgesic properties of opioids. However, further research is required to confirm the presence of κ opioid receptors in human Carotid Bodies and to evaluate the effectiveness of κ agonists in blocking OIRD with higher doses of fentanyl. The authors propose that activating Carotid Bodies may offer a therapeutic intervention for Opioid-Induced Respiratory Depression, minimizing risks associated with respiratory depression while preserving opioids' analgesic benefits.
      Keywords: carotid body, opioid receptor, respiratory depression
    • Mu opioid receptors gate the locus coeruleus pain generator

      The study by Kuo et al. identifies the locus coeruleus as a key player in chronic pain regulation, transforming from an endogenous analgesia source to a pain generator following nerve injury. Mu opioid receptors (MOR) on distinct projections within the LC were found to robustly inhibit it and drive antinociception. The research suggests that restoring LC opioid signaling could potentially reverse neuropathic hypersensitivity, offering potential therapeutic targets for pain management, although with limitations such as global NE-conditional MOR knockout and focus on specific time points post-neuropathic injury.

    • Protocol for head-restrained drug self-administration in mice
      Doncheck, Clarke, Gordon, Boquiren, Manusky, Baek, Paniccia, Dinu, Dereschewitz, Grant, Stuber, Otis | 2026
      This protocol article describes a low-cost, standardized method for training head-fixed mice to self-administer drugs using 3D-printed equipment and open-source software (REACHER/Labrynth). The aim is to facilitate research on drug reward and motivation in rodents using head fixation systems. Key steps include habituating mice, training nose poke behavior, validating self-administration procedures, and implementing experimental manipulations like modulating drug doses or reward schedules.     • Pulmonary and carotid body afferents in OIRD
      Peng, Ramirez, Prabhakar | 2026
      The scientific paper "Pulmonary and carotid body afferents in Opioid-Induced Respiratory Depression (OIRD)" investigates the effects of opioids on various bodily systems, particularly the respiratory system, through a review of previous studies and experiments on rodent models.     • Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance
      Kipnis, Benveniste, Eichmann, Thomas, Reich, Lewis, Tsai, Drieu, Bakker, Kelley, Lundgaard, Mestre, Zott, Eide, van Osch, van Veluw, Iliff, Yang, Santambrogio, Da Mesquita, Daneman, Rustenhoven, Goldman, Nedergaard
      The scientific paper "Resolving the mysteries of brain clearance and immune surveillance" aims to advance our understanding of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) dynamics in brain waste clearance, focusing on periarterial spaces, glymphatic and meningeal lymphatic systems, their functional connections, and immune cell involvement. Key findings reveal primary CSF influx along periarterial spaces, solute clearance through multiple routes, and functional influence by vascular pulsation, neural activity, sleep, and immunity. Horner, explores the role of cholinergic neurons in controlling tongue movements during sleep using a combined optogenetics (optical manipulation of neurons) and dialysis (substance removal from living organisms) approach.     • The Rostral Ventrolateral Medulla Input to Central Amygdala Regulates Anxiety-­Like Behaviors in Mice for Chronic Light Exposure
      Jing, Tan, Xu, Yuan, Fan, Wang | 2026
      This Original Article, published in 2026 in CNS Neuroscience & Therapeutics, investigates the impact of chronic light exposure on anxiety-like behaviors in mice and the role of the rostral ventrolateral medulla (RVLM) input to the central amygdala (CeA). The study reveals that chronic light exposure enhances anxiety-like behaviors, which are regulated by the RVLM input to the CeA, suggesting a potential novel approach for treating anxiety disorders under conditions of prolonged light exposure. However, the research was limited by not addressing physical or pathological disconnection effects under light exposure conditions, being conducted exclusively in male mice, and not publicly releasing the data due to privacy or ethical restrictions. Future studies are necessary to validate the RVLM→CeA bidirectional feedback loop hypothesis and determine whether findings generalize to female subjects.
      Keywords: anxiety-­like behaviors, blood pressure, central amygdala, chronic light exposure, rostral ventrolateral medulla

    • Xylazine exacerbates fentanyl-induced respiratory depression and bradycardia
      Demery, Moore, Levitt, Anand, Traynor | 2025
      This research explores the combined impact of fentanyl and xylazine on respiratory measures in mice, finding that the combination significantly reduces breathing rate more than fentanyl alone, with distinct effects on inspiration and expiration flow rates. Fentanyl decreases blood oxygen saturation and heart rate, while xylazine has less impact but exacerbates fentanyl's effects. The study suggests potential implications for anesthesia and sedation in mice, offering insights into mechanisms underlying xylazine's contribution to opioid overdoses in humans; however, these findings may not directly apply to humans due to the study being conducted on mice.
      Keywords: Opioid overdose, Plethysmography, Fentanyl, Xylazine, Respiratory depression

    • A Review of Pharmacological Therapies for Obstructive Sleep Apnea and Impact on Cardiovascular Outcomes
      Robinson, De Sarkar, Moore, Malhotra | 2026
      The scientific paper advocates for a personalized approach to treating Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA), focusing on six potential pharmacological strategies including enhancing upper airway dilator muscle activity, stabilizing breathing control, blocking cardiovascular consequences, addressing symptoms, promoting CPAP adherence, and facilitating weight loss. Promising results have been shown for a drug combination of atomoxetine and oxybutynin, GLP-1 receptor agonists like tirzepatide, and future pharmacological strategies include orforglipron and retatrutide.     • Adenosine, caffeine, and sleep–wake regulation: state of the science and perspectives
      Reichert, Deboer, Landolt | 2022
      The review article "Adenosine, caffeine, and sleep–wake regulation: state of the science and perspectives" investigates the effects of adenosine and caffeine on sleep regulation over centuries, discussing their roles in both acute and chronic consumption, and their potential impact on circadian rhythms.     • Amino acid modulation of carotid body function
      Gold, Pauza, Felippe, Shen, Paton | 2026
      This scientific paper published in The Journal of Physiology investigates the role of amino acids, particularly glutamate, in modulating carotid body function for arterial chemoreception and respiratory regulation. The study finds that during hypoxia, glutamate stimulates postsynaptic petrosal neurons and adjacent glomus cells, while GABA inhibits excitation of neighboring glomus cells, creating an 'accelerator-brake' mechanism that regulates the sensitivity of the carotid body to hypoxia. The study suggests that amino acids such as glutamate and GABA play significant roles in regulating respiratory control through carotid body function, particularly contributing to long-term adaptations seen after repeated hypoxia, potentially explaining heightened chemoreflex sensitivity observed in human conditions involving intermittent hypoxia, such as sleep apnea. The study has experimental limitations, including using potassium cyanide instead of physiological hypoxia and relying on pharmacological approaches to investigate glutamatergic and GABAergic signaling.
    • An optimized 'hypoxia in a pill' regimen reverses neurodegenerative disease phenotypes in multiple preclinical models
      Wang1,2,3, Marutani4, Zazzeron4, Menard4, Volpicelli-Daley5, Ichinose4, Mootha1,2,3
      This study investigates a novel drug combination (GBT601/PT2399) as a potential treatment for neurodegenerative diseases such as Leigh syndrome, Friedreich's ataxia, Parkinson's disease, and Huntington's disease. Preclinical results reveal that the drug combo significantly extends median lifespan in the Leigh syndrome model, improves motor functions in Parkinson's and Huntington's disease models, and reduces brain tissue hyperoxia without inducing weight loss or pulmonary hypertension. The findings suggest a promising therapeutic approach for neurodegenerative diseases, with future research focusing on clinical trials to evaluate safety and efficacy.
    • BDNF is necessary and sufficient for spinal respiratory plasticity following intermittent hypoxia
      Baker-Herman, Fuller, Bavis, Zabka, Golder, Doperalski, Johnson, Watters, Mitchell | 2003
      In the study, researchers found that brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) plays a crucial role in long-lasting phrenic facilitation (pLTF), a model of respiratory plasticity induced by intermittent hypoxia. The process involves serotonin-dependent BDNF synthesis near phrenic motoneurons, and BDNF administration elicits pLTF without hypoxia. Endogenous BDNF synthesis is necessary for pLTF, and the effect requires activation of a high affinity TrkB receptor. The findings could have implications for conditions such as Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS), obstructive sleep apnea, and respiratory failure following spinal cord injury.

      The scientific paper discusses a workshop on 'Lung Sensing', a sensory interface beyond gas exchange in the lungs.     • Breathing: Rhythmicity, Plasticity, Chemosensitivity
      Feldman, Mitchell, Nattie | 2003
      The paper reviews recent advancements in understanding the neural mechanisms that generate rhythmic breathing patterns (rhythmicity), adaptive changes in breathing patterns (plasticity), and the role of chemoreceptors in regulating respiratory drive based on blood gas changes (chemosensitivity). Key findings include identifying the preBötzinger Complex as a key site for generating the respiratory rhythm, discussing serotonin-dependent long-term facilitation during intermittent hypoxia, and highlighting neurons with chemosensitivity spread throughout the brainstem. These studies could lead to more effective therapeutic strategies for various respiratory control disorders like Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS), sleep-disordered breathing or sleep apnea, Congenital Central Hypoventilation Syndrome, and respiratory insufficiency in spinally injured patients.

    • Decoding neuronal criticality firing patterns for large brain based EEG
      Beres, Ribeiro Rodrigues, Myers, Napoli
      This scientific study introduces a new method for decoding large-scale Electroencephalogram (EEG) neural recordings during cognitive tasks, focusing on demonstrating self-organization of neuronal networks indicative of neural criticality. By analyzing EEG data from 49 subjects and using hierarchical arrangements, the researchers discover power laws for multiple neuronal oscillatory groups, demonstrating scale-invariance through log–log plots, and characterizing criticality on the millisecond to seconds time scale relevant to cognitive tasks. This method could offer a significant advancement in understanding the dynamics of the neuronal network and its role in cognitive processes, potentially informing AI and machine learning developments.

      In the scientific paper "Early Microglial Response to Amyloid Plaques Drives Sleep Loss in Alzheimer's Disease," researchers discover that an early microglial response to amyloid plaques causes sleep loss, particularly NREM sleep, in Alzheimer's disease.     • Exercise and hypoxia independently stimulate platelet liberation of brain-derived neurotrophic factor
      Thomas, Caldwell, Macleod, Abraham, Carr, Gasho, Brewster, Bruce, Monteleone, Duffy, Howe, Tremblay, Jenkins, Talbot, Stembridge, Ainslie, Gibbons | 2025
      This study published in 'The Journal of Physiology' investigates the role of Brain-Derived Neurotrophic Factor (BDNF) and Platelet Factor 4 (PF4) during exercise at sea level and high altitude, revealing that maximal exercise doubles free BDNF concentration, with platelets playing a role in liberating BDNF and potentially contributing to the neuroprotective effects of exercise.
