    • GABAA receptor mediated facilitation of proprioceptive afferent transmission to motoneurons is facilitated by TRPM8 channels on C-fibres that are gated by serotonin
      Black, Li, Hari, Lin, Stephens, Fouad, Fenrich, Lucas-Osma, Gorassini, Bennett | 2026
      The study conducted at the University of Alberta investigates how serotonin modulates sensory transmission within the monosynaptic stretch reflex from proprioceptive Ia afferents to motoneurons, using zolmitriptan as an example. It finds that serotonin, via its main receptor subtype 5-HT1D on nociceptive C-fibers, induces a long-lasting tonic depolarization in Ia afferents through increased GABAergic input onto them, promoting sodium spike initiation. Activation of 5-HT1D receptors with zolmitriptan inhibits C-fiber activity and reduces the GABAA receptor-mediated tonic depolarization on Ia afferents, decreasing spike propagation to motoneurons. The research suggests that brainstem-derived serotonin not only inhibits ongoing nociceptive C-fibers activity but also reduces GABAergic neuron activity and sensory transmission in large proprioceptive afferents, leading to decreased motoneuron activity. This could have implications for pain studies and challenges traditional concepts of sensory gating.

    • MINI-REVIEW Opioids and Respiratory Depression: The role of carotid bodies in opioid-induced respiratory depression
      Spiller, Pedro F., Amorim, Mateus R., Polotsky, Vsevolod Y. | 2023
      This mini-review discusses Carotid Bodies as a potential target to mitigate Opioid-Induced Respiratory Depression, a significant public health concern in the U.S., causing over 130 deaths daily. The review suggests that activating opioid receptors (μ, d, or κ) in Carotid Bodies may protect against respiratory depression and reduce its effects without compromising analgesic properties of opioids.       Serebrovskaya, Manukhina, Smith, Downey, Mallet | 2008
      This mini-review discusses the conflicting effects of Intermittent Hypoxia (IH) on systemic hypertension, particularly its potential as a treatment due to its ability to lower blood pressure in individuals with hypertension. The review highlights research conducted primarily in the former Soviet Union, focusing on mechanisms such as enhanced sarcoplasmic reticular Ca2+ sequestration and increased antioxidant defenses that contribute to IH's effects on preventing endothelial dysfunction and high blood pressure in rats with various forms of experimental hypertension. The paper also provides references related to nitric oxide, intermittent hypoxia, and cardiovascular physiology.

The research finds that NE increases action potential frequency while decreasing the average action potential number during network bursts, and NO attenuates synchrony by activating soluble guanylyl cyclase and cGMP-dependent protein kinase (PKG).       This study by Fleming et al. (2006) investigates the cellular inflammatory response in human spinal cords after injury, focusing on neutrophils, monocytes/macrophages, lymphocytes, oxidative enzymes, and proteolytic enzymes.       The study "Vip+ vagal neurons control allergen-induced responses" found that Vip+ vagal sensory neurons play a crucial role in asthma pathogenesis by mediating airway hyperreactivity and type 2 inflammation through the neurotrophin-NGFR signaling axis.     • Xylazine exacerbates fentanyl-induced respiratory depression and bradycardia
      Demery, Moore, Levitt, Anand, Traynor | 2025
      This research explores the combined impact of fentanyl and xylazine on respiratory measures in mice, finding that the combination significantly reduces breathing rate more than fentanyl alone, with distinct effects on inspiration and expiration flow rates. Fentanyl decreases blood oxygen saturation and heart rate, while xylazine has less impact but exacerbates fentanyl's effects. The study suggests potential implications for anesthesia and sedation in mice, offering insights into mechanisms underlying xylazine's contribution to opioid overdoses in humans; however, these findings may not directly apply to humans due to the study being conducted on mice.

    • 4-Aminopyridine-sensitive outward currents in preBötzinger complex neurons influence respiratory rhythm generation in neonatal mice
      Hayes, Mendenhall, Brush, Del Negro | 2008
      The 2008 study by Hayes et al. investigates the role of a transient outward potassium current, IA, in shaping inspiratory bursts during respiratory activity within the preBötzinger complex (preBötC) in neonatal mice. The researchers found that more than half of inspiratory neurons express IA and that its maximum conductance positively correlates with membrane capacitance and negatively with resistance, with a time constant of inactivation between 20-80ms. 4-aminopyridine (4-AP), an IA blocker, was used to study its role in preBc neurons and caused disorganized XII output and noisy preBötC rhythms that fluctuate in amplitude and period, suggesting that IA plays a crucial role in the regularity of the cycle period and drive potential magnitude in preBötC neurons. The study proposes that further research is needed to clarify the specific contributions of different Kv subunits to preBötC IA and its functional significance in respiration.

    • A projection atlas of excitatory and inhibitory inputs to the preB€otzinger complex: substrates for multimodal breathing control
      Reilly, Kadri, Arthurs, Sedano, Brezinski, Baertsch | 2021
      The research "A Projection Atlas of Excitatory and Inhibitory Inputs to the preBötzinger Complex" reveals that the preBötzinger complex, a critical region for rhythmic breathing in the brain, is modulated by diverse long-range excitatory and inhibitory inputs from various brain regions. The study identifies multiple sources of excitatory inputs converging on the preBötzinger complex, such as the caudal and rostral medullary regions, the rostral ventral respiratory group (rVRG), the lateral parafacial region/retrotrapezoid nucleus (PFl/RTN), and the spinal trigeminal nucleus caudalis (Sp5c). This study provides a foundation for understanding the neural mechanisms underlying breathing regulation and could potentially lead to new treatments for respiratory disorders and sleep apnea.

    • Amino acid modulation of carotid body function
      Gold, Pauza, Felippe, Shen, Paton | 2026
      This scientific paper published in The Journal of Physiology investigates the role of amino acids, particularly glutamate, in modulating carotid body function for arterial chemoreception and respiratory regulation. The study finds that during hypoxia, glutamate stimulates postsynaptic petrosal neurons and adjacent glomus cells, while GABA inhibits excitation of neighboring glomus cells, creating an 'accelerator-brake' mechanism that regulates the sensitivity of the carotid body to hypoxia. The study suggests that amino acids such as glutamate and GABA play significant roles in regulating respiratory control through carotid body function, particularly contributing to long-term adaptations seen after repeated hypoxia, potentially explaining heightened chemoreflex sensitivity observed in human conditions involving intermittent hypoxia, such as sleep apnea. The study has experimental limitations, including using potassium cyanide instead of physiological hypoxia and relying on pharmacological approaches to investigate glutamatergic and GABAergic signaling.

    • BDNF is necessary and sufficient for spinal respiratory plasticity following intermittent hypoxia
      Baker-Herman, Fuller, Bavis, Zabka, Golder, Doperalski, Johnson, Watters, Mitchell | 2003
      In the study, researchers found that brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) plays a crucial role in long-lasting phrenic facilitation (pLTF), a model of respiratory plasticity induced by intermittent hypoxia. The process involves serotonin-dependent BDNF synthesis near phrenic motoneurons, and BDNF administration elicits pLTF without hypoxia. Endogenous BDNF synthesis is necessary for pLTF, and the effect requires activation of a high affinity TrkB receptor. The findings could have implications for conditions such as Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS), obstructive sleep apnea, and respiratory failure following spinal cord injury.

    • Brain motion is driven by mechanical coupling with the abdomen

      The study published in Nature Neuroscience investigates the motion of the dorsal cortex within the skull of awake mice, revealing that brain motion is primarily driven by locomotion rather than respiration or the cardiac cycle. Researchers found that forces generated by abdominal muscle contractions reach the brain via the vertebral venous plexus (VVP), a network of valveless veins connecting the abdomen and spinal canal, suggesting a potential hydraulic system driving cerebrospinal fluid flow.     • Breathing during Exercise: Respiratory and Circulatory Interactions
      Sheel, Angus | 2026
      The paper 'Breathing during Exercise: Respiratory and Circulatory Interactions' delves into the interplay between respiratory and circulatory systems during rest and exercise, emphasizing intrathoracic pressure as a key factor. At rest, ITP affects lung volume, cardiac functions, and vascular resistance. During exercise, increased muscle contraction alters ITP, VT, and MSP, influencing blood flow distribution between respiratory and locomotor muscles.     • Breathing: Rhythmicity, Plasticity, Chemosensitivity

    • Challenging conventional wisdom: A fresh look at the litter effect in rats
      Huxtable, Flanigan, Frazure, Wollman, Sun, Pandey, Restifo, Fregosi | 2026
      The scientific paper led by Dr. Adrianne G. Huxtable challenges the traditional belief that physiological variance among neonatal Sprague–Dawley rat litters is greater between litters than within litters. Using intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC) analysis, the study found that a significant portion of total variation in body weight and breathing frequency originated within litters rather than between them. The findings suggest a need for more accurate experimental designs, taking into account specific research questions, outcome variables, rat genetics, raw variance both within and between litters, and minimizing animal use in research. The authors propose using multiple pups per litter and applying mixed-model ANOVA with litter as a random effect for more reliable results. However, limitations include the study's applicability to other lab rodents and small sample sizes in some cohorts.

    • Decoding neuronal criticality firing patterns for large brain based EEG
      Beres, Ribeiro Rodrigues, Myers, Napoli
      This scientific study introduces a new method for decoding large-scale Electroencephalogram (EEG) neural recordings during cognitive tasks, focusing on demonstrating self-organization of neuronal networks indicative of neural criticality.     • Finding the Groove in Neural Space

    • GABAA receptor mediated facilitation of proprioceptive afferent transmission to motoneurons is facilitated by TRPM8 channels on C-fibres that are gated by serotonin
      Black, Li, Hari, Lin, Stephens, Fouad, Fenrich, Lucas-Osma, Gorassini, Bennett | 2026
      The study conducted at the University of Alberta investigates how serotonin modulates sensory transmission within the monosynaptic stretch reflex from proprioceptive Ia afferents to motoneurons, using zolmitriptan as an example. It finds that serotonin, via its main receptor subtype 5-HT1D on nociceptive C-fibers, induces a long-lasting tonic depolarization in Ia afferents through increased GABAergic input onto them, promoting sodium spike initiation. Activation of 5-HT1D receptors with zolmitriptan inhibits C-fiber activity and reduces the GABAA receptor-mediated tonic depolarization on Ia afferents, decreasing spike propagation to motoneurons. The research suggests that brainstem-derived serotonin not only inhibits ongoing nociceptive C-fibers activity but also reduces GABAergic neuron activity and sensory transmission in large proprioceptive afferents, leading to decreased motoneuron activity. This could have implications for pain studies and challenges traditional concepts of sensory gating.
    • GABAergic Hub Neurons Orchestrate Synchrony in Developing Hippocampal Networks
      Bonifazi, Goldin, Picardo, Jorquera, Cattani, Bianconi, Represa, Ben-Ari, Cossart
      The scientific paper investigates the significant role of high connectivity GABAergic interneurons, or hub neurons, in regulating network dynamics during hippocampus development, particularly their impact on Giant Depolarizing Potentials (GDPs). Key findings show that a third of targeted hub neurons significantly affect network dynamics compared to minimal impact from low connectivity neurons. Hub neurons are predominantly found in specific layers like the stratum oriens and lucidum, and activation of these neurons before synchrony contributes to network synchronization in the developing CA3 region. The study also highlights differences between hub and low connectivity interneurons in terms of electrophysiological properties and references notable authors' works to provide insights into physiological and pathological network oscillations.

      Belliveau, Footer, Platenkamp, Rodriguez, Kim, Prinz, van Loon, Lin, Eustis, Chan, Cohen, Theriot | 2026
      The research published in Cell (2026) by Belliveau et al. identifies Galvanin as a crucial electric-field sensor essential for directed migration in various rapidly moving cells such as immune and epithelial cells. Upon exposure to electric fields, Galvanin localizes at the front and rear of cells, changing spatial patterns, exhibiting an exponential decay with a net charge of -18e, and regulating subsequent movement responses. The study suggests that a highly negatively charged construct can rescue directed migration in knockout cell lines, indicating a high net negative ectodomain charge is sufficient for Galvanin-mediated electrotaxis. Further studies are needed to understand the molecular mechanisms underlying Galvanin's role in electrotaxis and its implications for cell signaling and other biological processes.

    • Heart-­Lung Interactions in Health and Disease

      This Nature Metabolism study investigates the role of hyperglycosylation, specifically N-glycosylation, in Alzheimer's disease (AD). The research suggests that excessive N-glycosylation contributes to the conserved phenotype of brain hyperglycosylation in AD, and reducing this excess improves social recognition memory in AD mouse models.     • MINIREVIEW Intermittent Hypoxia: Cause of or Therapy for Systemic Hypertension?
      Serebrovskaya, Manukhina, Smith, Downey, Mallet | 2008
      This mini-review discusses the conflicting effects of Intermittent Hypoxia (IH) on systemic hypertension, particularly its potential as a treatment due to its ability to lower blood pressure in individuals with hypertension. The review highlights research conducted primarily in the former Soviet Union, focusing on mechanisms such as enhanced sarcoplasmic reticular Ca2+ sequestration and increased antioxidant defenses that contribute to IH's effects on preventing endothelial dysfunction and high blood pressure in rats with various forms of experimental hypertension.     • Molecular mechanism of NMDA receptor potentiation by lactate
      Fiumelli, Herrera-López, Lemtiri-Chlieh, Mottier, Girgis, Ben-Adiba, Jourdain, Carrano, Mahmood, Ooi, Arold, Di Luca, Gardoni, Magistretti | 2026
      The research study published in The Journal of Physiology in 2026 investigates the role of lactate produced by astrocytes in enhancing neuronal NMDA receptor function, synaptic plasticity, and memory formation via a redox mechanism. This process involves lactate entering neurons through monocarboxylate transporters (MCTs), being oxidized to pyruvate, and generating NADH, which increases the intracellular NADH/NAD+ ratio. This redox shift promotes calcium release from ryanodine-sensitive stores (RyR) and recruits Ca2+/calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II (CaMKII) to GluN2B-containing NMDARs, leading to enhanced NMDAR currents and prolonged decay kinetics. This strengthened NMDAR–CaMKII signaling increases NMDAR presence at postsynaptic sites, facilitating the molecular processes underlying long-term synaptic plasticity and memory formation. The study was conducted by a team from various institutions and reveals a molecular pathway linking brain metabolism with learning and memory processes.

    • Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation
      Balaguer, Prat-Ortega, Ostrowski, Borda, Verma, Yadav, Sorensen, de Freitas, Ensel, Donadio, Liang, Ho, Damiani, Grigsby, Fields, Gonzalez-Martinez, Gerszten, Fisher, Weber, Pirondini, Capogrosso | 2025
      In the study 'Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation' (Balaguer et al., 2025), researchers discovered that residual brain inputs modulate motoneuron membrane potentials, allowing subthreshold SCS pulses to transform into action potentials and entrain motoneuron activity to the stimulation. The study found that only a subset of SCS parameters enables volitional motor control, and this functional parameter space shrinks with lesion severity. The findings have potential implications for treating neurological disorders like spinal cord injuries, where SCS could help restore motor function by allowing participants to leverage residual supraspinal inhibition to control motoneuron firing rate. However, the study's limitations include focusing on immediate SCS effects and not accounting for long-term changes due to physical training.

    • Physiological Differences Underlying Divergent Hypoxia
      Burtscher, Mallet, Sah, Gassmann, Burtscher, Iturriaga | 2025
      This review paper, published in 2025 by Johannes Burtscher et al., compares the physiological differences underlying altitude adaptations and responses to hypoxia among humans, rats, and mice. The authors find that humans and rodents have distinct reactions: humans boost ventilation, oxygen transport, and anaerobic metabolism during hypoxia, while rodents initially lower their metabolic rate. Rodents demonstrate higher-altitude tolerance due to factors like larger lung volume, efficient gas exchange, and hypoxic hypometabolism. However, rats are more susceptible to hypoxia than mice or humans, with myocardial injury, excessive erythropoiesis, and significant pulmonary vascular remodeling in response. The authors propose combining rodent studies with large animal models, focusing on comparative physiology and molecular biology, developing valid in vitro systems for systemically relevant hypoxia responses, using biomarkers for back-translation, and considering dosing windows of intermittent/continuous hypoxia protocols. They emphasize that no single animal model is ideal for pre-clinical hypoxia research due to the species' distinct adaptations.
      Keywords: chronic continuous hypoxia, erythropoietin, high altitude adaptation, human physiology, hypoxia inducible factor, intermittent hypoxia conditioning, mouse, rat, red blood cells, species differences
    • Potentiation of active locomotor state by spinal-projecting serotonergic neurons

      The scientific paper by Fenstermacher et al. investigates the roles of spinal-projecting serotonergic (5-HT) neurons in locomotor behavior using optogenetic techniques on mice. Activation of these neurons increases the speed and duration of ongoing locomotion, with key findings showing an increase in individual run bouts' length, fewer overall run bouts, and a shift towards faster speeds during locomotion. The study suggests that serotonergic neurons can potentiate motor output over extended time scales, enhancing locomotor output and modulating the temporal dynamics of motor circuits. The research has potential implications for understanding the function of serotonin in motor behavior and developing innovative approaches to control locomotion in neuroprosthetics.

    • Regulation of sleep homeostasis mediator adenosine by basal forebrain glutamatergic neurons
      Peng*, Wu*, Song*, Zhang, Li, Xu†
      The research paper "GRABAdo1.0: A Novel Sensor for Extracellular Adenosine Detection and Its Role in Sleep Homeostasis" presents a study on glutamatergic neurons in the basal forebrain (BF), demonstrating their role as key regulators of sleep homeostasis through adenosine release. The study found higher adenosine levels during wakefulness and REM sleep compared to NREM sleep, and revealed a correlation between calcium activity in BF cholinergic neurons and adenosine levels. Optogenetic activation increased extracellular adenosine levels, while lesioning these glutamatergic neurons resulted in increased wakefulness during the active period, less recovery sleep after prolonged wakefulness, and impaired sleep rebound. The 'ion (38)' experiment showed statistically significant differences in NREM sleep time between the lesion group and control group. The discussion section reviews existing studies on sleep regulation and neural activity in the basal forebrain, confirming adenosine's role in mediating homeostatic control of sleep in cholinergic BF neurons, and contributing new insights into how neural activity during wakefulness contributes to sleep pressure through the release of sleep-inducing factors by BF glutamatergic neurons.
    • Resistance training-induced adaptations in the neuromuscular system: Physiological mechanisms and implications for human performance
      Lecce, Amoruso, Felici, Bazzucchi | 2026
      This topical review published in The Journal of Physiology by Edoardo Lecce's team from the University of Rome 'Foro Italico' explores neuromuscular adaptations resulting from resistance training (RT). They find increased excitability, improved synaptic input transmission, and decreased synaptic noise in the brain and spinal cord, leading to a more stable neural drive. At the spinal level, intrinsic motoneuron excitability, neuromodulatory input, and spinal circuitry are modified, resulting in enhanced motor unit (MU) excitability due to attenuated inhibitory pathways. Skeletal muscle adaptations involve contractile protein synthesis, structural repair by satellite cells, and endurance capacity improvement through mitochondrial biogenesis and capillarization. These changes lead to increased strength, improved physical performance, and alterations in muscle fiber type composition, energy metabolism, glucose uptake, and hormone levels, with practical applications for healthy individuals and those affected by chronic disorders.

    • Review: Cell-type-specific APOE4 cascade across the Alzheimer’s disease continuum
      Shostak, Liang, Huang | 2026
      The review "Cell-type-specific APOE4 cascade across the Alzheimer’s disease continuum" highlights Apolipoprotein E4 (APOE4) as the leading genetic risk factor for Alzheimer's disease (AD), causing early neuronal deficits in preclinical AD stages through age-associated stress. This neuronal damage triggers a harmful glial response, amplifying neurodegeneration and accelerating the onset of prodromal and dementia AD stages. The proposed model suggests potential therapeutic targets like HMGB1 and MHC class I for treating neuroinflammation but emphasizes the need for further investigation into their broader physiological impact and safety, with the ultimate goal of preventing APOE4's harmful effects on the aging brain before cognitive decline begins.
    • Review: Neurologic mechanisms regulating cough in health and disease
      Mazzone, Pflaumer, Richards | 2026
      The 2026 review "Neurologic mechanisms regulating cough in health and disease" published by the American College of Allergy, Asthma & Immunology explores the neurology behind both acute and chronic cough, focusing on vagal sensory neurons and brainstem circuits. The study suggests that chronic cough is associated with peripheral sensory nerve fiber sensitivity and central neural mechanisms amplification, leading to excessive coughing in response to low levels of thermal, mechanical, or chemical exposure. Brain imaging studies suggest chronic cough may be a disorder of reduced inhibitory control, and therapies restoring sensorimotor balance could potentially control excessive cough. The review highlights the clinical significance of chronic cough and significant therapeutic advances in recent years for managing it, though uniformity in responsivity to trialed drugs indicates the need for therapies directed at specific endotypic mechanisms or responsive treatable traits for effective cough management. Funding was provided by the National Health and Medical Research Council of Australia, with some authors receiving grants and consulting fees from various pharmaceutical companies.

    • Test-retest reliability of threshold-tracking TMS and peripheral nerve testing in healthy adults and individuals with chronic cervical spinal cord injury
      Hrycyk, Gramm Kristensen, Tankisi, Spooren, Tedesco Triccas
      The study investigates the test-retest reliability of neuroexcitability measures using threshold-tracking methods in both healthy adults and individuals with chronic cervical spinal cord injury (SCI). It finds excellent relative reliability for TMS measures in both groups, high test-retest reliability for NET using the TROND protocol across all age groups, and the T-T RMT test being more efficient.       Abraira, Eisdorfer, Lin, Thackray, Theis, Vivinetto, Oputa, Nacht, Martinez, Tschang, Mahmood, Tucker, Ruven, Pusuloori, Zymoro, Phu, Birch, Kumar, Schachner, Sahni, Popovich, Ferguson, McTigue, Tysseling, Dulin, Hollis II, Datta | 2026
      The multi-institutional study investigates motor recovery in mice and rats following spinal cord injury using a unique syllable-level behavioral analysis approach, revealing heterogeneity in recovery across subjects and changes shaped by injury severity and individual identity. The findings suggest that recovery after SCI is composed of repertoire-level changes under a disrupted brain-spinal cord axis, and the researchers propose that a more comprehensive understanding of post-SCI recovery can be gained by analyzing various behaviors and biomechanical signatures rather than relying solely on traditional methods like the BMS scale.
    • The crucial role of normative ageing in respiratory pathogenesis
      Weight*, Tatler*, Huckstepp*, Adcock, Barnes, van Beek, Christopher, Clifford, Dhasmana, Hart, Hashmat, Noble, Sayers, Short, Stinson, Calimport†, Bentley†
      This scientific paper, written by the International Consortium to Classify Ageing-Related Pathologies (ICCARP) respiratory working group, explores genetic, epigenetic, and inflammatory factors contributing to age-related changes in lung function. Key findings suggest chronic low-grade inflammation plays a significant role in these changes, while senescent cells, telomere shortening, and altered secretomes also contribute. The paper proposes classifying pulmonary fibrosis driven by senescence as an ageing-related pathology and advocates for a multiprofessional approach to manage lung aging due to its complex nature involving both physiological and psychological aspects. However, the research relies heavily on inference from currently classified diseases and has a paucity of human data, highlighting the need for further study of human tissue samples across various ages to better understand lung ageing, particularly regarding the mucociliary system.
    • The neuroscience of cancer
      Mancusi1, Monje1,2 | 2023
      The scientific paper investigates complex interactions between the nervous system, cancer cells, and immune cells in various types of cancers, such as glioma, gastrointestinal, oral squamous cell carcinomas, and pancreatic cancer. It reveals that neuronal activity promotes cancer progression, co-opted by cancer cells to enhance metastasis potential.     • Timed microglia depletion promotes functional network reorganization and motor recovery after ischaemic stroke
      Isla Cainzos, Quandt, Borggrewe, Altjohann, Magnus, Biskamp | 2026
      The study published in Brain Communications investigates the impact of short-term microglia depletion during the subacute phase following ischemic stroke on functional recovery and network dynamics in mice, using PLX5622. It found that this treatment significantly improved fine motor skills by day 7, as demonstrated by better performance in a sensorimotor test (SPR task), and revealed changes in sensorimotor network dynamics, including increased lower beta connectivity within the ipsilesional motor cortex during active periods, and a decrease in gamma connectivity in the contralesional sensorimotor system. The findings suggest that targeted suppression of activated microglia during the subacute phase may facilitate synaptic plasticity and network reorganization, enhancing functional recovery.
      Keywords: ischaemic stroke, CSF-1R inhibitor, microglial repopulation, sensorimotor networks, motor rehabilitation
    • Trends in Neurosciences: Making of BDNF: role of promoters, enhancers, and untranslated regions

      This scientific paper explores the complex regulation of Brain-Derived Neurotrophic Factor (BDNF), a protein essential for brain development, learning, and memory, focusing on various transcription factors and four enhancer regions within the BDNF gene.     • V_ CO2: the essential underpinning to homeostatic control of breathing
      Dempsey, McCrimmon
      The paper "CO2, V_CO2, and Breathing" argues against CO2/H+ chemoreception as the primary regulator of arterial partial pressure of carbon dioxide (PaCO2), particularly during non-rapid eye movement (NREM) sleep. Instead, it suggests that pulmonary CO2 exchange plays a crucial role in homeostatic ventilatory control by maintaining a near-proportional relationship between ventilation (V_A) and carbon dioxide production (V_CO2), with factors like body mass, food intake, exercise, and environmental changes playing a role. The research highlights the need for further investigation into potential mediators of these CO2 effects, such as the retrotrapezoid nucleus (RTN), to better understand the role of pulmonary CO2 exchange in PaCO2 homeostasis.
    • What Does Dopamine Actually Do?
      Adam | 2026
      The scientific paper reevaluates the traditional Reinforcement Prediction Error (RPE) model's perspective on dopamine's role in the brain, proposing expansive functions such as retrospective learning and decision-making enhancement, challenging fundamental assumptions and potentially impacting treatment strategies for neurological and psychiatric disorders. The debate within the scientific community regarding these new ideas is ongoing.

    • 4-Aminopyridine-sensitive outward currents in preBötzinger complex neurons influence respiratory rhythm generation in neonatal mice
      Hayes, Mendenhall, Brush, Del Negro | 2008
      The 2008 study by Hayes et al. investigates the role of a transient outward potassium current, IA, in shaping inspiratory bursts during respiratory activity within the preBötzinger complex (preBötC) in neonatal mice. The researchers found that more than half of inspiratory neurons express IA and that its maximum conductance positively correlates with membrane capacitance and negatively with resistance, with a time constant of inactivation between 20-80ms. 4-aminopyridine (4-AP), an IA blocker, was used to study its role in preBc neurons and caused disorganized XII output and noisy preBötC rhythms that fluctuate in amplitude and period, suggesting that IA plays a crucial role in the regularity of the cycle period and drive potential magnitude in preBötC neurons. The study proposes that further research is needed to clarify the specific contributions of different Kv subunits to preBötC IA and its functional significance in respiration.
    • A Review of Pharmacological Therapies for Obstructive Sleep Apnea and Impact on Cardiovascular Outcomes
      Robinson, De Sarkar, Moore, Malhotra | 2026
      The scientific paper advocates for a personalized approach to treating Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA), focusing on six potential pharmacological strategies including enhancing upper airway dilator muscle activity, stabilizing breathing control, blocking cardiovascular consequences, addressing symptoms, promoting CPAP adherence, and facilitating weight loss. Promising results have been shown for a drug combination of atomoxetine and oxybutynin, GLP-1 receptor agonists like tirzepatide, and future pharmacological strategies include orforglipron and retatrutide. The authors suggest that a combination of therapies targeting mechanical airway obstruction, underlying obesity, and cardiovascular risk factors will optimize clinical outcomes for OSA patients, with CPAP remaining the standard of care for OSA evaluation and treatment by sleep medicine specialists.
      Keywords: Obstructive sleep apnea, GLP-1 receptor agonists, Pharmacological therapies, Cardiovascular disease
    • A neuronal architecture underlying autonomic dysreflexia

      The scientific paper explores the neuronal architecture responsible for autonomic dysreflexia (AD) in mice with spinal cord injury, focusing on maladaptive connections between lower lumbosacral and lower thoracic spinal cord after SCI. Key findings suggest vGLUT2ON excitatory neurons are activated during AD, with Vsx2ON being the most transcriptionally responsive subpopulation.     • A projection atlas of excitatory and inhibitory inputs to the preB€otzinger complex: substrates for multimodal breathing control
      Reilly, Kadri, Arthurs, Sedano, Brezinski, Baertsch | 2021
      The research "A Projection Atlas of Excitatory and Inhibitory Inputs to the preBötzinger Complex" reveals that the preBötzinger complex, a critical region for rhythmic breathing in the brain, is modulated by diverse long-range excitatory and inhibitory inputs from various brain regions. The study identifies multiple sources of excitatory inputs converging on the preBötzinger complex, such as the caudal and rostral medullary regions, the rostral ventral respiratory group (rVRG), the lateral parafacial region/retrotrapezoid nucleus (PFl/RTN), and the spinal trigeminal nucleus caudalis (Sp5c). Inhibitory projections were predominantly ipsilateral to the injection site in the caudal medulla, while excitatory projections were found in the contralateral cVRG. This study provides a foundation for understanding the neural mechanisms underlying breathing regulation and could potentially lead to new treatments for respiratory disorders and sleep apnea.
      Keywords: breathing, inputs, preB€otzinger complex, projections, respiratory circuits
    • Adenosine and ATP Link PCO2 to Cortical Excitability via pH
      Dulla, Dobelis, Pearson, Frenguelli, Staley, Masino | 2005
      This 2005 scientific paper published in Neuron investigates the impact of carbon dioxide (CO2) levels on neural function, particularly in the hippocampus, and finds that increased CO2 levels decrease neuronal excitability through adenosine A1 receptor activation, while decreased CO2 levels increase excitability. The study suggests that CO2 modulates neuronal excitability and epileptiform activity through its effects on adenosine and purines, which could have implications for the development of new therapies for epilepsy and other neurological disorders.
    • Amino acid modulation of carotid body function
      Gold, Pauza, Felippe, Shen, Paton | 2026
      This scientific paper published in The Journal of Physiology investigates the role of amino acids, particularly glutamate, in modulating carotid body function for arterial chemoreception and respiratory regulation. The study finds that during hypoxia, glutamate stimulates postsynaptic petrosal neurons and adjacent glomus cells, while GABA inhibits excitation of neighboring glomus cells, creating an 'accelerator-brake' mechanism that regulates the sensitivity of the carotid body to hypoxia. The study suggests that amino acids such as glutamate and GABA play significant roles in regulating respiratory control through carotid body function, particularly contributing to long-term adaptations seen after repeated hypoxia, potentially explaining heightened chemoreflex sensitivity observed in human conditions involving intermittent hypoxia, such as sleep apnea. The study has experimental limitations, including using potassium cyanide instead of physiological hypoxia and relying on pharmacological approaches to investigate glutamatergic and GABAergic signaling.
    • An optimized 'hypoxia in a pill' regimen reverses neurodegenerative disease phenotypes in multiple preclinical models

      This study investigates a novel drug combination (GBT601/PT2399) as a potential treatment for neurodegenerative diseases such as Leigh syndrome, Friedreich's ataxia, Parkinson's disease, and Huntington's disease. Preclinical results reveal that the drug combo significantly extends median lifespan in the Leigh syndrome model, improves motor functions in Parkinson's and Huntington's disease models, and reduces brain tissue hyperoxia without inducing weight loss or pulmonary hypertension. The findings suggest a promising therapeutic approach for neurodegenerative diseases, with future research focusing on clinical trials to evaluate safety and efficacy.
    • Astrocyte activation in the ventrolateral medulla modulates breathing and arousal states Luiz Marcelo Oliveira
      Oliveira, Miranda, Lim, Severs, Takakura, Moreira, Li, Kalume, Ramirez
      This scientific study investigates the role of astrocytes, brain cells that regulate physiological functions, in modulating breathing and arousal states during sigh generation in alert mice, specifically focusing on those located in the ventral respiratory column (VRC).     • Astrocytes: Orchestrators of brain gas exchange and oxygen homeostasis
      Christie | 2026
      Astrocytes play a critical role in brain oxygen sensing, cerebral perfusion, and cognition, with a higher sensitivity to changes in oxygen availability compared to glomus cells. They respond to hypoxia by releasing nitric oxide, regulating sympathetic drive, and cardiac output to maintain brain oxygenation. However, during ischemia, astrocytes can both protect neurons and exacerbate brain damage due to their involvement in neurotransmitter release, calcium signaling, and gap junction communication. Future research should explore therapeutic interventions targeting astrocytic functions for improving neurological disorders related to oxygen homeostasis, cognitive impairment, and dementia.
    • BDNF is necessary and sufficient for spinal respiratory plasticity following intermittent hypoxia
      Baker-Herman, Fuller, Bavis, Zabka, Golder, Doperalski, Johnson, Watters, Mitchell | 2003
      In the study, researchers found that brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) plays a crucial role in long-lasting phrenic facilitation (pLTF), a model of respiratory plasticity induced by intermittent hypoxia. The process involves serotonin-dependent BDNF synthesis near phrenic motoneurons, and BDNF administration elicits pLTF without hypoxia. Endogenous BDNF synthesis is necessary for pLTF, and the effect requires activation of a high affinity TrkB receptor. The findings could have implications for conditions such as Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS), obstructive sleep apnea, and respiratory failure following spinal cord injury.

    • Brain motion is driven by mechanical coupling with the abdomen
      Garborg, Ghitti, Zhang, Ricotta, Frank, Mueller, Greenawalt, Turner, Kedarasetti, Mostafa, Lee, Costanzo, Drew | 2026
      The study published in Nature Neuroscience investigates the motion of the dorsal cortex within the skull of awake mice, revealing that brain motion is primarily driven by locomotion rather than respiration or the cardiac cycle. Researchers found that forces generated by abdominal muscle contractions reach the brain via the vertebral venous plexus (VVP), a network of valveless veins connecting the abdomen and spinal canal, suggesting a potential hydraulic system driving cerebrospinal fluid flow. The findings suggest a mechanical link between the brain and the abdominal compartment, with implications for understanding pathologies such as intracranial hypertension, meningioma, Chiari malformations, and conditions like obesity that elevate intra-abdominal pressure.
    • Breathing during Exercise: Respiratory and Circulatory Interactions
      Sheel, Angus | 2026
      The paper 'Breathing during Exercise: Respiratory and Circulatory Interactions' delves into the interplay between respiratory and circulatory systems during rest and exercise, emphasizing intrathoracic pressure as a key factor. At rest, ITP affects lung volume, cardiac functions, and vascular resistance. During exercise, increased muscle contraction alters ITP, VT, and MSP, influencing blood flow distribution between respiratory and locomotor muscles. The paper also explores sex differences in respiratory muscle metaboreflex sensitivity, showing that young females exhibit reduced sensitivity due to higher estrogen levels compared to males.
    • Breathing: Rhythmicity, Plasticity, Chemosensitivity

    • Challenging conventional wisdom: A fresh look at the litter effect in rats
      Huxtable, Flanigan, Frazure, Wollman, Sun, Pandey, Restifo, Fregosi | 2026
      The scientific paper led by Dr. Adrianne G. Huxtable challenges the traditional belief that physiological variance among neonatal Sprague–Dawley rat litters is greater between litters than within litters. Using intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC) analysis, the study found that a significant portion of total variation in body weight and breathing frequency originated within litters rather than between them. The findings suggest a need for more accurate experimental designs, taking into account specific research questions, outcome variables, rat genetics, raw variance both within and between litters, and minimizing animal use in research. The authors propose using multiple pups per litter and applying mixed-model ANOVA with litter as a random effect for more reliable results. However, limitations include the study's applicability to other lab rodents and small sample sizes in some cohorts.

The research suggests that excessive N-glycosylation contributes to the conserved phenotype of brain hyperglycosylation in AD, and reducing this excess improves social recognition memory in AD mouse models. Genetic knockdown of glycan biosynthetic enzymes showed improved cognitive outcomes, while glucosamine supplementation worsened behavior in these mice. A retrospective analysis of electronic health records revealed that glucosamine supplementation was associated with accelerated AD progression and reduced survival in patients with mild cognitive impairment (MCI) and dementia, highlighting the need for further investigation into cell-type-specific spatial information and pharmacological interventions targeting the hexosamine pathway in animal models.
    • Hypothalamic Glutamatergic/Orexinergic Neurons Attenuate Fentanyl-Induced Respiratory Depression via Medullary Pre-BöTzinger Complex Pathways

      This scientific paper by Arakawa et al. investigates the role of hypothalamic orexin (ORX) and glutamatergic neurons in counteracting fentanyl-induced respiratory depression (FIRD) in mice, focusing on the lateral hypothalamus (LH) and paraventricular nucleus (PVN).     • MINI-REVIEW Opioids and Respiratory Depression: The role of carotid bodies in opioid-induced respiratory depression
      Spiller, Pedro F., Amorim, Mateus R., Polotsky, Vsevolod Y. | 2023
      This mini-review discusses Carotid Bodies as a potential target to mitigate Opioid-Induced Respiratory Depression, a significant public health concern in the U.S., causing over 130 deaths daily. The review suggests that activating opioid receptors (μ, d, or κ) in Carotid Bodies may protect against respiratory depression and reduce its effects without compromising analgesic properties of opioids. However, further research is required to confirm the presence of κ opioid receptors in human Carotid Bodies and to evaluate the effectiveness of κ agonists in blocking OIRD with higher doses of fentanyl.       Keywords: carotid body, opioid receptor, respiratory depression
    • MINIREVIEW Intermittent Hypoxia: Cause of or Therapy for Systemic Hypertension?
      Serebrovskaya, Manukhina, Smith, Downey, Mallet | 2008
      This mini-review discusses the conflicting effects of Intermittent Hypoxia (IH) on systemic hypertension, particularly its potential as a treatment due to its ability to lower blood pressure in individuals with hypertension. The review highlights research conducted primarily in the former Soviet Union, focusing on mechanisms such as enhanced sarcoplasmic reticular Ca2+ sequestration and increased antioxidant defenses that contribute to IH's effects on preventing endothelial dysfunction and high blood pressure in rats with various forms of experimental hypertension. The article also distinguishes Intermittent Hypoxia Treatment (IHT) from Obstructive Sleep Apnea, emphasizing IHT's potential benefits over traditional drug therapies due to its minimal side effects and possible stimulation of an organism's general resistance. The paper also provides references related to nitric oxide, intermittent hypoxia, and cardiovascular physiology.
      Keywords: angiogenesis, hypertension, intermittent hypoxia, nitric oxide, obstructive sleep apnea

      The July 2025 study published in the Journal of Asthma presents two pilot clinical trials investigating neuromodulation as a potential treatment for moderate to severe asthma.       The study "Respiratory Control in Parkinson's Disease" by Tainá O.     • Review: Cell-type-specific APOE4 cascade across the Alzheimer’s disease continuum
      Shostak, Liang, Huang | 2026
      The review "Cell-type-specific APOE4 cascade across the Alzheimer’s disease continuum" highlights Apolipoprotein E4 (APOE4) as the leading genetic risk factor for Alzheimer's disease (AD), causing early neuronal deficits in preclinical AD stages through age-associated stress. This neuronal damage triggers a harmful glial response, amplifying neurodegeneration and accelerating the onset of prodromal and dementia AD stages.     • Review: Neurologic mechanisms regulating cough in health and disease
      Mazzone, Pflaumer, Richards | 2026
      The 2026 review "Neurologic mechanisms regulating cough in health and disease" published by the American College of Allergy, Asthma & Immunology explores the neurology behind both acute and chronic cough, focusing on vagal sensory neurons and brainstem circuits. The study suggests that chronic cough is associated with peripheral sensory nerve fiber sensitivity and central neural mechanisms amplification, leading to excessive coughing in response to low levels of thermal, mechanical, or chemical exposure. Brain imaging studies suggest chronic cough may be a disorder of reduced inhibitory control, and therapies restoring sensorimotor balance could potentially control excessive cough. The review highlights the clinical significance of chronic cough and significant therapeutic advances in recent years for managing it, though uniformity in responsivity to trialed drugs indicates the need for therapies directed at specific endotypic mechanisms or responsive treatable traits for effective cough management. Funding was provided by the National Health and Medical Research Council of Australia, with some authors receiving grants and consulting fees from various pharmaceutical companies.

    • Safety and feasibility of paired vagus nerve stimulation with rehabilitation for improving upper extremity function in people with cervical spinal cord injury: a pilot randomized controlled trial
      Korupolu, Yozbatiran, Miller, Shields, Tandon | 2026
      This scientific paper titled "Safety and feasibility of paired vagus nerve stimulation with rehabilitation for improving upper extremity function in people with cervical spinal cord injury: a pilot randomized controlled trial" aimed to evaluate the safety, feasibility, and therapeutic potential of pairing invasive Vagus Nerve Stimulation (VNS) with upper extremity rehabilitation in individuals with chronic cervical Spinal Cord Injury (SCI). The study demonstrated that the intervention was safe, well-tolerated, and feasible in SCI patients. At immediate post-intervention assessment, all participants receiving active VNS showed improvement in upper extremity motor function compared with baseline, whereas none of the participants in the control group showed improvement. However, these findings should be interpreted cautiously due to the small sample size and exploratory nature of the analysis. The study also found that transportation and access barriers affected recruitment and limited completion of in-clinic sessions, highlighting the need for more accessible and flexible rehabilitation delivery models. Further investigation is required with larger trials to determine the efficacy, durability, and optimal implementation of VNS paired with rehabilitation in spinal cord injury.

    • V_ CO2: the essential underpinning to homeostatic control of breathing

      Kalajian, Stettler, Conradi Smith, Del Negro | 2026
      This scientific paper published in 'The Journal of Physiology' explores the effects of mu-opioid receptor (μOR) signaling on Kir3 currents in glutamatergic preBötzinger complex neurons in mice, aiming to understand opioid-induced respiratory depression (OIRD). The study found that μOR agonist DAMGO increases passive potassium conductance in glutamatergic neurons via membrane-delimited modulation of Kir3 channels. Researchers propose targeting signaling molecules downstream from the μ-opioid receptor and attenuating Gβγ–Kir3 interactions as a potential strategy to reverse OIRD, with promising approaches including gallein, nanobodies, and short peptides that mimic Gβγ-binding sites on Kir3.
      Keywords: μ-Opioid receptor, Kir3 currents, glutamatergic preBötzinger complex

    • Auricular vagus nerve stimulation: a new option to treat inflammation in COVID-19?
      Fernando Mendes Sant’Anna, Rodrigo César Lima Resende, Lucas Bonacossa Sant’Anna, Sérgio Lívio Menezes Couceiro, Romero Bonacossa Soares Pinto, Mariana Bonacossa Sant’Anna, Liaw Wen Chao, Jozsef Constantin Szeles, Eugenijus Kaniusas | 2023
      The review article published in Rev Assoc Med Bras (2023) examines auricular vagus nerve stimulation (aVNS) as a potential novel treatment for managing inflammation related to COVID-19, suggesting it could modulate the immune response and reduce inflammatory markers, with more research required to confirm its efficacy and optimize usage.
    • Breathing during Exercise: Respiratory and Circulatory Interactions
      Sheel, Angus | 2026
      The paper 'Breathing during Exercise: Respiratory and Circulatory Interactions' delves into the interplay between respiratory and circulatory systems during rest and exercise, emphasizing intrathoracic pressure as a key factor. At rest, ITP affects lung volume, cardiac functions, and vascular resistance. During exercise, increased muscle contraction alters ITP, VT, and MSP, influencing blood flow distribution between respiratory and locomotor muscles. The paper also explores sex differences in respiratory muscle metaboreflex sensitivity, showing that young females exhibit reduced sensitivity due to higher estrogen levels compared to males.
    • Exercise and the organ-brain axis: Regulation of neurological disorders by emerging exerkines
      Dai, Dou, Nie, Sun, Chen, Fu, Zhang, Chu, Gao, Ge, Lin
      The scientific paper investigates the complex interplay between various organs and the brain, revealing how conditions like heart failure, non-alcoholic fatty liver disease, and splenectomy can lead to cognitive decline or neurological diseases such as Alzheimer's disease. Conversely, brain disorders affect peripheral organ functions. The study emphasizes the potential benefits of physical exercise in improving these organ-brain interactions and managing neurological disorders like AD and depression by engaging neural and endocrine pathways, regulating lipid metabolism, and secreting bioactive factors like lactate that promote neuroplasticity and cognitive function. The research calls for further study to fully comprehend these interactions' implications for human brain health.
      Keywords: Peripheral organs, Crosstalk
    • Funding Distributions, Trends, Gaps, and Policy Implications for Spinal Cord Injury Research: A Systematic Analysis of U.S. Federal Funds
      Tucker Gillespie, Andrew Buxton, Bethany R. Kondiles, Miranda Leal-Garcia, Mia R. Pacheco, Ashley V. Tran, Katie Vo, Lucy Abu, James Barr, Tanya A. Barretto, Jason Biundo, Sam Duenwald, Abigail Evans, Timothy N. Friedman, Isabella Gadaleta, Saahas Ganesh, Bryson Gottschall, Peyton Green, Grant Lee, Lilian Liu, Raza N. Malik, Elizabeth J. Nava, Chiara Sorani, Vansh Tandon, Hannah Thomas, Kyndal Thomas, Chris Barr, Ian Burkhart, Dylan A. McCreedy, Peter Nowell, Heath Blackmon, Alexander G. Rabchevsky, Matthew Rodreick, Abel Torres-Espín, Jennifer N. Dulin | 2023
      The scholarly paper "Funding Distributions, Trends, Gaps, and Policy Implications for Spinal Cord Injury Research" analyzes U.S. federal funding for spinal cord injury (SCI) research between 1976 and 2021 to identify trends, distributions, gaps, and policy implications. The study found that the National Institutes of Health primarily supports basic and early-stage translational research, while the Department of Defense's CDMRP Spinal Cord Injury Research Program concentrates on applied and clinical research, particularly in pain, bladder function, and neuromodulatory device development. The Department of Veterans Affairs invests in rehabilitation-focused studies aimed at improving musculoskeletal and functional health outcomes. Critical gaps in funding were identified for high-priority areas like bowel/gastrointestinal health, cardiovascular function, and mental health. To address these issues, the authors propose developing a centralized, living research dashboard, forming working groups consisting of researchers, program staff, industry representatives, and individuals with lived SCI experience, improving standardization and transparency in grant award labeling and classification, and creating dynamic, publicly accessible platforms to track federally funded SCI research across agencies.
    • Glucagon-­Like Peptide-­1 Receptor Agonists in Obesity-­Induced Respiratory Pathophysiology
      Ruiz, Furquim, Davaanyam, Williams, Polotsky, Amorim | 2026
      The scientific paper published in Acta Physiologica (2026) explores the potential for Glucagon-Like Peptide-1 Receptor Agonists (GLP1RAs), traditionally used for Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (TD2M) and obesity, to address respiratory disorders like Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA). The study finds that Liraglutide and Tirzepatide, including the dual receiver long-acting agonist Tirzepatide which was recently approved by the FDA for OSA in overweight individuals, reduce AHI in patients with obesity and moderate/severe OSA. The research suggests that GLP1RAs may have a promising role in treating OSA, independent of weight loss, and ongoing clinical trials for triple-hormone receptor agonist retatrutide offer hope for future treatments in this area.
    • Hyperglycosylation is a metabolic driver of Alzheimer’s disease
      Liu, Ribas, Medina, Nielsen, Clarke, Ma, Mueller, Plasencia, Sheer, Simpson, Soto, Sudderth, Cai, Cantrell, Colpaert, Shedlock, Wu, Young, Kooser, Chen, Huang, He, Alonge, Guo, Bian, Vander Kooi, Gentry, Sun, Quinones, Son, Azadi, Deberardinis, Prokop, Allison, Yang, Chen, Huang, He, Guo, Gao, Kooi, Gentry, & Sun | 2026
      This Nature Metabolism study investigates the role of hyperglycosylation, specifically N-glycosylation, in Alzheimer's disease (AD). The research suggests that excessive N-glycosylation contributes to the conserved phenotype of brain hyperglycosylation in AD, and reducing this excess improves social recognition memory in AD mouse models. Genetic knockdown of glycan biosynthetic enzymes showed improved cognitive outcomes, while glucosamine supplementation worsened behavior in these mice. A retrospective analysis of electronic health records revealed that glucosamine supplementation was associated with accelerated AD progression and reduced survival in patients with mild cognitive impairment (MCI) and dementia, highlighting the need for further investigation into cell-type-specific spatial information and pharmacological interventions targeting the hexosamine pathway in animal models.
    • In vivo multiplex imaging of dynamic neurochemical networks with designed far-­red dopamine sensors
      Zheng, Cai, Wang, Zhang, Zhuo, Dong, Zhang, Wang, Deng, Ji, Cui, Fang, Xinzhang, Huang, Kecheng Zhang, Jinxu Wang, Grimm, Johnsson, Schreiter, Lavis, Chen, Mu, Li
      The scientific paper presents HaloDA1.0, a new genetically encoded sensor for real-time monitoring of dopamine dynamics in various biological systems. It demonstrates rapid response to DA release and potential for multi-color imaging, reducing autofluorescence interference. In vivo experiments reveal simultaneous monitoring of DA, acetylcholine, and cyclic adenosine monophosphate dynamics in the mouse nucleus accumbens, and the effects of addictive drugs like cocaine on this regulatory mechanism are explored. The discussion highlights the importance of genetically encoded indicators (GEIs) in understanding brain function, addiction, and neurodegenerative diseases, while a separate study suggests investigating noncoding variants could be crucial in understanding complex diseases such as Alzheimer's disease.
    • Physiological Differences Underlying Divergent Hypoxia
      Burtscher, Mallet, Sah, Gassmann, Burtscher, Iturriaga | 2025
      This review paper, published in 2025 by Johannes Burtscher et al., compares the physiological differences underlying altitude adaptations and responses to hypoxia among humans, rats, and mice. The authors find that humans and rodents have distinct reactions: humans boost ventilation, oxygen transport, and anaerobic metabolism during hypoxia, while rodents initially lower their metabolic rate. Rodents demonstrate higher-altitude tolerance due to factors like larger lung volume, efficient gas exchange, and hypoxic hypometabolism. However, rats are more susceptible to hypoxia than mice or humans, with myocardial injury, excessive erythropoiesis, and significant pulmonary vascular remodeling in response. The authors propose combining rodent studies with large animal models, focusing on comparative physiology and molecular biology, developing valid in vitro systems for systemically relevant hypoxia responses, using biomarkers for back-translation, and considering dosing windows of intermittent/continuous hypoxia protocols. They emphasize that no single animal model is ideal for pre-clinical hypoxia research due to the species' distinct adaptations.
      Keywords: chronic continuous hypoxia, erythropoietin, high altitude adaptation, human physiology, hypoxia inducible factor, intermittent hypoxia conditioning, mouse, rat, red blood cells, species differences
    • Slowed canonical progress in large fields of science
      Chu, Evans | 2021
      The study by Johan S. G. Chua and James A. Evans suggests that an abundance of scientific publications in large fields can lead to stagnation due to cognitive overload and competition among many new ideas, causing established papers to maintain their citations while new ideas struggle to gain traction. The findings support this theory, revealing that as fields grow, top-cited papers become durably dominant, and policy measures such as establishing clear journal hierarchies, rewarding novel contributions, adopting a measure of novelty, and revising graduate training might help encourage innovation in science.
    • V_ CO2: the essential underpinning to homeostatic control of breathing
      Dempsey, McCrimmon
      The paper "CO2, V_CO2, and Breathing" argues against CO2/H+ chemoreception as the primary regulator of arterial partial pressure of carbon dioxide (PaCO2), particularly during non-rapid eye movement (NREM) sleep. Instead, it suggests that pulmonary CO2 exchange plays a crucial role in homeostatic ventilatory control by maintaining a near-proportional relationship between ventilation (V_A) and carbon dioxide production (V_CO2), with factors like body mass, food intake, exercise, and environmental changes playing a role. The research highlights the need for further investigation into potential mediators of these CO2 effects, such as the retrotrapezoid nucleus (RTN), to better understand the role of pulmonary CO2 exchange in PaCO2 homeostasis.

    • A neuroprosthesis for restoring hand movement and sensation in a person with complete tetraplegia
      Bouton | 2026
      The scientific paper presents a hybrid Dual Neuromodulation (DNB) system that combines an intracortical brain-computer interface, transcutaneous spinal cord stimulation, and peripheral stimuli for restoring motor and sensory functions in individuals with spinal cord injuries. The study on one participant showed significant improvements in motor control and sensory recovery, with potential for home use. Two related studies were discussed, both focusing on neural modulation in response to tactile stimuli and emphasizing the importance of understanding brain activity associated with different body parts and various types of stimuli. Challenges remain in optimizing stimulation parameters, adapting to interindividual variability, and streamlining identification of effective stimulation patterns for the DNB system.
    • Astrocyte activation in the ventrolateral medulla modulates breathing and arousal states Luiz Marcelo Oliveira
      Oliveira, Miranda, Lim, Severs, Takakura, Moreira, Li, Kalume, Ramirez
      This scientific study investigates the role of astrocytes, brain cells that regulate physiological functions, in modulating breathing and arousal states during sigh generation in alert mice, specifically focusing on those located in the ventral respiratory column (VRC). Activating a specific subset of Aldh1l1 cells within the VRC through chemogenetic or optogenetic methods increased the probability of inducing arousals accompanied by sighs, especially during hypoxia. The research suggests that medullary astrocytes play a crucial role in modulating sigh generation and arousal transitions during low oxygen levels, thereby contributing to the ventilatory and arousal response to low oxygen environments.
    • Astrocytes connect specific brain regions through plastic networks
      Cooper, Selles, Cammer, Redd, Gildea, Sall, Chiurri, Cheung, Wheeler, Saab, Liddelow, Chao | 2026
      This scientific paper explores the structural plasticity of astrocyte networks in mice brains following whisker trimming, and the role of connexin43 (Cx43) gap junctions in these networks. Key findings suggest that long-range astrocyte networks exhibit substantial deviations post-whisker trimming, and deleting Cx43 results in enhanced locomotor activity and accelerated hippocampal spreading depression. The study contributes to a better understanding of the role of astrocytes in neural signaling and various neurological disorders, emphasizing the importance of gap junctions like Cx43 for inter-astrocytic communication and their crucial roles during various neurological conditions.

    • Magnetogenetics: Tools for Noninvasive Neuromodulation with Cellular Resolution

    • Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation
      Balaguer, Prat-Ortega, Ostrowski, Borda, Verma, Yadav, Sorensen, de Freitas, Ensel, Donadio, Liang, Ho, Damiani, Grigsby, Fields, Gonzalez-Martinez, Gerszten, Fisher, Weber, Pirondini, Capogrosso | 2025
      In the study "Neural mechanisms underlying the recovery of voluntary control of motoneurons after paralysis with spinal cord stimulation," Balaguer et al. (2025) reveal that residual brain inputs do not facilitate SCS-mediated motor control but rather transform subthreshold SCS-generated excitatory postsynaptic potentials into action potentials, enabling brain regulation and volitional rate control during spinal cord stimulation in individuals with spinal cord injury and stroke. The study also highlights the limitation of SCS in severe injury cases due to a restricted set of functional stimulation parameters, while demonstrating fine control over force output by participants and improving the ability to modulate motor neuron firing rates through incorporating supraspinal inhibitory drive.
    • Spinal cord stimulation for upper limb motor function in people with chronic post-stroke hemiparesis: a feasibility trial
      2026
      The Nature Medicine study investigates the feasibility of cervical epidural spinal cord stimulation (SCS) for improving upper limb motor function in individuals with chronic post-stroke hemiparesis over a four-week period. Significant improvements were observed in strength, reduced spasticity, and increased Fugl-Meyer Assessment points. The study suggests that cervical SCS is safe, feasible, and effective for chronic post-stroke hemiparesis, but further research is needed to determine long-term effectiveness and safety, as well as the determinants of responsiveness to SCS, particularly spared sensory function.

    All

    • 4-Aminopyridine-sensitive outward currents in preBötzinger complex neurons influence respiratory rhythm generation in neonatal mice
      Hayes, Mendenhall, Brush, Del Negro | 2008
      The 2008 study by Hayes et al. investigates the role of a transient outward potassium current, IA, in shaping inspiratory bursts during respiratory activity within the preBötzinger complex (preBötC) in neonatal mice. The researchers found that more than half of inspiratory neurons express IA and that its maximum conductance positively correlates with membrane capacitance and negatively with resistance, with a time constant of inactivation between 20-80ms. 4-aminopyridine (4-AP), an IA blocker, was used to study its role in preBc neurons and caused disorganized XII output and noisy preBötC rhythms that fluctuate in amplitude and period, suggesting that IA plays a crucial role in the regularity of the cycle period and drive potential magnitude in preBötC neurons. The study proposes that further research is needed to clarify the specific contributions of different Kv subunits to preBötC IA and its functional significance in respiration.
    • A Review of Pharmacological Therapies for Obstructive Sleep Apnea and Impact on Cardiovascular Outcomes
      Robinson, De Sarkar, Moore, Malhotra | 2026
      The scientific paper advocates for a personalized approach to treating Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA), focusing on six potential pharmacological strategies including enhancing upper airway dilator muscle activity, stabilizing breathing control, blocking cardiovascular consequences, addressing symptoms, promoting CPAP adherence, and facilitating weight loss. Promising results have been shown for a drug combination of atomoxetine and oxybutynin, GLP-1 receptor agonists like tirzepatide, and future pharmacological strategies include orforglipron and retatrutide. The authors suggest that a combination of therapies targeting mechanical airway obstruction, underlying obesity, and cardiovascular risk factors will optimize clinical outcomes for OSA patients, with CPAP remaining the standard of care for OSA evaluation and treatment by sleep medicine specialists.
      Keywords: Obstructive sleep apnea, GLP-1 receptor agonists, Pharmacological therapies, Cardiovascular disease
    • A neuronal architecture underlying autonomic dysreflexia
      Soriano, Hudelle, Mahe, Gautier, Teo, Skinnider, Laskaratos, Ceto, Kathe, Hutson, Charbonneau, Girgis, Casha, Rimok, Tso, Larkin-Kaiser, Gandhi, Amir, Kang, Vyza, Martin-Moraud, Lacour, Demesmaeker, Asboth, Barraud, Anderson, Bloch, Squair, Phillips, Courtine | 2025
      The scientific paper explores the neuronal architecture responsible for autonomic dysreflexia (AD) in mice with spinal cord injury, focusing on maladaptive connections between lower lumbosacral and lower thoracic spinal cord after SCI. Key findings suggest vGLUT2ON excitatory neurons are activated during AD, with Vsx2ON being the most transcriptionally responsive subpopulation. The study also discusses an implantable system designed to restore hemodynamic stability in individuals with spinal cord injuries, featuring epidural electrodes, a brain-computer interface, and a closed-loop control system, which showed improved results compared to the control group in preclinical models and one human participant. The research highlights potential applications of this system beyond SCI management, but acknowledges limitations such as biocompatibility, long-term stability, and safety concerns for future optimization.

    • A new sense for electrical fields
      Riedl, Sixt | 2026
      The Cell-published study by Belliveau et al. confirms TMEM154/Galvanin as a cellular antenna that senses electrical gradients, facilitating cell migration towards the cathode. They found that Galvanin is essential for electrotaxis in human granulocytes and can transform non-electrotactic cell types into electrotactic ones, suggesting an electrophoretic process where Galvanin redistributes towards the anodal pole of the cell in electrical fields.
    • A projection atlas of excitatory and inhibitory inputs to the preB€otzinger complex: substrates for multimodal breathing control
      Reilly, Kadri, Arthurs, Sedano, Brezinski, Baertsch | 2021
      The research "A Projection Atlas of Excitatory and Inhibitory Inputs to the preBötzinger Complex" reveals that the preBötzinger complex, a critical region for rhythmic breathing in the brain, is modulated by diverse long-range excitatory and inhibitory inputs from various brain regions. The study identifies multiple sources of excitatory inputs converging on the preBötzinger complex, such as the caudal and rostral medullary regions, the rostral ventral respiratory group (rVRG), the lateral parafacial region/retrotrapezoid nucleus (PFl/RTN), and the spinal trigeminal nucleus caudalis (Sp5c). Inhibitory projections were predominantly ipsilateral to the injection site in the caudal medulla, while excitatory projections were found in the contralateral cVRG. This study provides a foundation for understanding the neural mechanisms underlying breathing regulation and could potentially lead to new treatments for respiratory disorders and sleep apnea.
      Keywords: breathing, inputs, preB€otzinger complex, projections, respiratory circuits
    • A µ-opioid receptor superagonist analgesic with minimal adverse effects
      Gomez, Ventriglia, Frangos, Sulima, Robertson, Sacco, Budinich, Giosan, Xie, Solis, Tischer, Bossert, Caldwell, Bonbrest, Essmann, Garçon-Poca, Choi, Noya, Limiac, Arce, Glatfelter, Robinson, Chen, Mullarkey, Brademan, Enten, Dunne, Quiroz, Schoenborn, Lee, Rais, Holt, Dannals, Shi, Hüttenhain, Ferré, Kiyatkin, Bonaventura, Shaham, Zachariou, Baumann, Skiniotis, Rice, Michaelides | 2026
      This scientific paper compares FNZ (fentanyl N-benzyl) and its metabolite DFNZ in terms of brain penetration, pharmacokinetics, MOR occupancy, dopamine neurotransmission, and potential for abuse. The research employs various techniques such as Caco-2 permeability assays, molecular docking, cryo-electron microscopy, and behavioral tests to demonstrate that FNZ has higher brain penetration than DFNZ, exhibits stronger MOR occupancy, and causes more potent dopamine neurotransmission effects. The study also highlights the critical role of VTA MOR-GAL1 heteromers in these effects and suggests potential implications for pain management, addiction, and neurological disorders. Further studies are recommended to explore the role of P-glycoprotein in drug transport across the blood-brain barrier.

    • Adenosine and ATP Link PCO2 to Cortical Excitability via pH
      Dulla, Dobelis, Pearson, Frenguelli, Staley, Masino | 2005
      This 2005 scientific paper published in Neuron investigates the impact of carbon dioxide (CO2) levels on neural function, particularly in the hippocampus, and finds that increased CO2 levels decrease neuronal excitability through adenosine A1 receptor activation, while decreased CO2 levels increase excitability. The study suggests that CO2 modulates neuronal excitability and epileptiform activity through its effects on adenosine and purines, which could have implications for the development of new therapies for epilepsy and other neurological disorders.
    • Adenosine signalling drives antidepressant actions of ketamine and ECT
      Yue1,2,3,4,5,12, Wang2,6,12, Zhai7,8, Yuan1,2,4, Cui1,2,4, Quan1,2,4, Zhou1,2,4, Fan2, Wang7, Wu9, Mi1,2, Ge1,2, Li10,11, Wang7,8, Luo1,2,3,4,11 | 2026
      This research investigates the role of adenosine in ketamine's antidepressant effects, revealing that ketamine increases adenosine levels in the mouse brain, particularly in the prefrontal cortex and hippocampus.     • Adenosine, caffeine, and sleep–wake regulation: state of the science and perspectives
      Reichert, Deboer, Landolt | 2022

    • Advances in the Regulation of Neural Function by Infrared Light
      Song, Wang, Peng | 2024
      The research paper "Advances in the Regulation of Neural Function by Infrared Light" investigates the potential use of infrared light as a replacement for traditional neural regulation methods due to its high spatial resolution, safety, and neural sensitivity. A novel image segmentation algorithm (NISA) was employed to analyze medical images against four established techniques, demonstrating superior performance with accuracy of 93.6%, precision of 92.7%, F1-score of 93.2%, and DICE coefficient of 94.0%. However, NISA has slower processing speed and requires advanced hardware for efficient implementation due to its increased computational complexity, but offers a promising solution for disease diagnosis and treatment by enabling precise identification of affected regions in medical imagery.
      Keywords: infrared light, regulation, nerve function, mechanism
    • Amino acid modulation of carotid body function
      Gold, Pauza, Felippe, Shen, Paton | 2026
      This scientific paper published in The Journal of Physiology investigates the role of amino acids, particularly glutamate, in modulating carotid body function for arterial chemoreception and respiratory regulation. The study finds that during hypoxia, glutamate stimulates postsynaptic petrosal neurons and adjacent glomus cells, while GABA inhibits excitation of neighboring glomus cells, creating an 'accelerator-brake' mechanism that regulates the sensitivity of the carotid body to hypoxia. The study suggests that amino acids such as glutamate and GABA play significant roles in regulating respiratory control through carotid body function, particularly contributing to long-term adaptations seen after repeated hypoxia, potentially explaining heightened chemoreflex sensitivity observed in human conditions involving intermittent hypoxia, such as sleep apnea. The study has experimental limitations, including using potassium cyanide instead of physiological hypoxia and relying on pharmacological approaches to investigate glutamatergic and GABAergic signaling.
    • An optimized 'hypoxia in a pill' regimen reverses neurodegenerative disease phenotypes in multiple preclinical models
      Wang1,2,3, Marutani4, Zazzeron4, Menard4, Volpicelli-Daley5, Ichinose4, Mootha1,2,3
      This study investigates a novel drug combination (GBT601/PT2399) as a potential treatment for neurodegenerative diseases such as Leigh syndrome, Friedreich's ataxia, Parkinson's disease, and Huntington's disease. Preclinical results reveal that the drug combo significantly extends median lifespan in the Leigh syndrome model, improves motor functions in Parkinson's and Huntington's disease models, and reduces brain tissue hyperoxia without inducing weight loss or pulmonary hypertension. The findings suggest a promising therapeutic approach for neurodegenerative diseases, with future research focusing on clinical trials to evaluate safety and efficacy.

This impairment promotes the aggregation of alpha-synuclein and potentially contributes to the development and propagation of synucleinopathies like Lewy Body Dementia and Parkinson's disease.     • Artificial hibernation reveals synaptic engram architecture associated with memory retention
      Lin, Takahashi-Nakazato, Tsutsumi, Takahashi, Mercier, Ashitomi, Chiang, Haberl, Uytiepo, Maximov, Makino, Nemoto, Enoki, Hirano, Soga, Looprasertkul, Ohno, Kubota, Sakurai, Tanaka | 2026
      The scientific paper "Artificial Hibernation Reveals Synaptic Engram Architecture Associated with Memory Retention" reveals that memories can remain stable despite widespread synapse loss during hibernation, and identifies clustered synaptic connections among memory-encoding neurons as a structural feature associated with memory retention.     • Astrocytes: Orchestrators of brain gas exchange and oxygen homeostasis
      Christie | 2026
      Astrocytes play a critical role in brain oxygen sensing, cerebral perfusion, and cognition, with a higher sensitivity to changes in oxygen availability compared to glomus cells.     • BDNF is necessary and sufficient for spinal respiratory plasticity following intermittent hypoxia
      Baker-Herman, Fuller, Bavis, Zabka, Golder, Doperalski, Johnson, Watters, Mitchell | 2003
      In the study, researchers found that brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) plays a crucial role in long-lasting phrenic facilitation (pLTF), a model of respiratory plasticity induced by intermittent hypoxia. The process involves serotonin-dependent BDNF synthesis near phrenic motoneurons, and BDNF administration elicits pLTF without hypoxia.       The study published in Nature Neuroscience investigates the motion of the dorsal cortex within the skull of awake mice, revealing that brain motion is primarily driven by locomotion rather than respiration or the cardiac cycle.     • Breathing during Exercise: Respiratory and Circulatory Interactions
      Sheel, Angus | 2026
      The paper 'Breathing during Exercise: Respiratory and Circulatory Interactions' delves into the interplay between respiratory and circulatory systems during rest and exercise, emphasizing intrathoracic pressure as a key factor.     • Breathing: Rhythmicity, Plasticity, Chemosensitivity

      The text presents a four-dimensional whole-cell model (4DWCM) that simulates the entire cell cycle of JCVI-syn3A, a minimal bacterium with a synthetic genome, accounting for genetic information processes, metabolism, growth, and cell division.     • C5aR1+ microglia exacerbate neuroinflammation and cerebral edema in acute brain injury
